The Sūtra of the Renunciation (Abhiniṣkramaṇasūtra)

The Sūtra of the Renunciation, known in Sanskrit as the Abhiniṣkramaṇa Sūtra and in Tibetan as Ngönpar Jungwé Do, is a narrative sūtra on the life and lineage of the Buddha, centered on his going forth from household life in pursuit of awakening. It opens with verses of homage to the Three Jewels, praising the Buddha for his boundless generosity in former lives, and goes on to tell the story of the bodhisattva and of the ancestry from which he was born, tracing the Śākya clan back through the Ikṣvāku kings to the founding of Kapilavastu and concluding with the marriage of King Śuddhodana to Queen Mahāmāyā, as the bodhisattva surveys the world from Tuṣita heaven before his final birth. It is Toh 301 in the Kangyur, where it belongs to the General Sūtra section, and its colophon records that it was translated by the Indian preceptor Dharmaśrībhadra and the Tibetan translator Rinchen Zangpo.

In the language of India: abhiniṣkramaṇsūtra
In the language of Tibet: The Sūtra of the Renunciation.

Homage to the Three Jewels.

He who gave his own head, glowing with jewels,
And his queen, radiant as an unwithered, opening lotus,
Delighting those who begged, and attained the state of a buddha,
The peaceful one who teaches the Dharma: to him I pay homage.

That which, once found, carries one beyond the fear of the lower realms,
Which brings all the bonds of existence to an end and is peace,
The single bridge that crosses the ocean of saṃsāra with its endless suffering,
The Dharma of the Sugata: to it I pay homage.

Abiding in excellent qualities, it is the field for giving,
Where even a small offering becomes vast;
Praised by the Sugata, adorned by the eight supreme persons,
The noble Saṅgha: to it I pay homage.

When the Sage's words are examined, there is nothing in them at all
That fails to awaken faith or fails to bring benefit.
Yet I lack the power to gather words and to retain them,
And since my mind is scattered, this labor of mine is but slight.

Just as a bee that dwells in the heart of an opened lotus
Circles about and gathers a little from the lotus,
So from the lotus grove of the Sage's mouth
I shall gather a little that delights my own mind.

The Sage's words in their entirety are very hard to fathom;
For a person of faith they grow more vast with every day.
If, on examination, one cannot gauge even the end of half a verse,
What need is there to speak of this collection of a hundred precious sūtras?

When he became a lord of the earth named Prabhāsa,
His fame and renown spread like light in every direction;
Since by the power of his merit he had conquered his enemies, the lesser kings
Bowed down, and the light of their jeweled crowns washed his face.

He saw a lord of elephants, in rut and lusting to mate, seized by desire,
Overcome by affliction, with no means to tame it on the spot.
Wishing to pacify the faults of beings, such as attachment,
He made the aspiration to become a steadfast lord of sages.

From then on, generosity, moral discipline, patience,
Diligence, meditative concentration and unwavering, keen intelligence
He cultivated for a very long time, without regard for his own happiness,
And for the benefit of beings he performed hundreds of very difficult deeds.

However many were the tireless deeds for the good of beings
That this supreme sage, endowed with great compassion, performed in other lives,
Even Śāriputra here could not recount them;
Yet for the sake of some, I shall recount a little.

As the tigress, as Śibi, the elephant, the monkey, the lord of ascetics,
Brahmā and others, he did good over many hundreds of lives.
Then the great being, striving to accomplish every aim, bound to one more birth,
The bodhisattva, took birth in the realm of Tuṣita.

Dwelling there, he looked well upon all beings,
And saw the world pervaded by many who were shelterless, unsteady and in want.
Through great compassion his mind was roused to enthusiasm,
And he contemplated the stream of his own vow to work for the good of others.

Therefore, how he came to the land of humans,
Saw the fears of birth, aging, death and sickness,
Went forth, and with resolute intelligence attained true awakening,
And likewise taught the Dharma, I shall tell. Listen!

Then the bodhisattva who dwelt in the realm of Tuṣita, who over many eons had gathered in many ways immeasurable stores of merit and wisdom and so had completed the accumulations for awakening, who was urged by compassion toward those swept far away by the powerful flood of the afflictions, and who strove constantly to work for the good of others, brought this thought clearly to mind:

"While I wandered through many births, I gave away my own flesh and blood, my head and eyes, my children and wife, in giving and utter giving; I kept difficult moral discipline; I bore patiently the many kinds of disrespect that others inflicted; I had enthusiasm that never slackened; I was unattached to the bliss of meditative absorption and its fruits; and, possessing discernment of the meaning of existence and nonexistence, with an intelligence devoted to the good of others, I generated supreme compassion for wretched creatures whose minds are tormented by many kinds of utterly unbearable painful feelings. Through the power of loving-kindness, in thousands of births such as those as Candraprabha, Śibi, the elephant, Viśvantara, Kṣāntivādin, the lord of monkeys, Sunetra, Mahauṣadha and others, I made aspirations and accumulated merit, thinking: 'How wonderful, and alas! The world is blind, without a guide, without a leader, unrighteous, gone under the sway of desire, pervaded by wrong teachings, its mind overwhelmed by unbearable craving, full of anger, ruined in the thicket of bad views, fettered by jealousy and stinginess, turning its back on taking up good conduct and wholly given over to misconduct. Having become a buddha, I shall ferry across those beings who have not crossed over. I shall free those who are not freed. I shall bring relief to those who have found no relief. I shall bring to complete nirvāṇa those who have not passed into complete nirvāṇa.' This I have long desired, and now the time has come to accomplish that aim. If I look to see what the state of the world is, I will be able to tame it."

Having reflected in this way, "I will be able to tame it," he turned his gaze upon all these worlds: "Who is declining? Who is growing in virtuous qualities? Who has come to harm? Who has become wretched? Who has fallen into suffering? To whom should I give relief through the gift of fearlessness, saying, 'Do not be afraid! Do not be afraid!'? Who is bent toward the lower realms? Who is sliding into the lower realms? Who has fallen into the lower realms? Whom should I draw out of the lower realms and establish in the higher realms and in the fruit of liberation? Who, sunk in the mire of desire, should I pull out by the hand? Who among those bereft of the noble wealth should I establish as lord and master of the noble wealth? Those in whom roots of virtue have not been generated I shall cause to generate them. Those in whom roots of virtue have been generated I shall bring to full ripening. Those who have fully ripened I shall bring to complete liberation."

When he turned upon them the gaze of his wisdom, he saw countless hundreds of thousands of living beings: some in whom roots of virtue had not been generated but who were fit to generate them; some whose roots of virtue had not ripened but who could be brought to full ripening; some whose roots of virtue had fully ripened and who were bent on going to the happy realms; some bent on attaining the first fruit; some bent on attaining the fruit of once-returner, of non-returner, of arhatship, and of solitary awakening; some who had the fortune for unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening; and some who, having gathered the aspirations of the past, were dependent on a condition, and who, because they had not heard it, had fallen away from the true Dharma.

Having seen this, he thought: "Ah! Over a long time my realization has become complete, and my toil has borne fruit." Rejoicing, he thought: "What I had to do I have done. The burden so hard to bear I have carried to the end. Since I have become a field possessing a fully purified continuum, and the beings to be trained will soon become like a lotus grove, fully ripened and ready to open, not yet touched by the sun, that blossoms when the sun rises, I shall not delay. Since, while staying here, I cannot bring all things to completion, it is not the time to stay. So let me go to the world of humans. Having gone there, with diligence as my companion, I shall realize the unsurpassed wisdom of the Tathāgata. In accordance with their fortunes, I shall cause these beings to generate roots of virtue, bring them to full ripening, and liberate them. Those living beings who have fallen into the ocean of suffering, of birth, aging, sickness, death, sorrow, lamentation, pain and mental unhappiness, who are without refuge and without protector, I shall pull out by stretching out the hand of the teaching of the true Dharma. For those utterly burned by the fires of desire, anger and delusion that have clung to them since beginningless time, I shall dispel all suffering, without remainder, by pouring out the flowing water of the noble eightfold path."

Having firmly formed this intention, as he prepared to enter Jambudvīpa he made five kinds of survey: he surveyed caste, country, time, lineage, and the woman.

For what reason do bodhisattvas survey caste? The bodhisattva dwelling in the realm of Tuṣita reflected: "In what kind of caste do bodhisattvas take conception? It is in the brahmin caste or the kṣatriya caste. Of these, sometimes the brahmin caste is honored as the highest, and sometimes the kṣatriya caste." Seeing that at present the kṣatriya caste is honored as the highest, he thought: "So that others will not have grounds to reproach me, saying, 'The bodhisattva took conception in a low caste,' let me take conception in the kṣatriya caste." Thus, because from beginningless time the bodhisattva has performed and accumulated karma that does not lead to disparagement, and because the Dharma taught by the buddhas, the blessed ones, is not without fruit, for this reason bodhisattvas survey caste.

For what reason do bodhisattvas survey country? The bodhisattva dwelling in the realm of Tuṣita reflected: "In what kind of country do bodhisattvas take conception?" He looked for a country rich in rice, sugarcane, cattle and buffalo, where hundreds of mendicants gather, where there are no border peoples, where people of the central land dwell, and where, by taming a single person, a great multitude of people would be tamed. Then the bodhisattva thought: "This central country is rich in rows of sugarcane, in rice, cattle and buffalo; hundreds of mendicants gather there; there are no border peoples; people of the central land dwell there; and there, by taming a single person, a great multitude of people will be tamed. So that others will not have grounds to reproach me, saying, 'The bodhisattva took conception in the border lands,' let me take conception in the central country." Thus, because from beginningless time the bodhisattva has performed and accumulated karma that does not lead to disparagement, and because the Dharma taught by the buddhas, the blessed ones, is not without fruit, for this reason the bodhisattva surveys country.

For what reason do bodhisattvas survey time? The bodhisattva dwelling in the realm of Tuṣita reflected: "At what kind of time do bodhisattvas take conception in Jambudvīpa?" When the lifespan of beings is increasing, they do not take conception when it reaches eighty thousand years or more; and when the lifespan of humans is decreasing, they do not take conception when it is a hundred years or less. For what reason does a bodhisattva not take conception when the lifespan of humans is eighty thousand years or more? Because when the lifespan of humans is eighty thousand years, they are hard to bring to disenchantment and hard to make weary of existence; they are foolish and of dull faculties; and, being heedless and enjoying much pleasure, they do not become vessels for the truths.

And when the lifespan is decreasing, at a hundred years or less, the five degenerations, namely the degeneration of lifespan, the degeneration of afflictions, the degeneration of beings, the degeneration of views and the degeneration of the age, are predominant. He thought: "So that others will not have grounds to reproach me, saying, 'The bodhisattva took conception at a time when the five degenerations were predominant,' I shall not do so." Thus, because from beginningless time the bodhisattva has performed and accumulated karma that does not lead to disparagement, and because the Dharma taught by the buddhas, the blessed ones, is not without fruit, for this reason bodhisattvas survey time.

For what reason do bodhisattvas survey lineage? The bodhisattva dwelling in the realm of Tuṣita reflected: "In what kind of lineage do bodhisattvas take conception?" He looked for a family that is honored as eminent and whose conduct has been without fault through seven generations on both the mother's and the father's side. Then he thought: "This Śākya lineage is honored as eminent, and from the time it was honored by many, its conduct has been without fault. So that others will not have grounds to reproach me, saying, 'The bodhisattva took conception from a low lineage,' let me take conception in the lineage of King Śuddhodana." Thus, because from beginningless time the bodhisattva has performed and accumulated karma that does not lead to disparagement, and because the Dharma taught by the buddhas, the blessed ones, is not without fruit, for this reason the bodhisattva surveys lineage.

For what reason do bodhisattvas survey the woman? The bodhisattva dwelling in the realm of Tuṣita reflected: "In the womb of what kind of woman do bodhisattvas take conception?" And he thought: He looked for a woman who is beautiful in form, of good family, one through whom the family will flourish, not given to anger, not slanderous, not harsh of speech, endowed with faith, good by nature, virtuous in intention, of excellent lineage, of high family, with a family of kinsfolk, excellent in wisdom, excellent in generosity, steady in character, stable by nature, not frivolous and without arrogance, with a mind of loving-kindness, lovely and charming, righteous toward all the world, foremost among all women; one who had made the aspiration before the buddhas of the past, "Ah! May I become the mother of a buddha!"; one able to carry the bodhisattva in her womb for ten months, and one who would not let her own aims decline.

Then the bodhisattva thought: "This Mahāmāyā has been without fault in conduct through seven generations on both her mother's and her father's side; she is endowed with beauty, with good family, with good conduct," and so on as above, up to "foremost among all women"; "she is not given to anger, the family will flourish through her, she is able to carry the bodhisattva in her womb for ten months, and her own aims will not decline. Moreover, Mahāmāyā made the aspiration before the buddhas of the past, 'Ah! May I become the mother of a buddha!' Therefore others will not reproach me, saying, 'The bodhisattva took conception in the womb of a woman with bad marks.'" Thus, because the bodhisattva has performed and accumulated karma that does not lead to disparagement, and because the Dharma taught by the buddhas, the blessed ones, is not without fruit, for this reason the bodhisattva surveys women.

Then the bodhisattva, having made the five surveys, said to the classes of gods who live in Tuṣita: "Friends, know this! I shall descend to Jambudvīpa and take conception in the central country. Those of you who seek the deathless, take conception, for the most part, in the six great cities of the central country, namely Śrāvastī, Sāketa, Vaiśālī, Campā, Rājagṛha and Vārāṇasī."

When he had spoken thus, the gods of the Tuṣita class said this to the bodhisattva: "Bodhisattva, please know this! At present the world is stained by evil, beings are of savage intent, and Jambudvīpa is thrown into turmoil by the six dialecticians, the six proclaimers and the six attainers of meditative absorption. Who are the six dialecticians? They are Pūraṇa Kāśyapa, Maskarin Gośālīputra, Sañjayin Vairaṭṭīputra, Ajita Keśakambala, Kakuda Kātyāyana and Nirgrantha Jñātiputra. Who are the six proclaimers? They are the brahmin Illusion-Crosser, the brahmin Ear-Striker, the brahmin Endowed with Goodness, the brahmin Brahmāyus, the brahmin Puṣkarasārin and the brahmin Lohita. Who are the six attainers of meditative absorption? They are Udraka Rāmaputra, Ārāḍa Kālāma, the wanderer Subhadra, the brahmin youth All-Victorious, the sage Free of Afflictions, and Uruvilvā Kāśyapa the matted-haired ascetic. Here, so that we may hear the Dharma, we shall prepare a seat for you, Bodhisattva, twelve yojanas in size, for we think: 'Since the bodhisattva dwells among us in the realm of Tuṣita, he will teach the Dharma; and once we have heard that Dharma, we shall practice it in whatever way will bring about our long-lasting benefit, welfare and happiness.'"

When they had made this request, the bodhisattva said this to the gods of Tuṣita: "Friends, then strike all the musical instruments!" The gods of the Tuṣita class struck all the musical instruments, and the bodhisattva too blew a conch and said: "Friends, which sound here is the loudest?"

"Blessed One, it is the sound of the conch."

"Friends, just as the sound of the conch overwhelms all the musical instruments, so I too shall go to Jambudvīpa, overwhelm the six dialecticians, the six proclaimers and the six attainers of meditative absorption, and realize the deathless; and with the deathless I shall satisfy beings. I shall blow the conch of impermanence. I shall beat the drum of emptiness. I shall roar the lion's roar of selflessness." Having said this, he spoke again in verse:

"A single lion conquers many hosts of beasts of prey;
A single vajra shatters many mountain peaks;
A single Śakra conquers many lords of the asuras;
A single sun dispels many darknesses.

Friends, those of you who seek the deathless, take conception in the six great cities of the central country."

Then Śakra, lord of the gods, thought: "This bodhisattva intends to take conception in the womb of Queen Mahāmāyā. So let me bestow splendor upon her, and let me also purify her womb." Having thought this, Śakra, lord of the gods, bestowed splendor upon Queen Mahāmāyā and made her womb pure as well.

Then the bodhisattva dwelling in the realm of Tuṣita, having surveyed caste, country, time, lineage and the woman, having made these five surveys, at midnight appeared as an elephant, passed from the realm of the Tuṣita gods, and entered his mother's womb.

At the time when the bodhisattva died and passed from the realm of the Tuṣita gods and entered his mother's womb, this great earth shook violently, and this whole world was lit up by a vast radiance. Even the darkness of the spaces between the worlds, black and pitch-dark, where even the sun and moon, so great in miraculous power and so great in might, cannot shed light with their rays, was at that time lit up by a great radiance. The beings born there, who could not even see their own outstretched hands, saw one another by that light and recognized, "Sirs, other beings too are born here! Sirs, other beings too are born here!"

Just as a vast, well-massed cloud,
Heavy with stored-up water, is stirred by the wind,
So that wise one, dwelling in the womb,
Was like the full moon in the womb of autumn clouds.

When the mighty, peerless one drew near,
Then in the spaces between the continents of the worlds
The covering darkness was dispelled, and in every direction
A great brightness arose: such is the nature of things here.

On the very night when the bodhisattva passed from the realm of the Tuṣita gods and entered his mother's womb, Queen Mahāmāyā saw four dreams: a white baby elephant split open her right side and entered; she walked cross-legged in the sky above; seated on a lion throne, she taught the Dharma to a great multitude of people; and the world with its gods and asuras paid homage to her.

When King Śuddhodana heard this from the queen, he asked the soothsayers what this dream portended, and they said: "May Your Majesty know this! That the queen saw in her dream a white baby elephant split open her right side and enter means that a son who is a wheel-turning king will be born. That she walked in the sky above means that he will be great in miraculous power and great in might. That she sat on a lion throne and taught the Dharma means that he too will sit on a lion throne and teach the Dharma to the fourfold assembly. That the world with its gods and asuras paid homage to her means that if he goes forth, he will be worshiped, honored and praised by the world with its gods and asuras."

It is in the nature of things that, at the time when the bodhisattva passes from the realm of the Tuṣita gods and enters his mother's womb, Śakra, lord of the gods, so that no one, human or nonhuman, may harm her, appoints four sons of the gods as the mother's guardians: one holding a sword, one holding a spear, one holding a javelin, and one holding a great bow. Of this it is said:

Śakra spoke to the four foremost gods:
"Go, and with respect protect in every way
That mother, supreme among gods and humans,
So that no one may harm her."

They made great effort and, rejoicing,
Received Śakra's command upon the crowns of their heads;
Holding in their hands arrows, spears and swords of every kind,
They protected the mother of the bodhisattva.

Guarded by the four gods, victorious in battle,
Wearing various ornaments day and night,
The queen took her pleasure within her own house
As if she were roaming in the Nandana Grove.

Then, at another time, Queen Mahāmāyā conceived this wish: "Ah! Your Majesty, let all the prisoners be set free!" And King Śuddhodana, heeding the queen's words, set all the prisoners free.

Then, at another time, the queen conceived a wish: "Ah! May Your Majesty give gifts and make merit for the lowly, the blind, the destitute, the wretched and the beggars in the middle of the eastern quarter of the city; and may you give gifts and make merit for the lowly, the blind, the destitute, the wretched and the beggars in the middle of the crossroads to the south, the west and the north."

Then King Śuddhodana, heeding the queen's words, gave gifts in the eastern quarter of the city to the lowly, the blind, the destitute, the wretched and the beggars, according to their wishes and just as they asked, and made merit. Likewise, in the middle of the crossroads to the south, the west and the north, he gave gifts to the lowly, the blind, the destitute, the wretched and the beggars, whatever they wished and just as they asked, and made merit.

When the bodhisattva Śākyamuni had passed from the realm of the Tuṣita gods and dwelt in his mother's womb, he was, like goods placed within a treasury, not soiled by the impurity of the womb, the impurity of mucus, the impurity of blood, or any kind of uncleanness whatever. Just as, for example, a precious jewel is not stained by Kāśī cloth, nor is Kāśī cloth stained by the precious jewel, so, when the bodhisattva dwelt in his mother's womb, he was, like goods placed within a treasury, not soiled by the impurity of the womb, the impurity of mucus, the impurity of blood, or any kind of uncleanness whatever.

Just as a jewel of colored crystal, luminous and clear,
Is not stained, nor is the spotless Kāśī cloth,
So when the great Sage dwelt in the womb,
The impurity of the womb stained him in no way at all.

When the bodhisattva had passed from the realm of the Tuṣita gods and dwelt in his mother's womb, she saw him dwelling there in her womb, complete in every part. Just as, for example, when a fine, eight-faceted beryl gem of good quality, limpid, clear and flawless, is threaded on a cord of five colors, blue, yellow, red, white and orange, a man with eyes who sees it recognizes, "This is the thread, this is the gem, and the gem is threaded on this thread," so, when the bodhisattva Śākyamuni had passed from the realm of Tuṣita and dwelt in his mother's womb, she saw him dwelling within her womb, complete in every aspect.

Just as a flawless beryl gem,
Luminous and clear, is seen when placed in the hand,
So the great Sage dwelling in his mother's womb
His mother clearly beheld, with knowing and with thought.

It is in the nature of things that, when the bodhisattva Śākyamuni had passed from Tuṣita and dwelt in his mother's womb, his mother did not become weary or fatigued in body from carrying the bodhisattva.

When the ripening of his karma was exhausted, he passed from the higher realm;
When he had entered the womb and dwelt there,
His mother did not carry him with weariness:
Such, at that time, was the nature of things here.

It is in the nature of things that, when the bodhisattva Śākyamuni had passed from the realm of Tuṣita and dwelt in his mother's womb, from then on, for as long as she lived, his mother took up the foundation of the five vows. For as long as she lived she gave up taking life and refrained from taking life. She gave up taking what is not given, unchaste conduct, lying, and grain liquor and fermented liquor, the intoxicants that are a ground for heedlessness; and she refrained from grain liquor and fermented liquor, the intoxicants that are a ground for heedlessness.

Not taking life, not taking what is not given,
Not lying, not craving liquor,
And giving up the unchaste conduct of sexual union:
This is the nature of things for the mother of Siddhārtha.

It is in the nature of things that, when the bodhisattva Śākyamuni had passed from the realm of Tuṣita and dwelt in his mother's womb, his mother did not turn her mind with desire toward men.

She did not become attached to the afflictions,
Her mind was not tormented by the causes of desire,
And that mother did not turn her mind
Toward men endowed with the qualities that are desired.

Then, at another time, Queen Mahāmāyā conceived a wish, thinking, "Ah! I should drink the waters of the four great oceans," and she told King Śuddhodana. King Śuddhodana said to the soothsayers, "Why has the queen conceived such a wish?" The soothsayers explained: "Your Majesty, the queen will give birth to a son whose body is adorned with the thirty-two marks of a great being. When he goes forth, he will drink the entire ocean of what is to be known."

King Śuddhodana said, "If she is not given the water of the great oceans to drink, what will happen?"

They said, "She will bear a son whose limbs are incomplete."

The king said, "Should a son of mine, whose name is Śuddhodana, be born with incomplete limbs?" At that time there lived in the city of Kapilavastu a wanderer named Red-Eyes who was skilled in magical illusions. The king summoned him and commanded, "Quickly bring water from the great oceans!" And he, while staying right where he was, drew forth the water of the four great oceans and had the queen drink it, and she was freed of the wish she had conceived.

Then, at another time, Queen Mahāmāyā conceived a wish of this kind, thinking: "Ah! I should go in person into the Lumbinī Grove and stay at the foot of a tree, holding the end of a branch." Then King Śuddhodana, knowing that Queen Mahāmāyā had conceived such a wish, had a palanquin made of the four kinds of precious substances prepared, had the fourfold army of elephant troops, cavalry, chariots and infantry put on armor, and, surrounded by a great multitude of people, set out together with Queen Mahāmāyā and went to the Lumbinī Grove.

When Queen Mahāmāyā saw the Lumbinī Grove, her mind became one-pointed. She descended from the palanquin, and seeing an aśoka tree in full bloom, joy and delight arose in her. She went over to it, took hold of a branch bending low under the weight of its flowers and leaves, and stood there, surrounded by elderly women.

Then Śakra, lord of the gods, thought: "Queen Mahāmāyā is close to giving birth to her son. So let me receive the bodhisattva when he is first born." Then he thought further: "Queen Mahāmāyā is endowed with modesty, so she will not dare to give birth in front of me. I must arrange some means." Thinking this, Śakra, lord of the gods, transformed himself into the guise of an old woman. He conjured up a fierce wind and rain of such force that those elderly women would flee in the ten directions, and when they had fled, he manifested himself in the guise of an old nurse and stood before Queen Mahāmāyā.

It is the nature of things that a bodhisattva enters his mother's womb knowingly, dwells there knowingly, and emerges knowingly. Then the bodhisattva thought: "It would not be fitting for me to come out through the passage of the womb like an ordinary person. So I shall break through my mother's right side and come forth." Then the bodhisattva broke through his mother's right side and came forth, and Śakra, lord of the gods, received him in his two hands.

The bodhisattva thought, and knew: "This Śakra will grow proud, thinking, 'I received this bodhisattva.'" Knowing this, he blessed his body so that its very nature was the core of a vajra. While in the womb, he had thought, "May my mother not be distressed by carrying me," and so he had made himself light as he dwelt there. Now Śakra, lord of the gods, was unable to hold him, and he trembled. The bodhisattva said, "Kauśika (that is, Śakra), let go, let go!" When he had said this, Śakra, lord of the gods, set the bodhisattva down on the ground.

The very moment he was set down, without anyone holding him, he looked to the four directions, took seven steps toward each, and spoke these words as well: "Kauśika, which direction is this?"
"Bodhisattva, it is the east."
The bodhisattva said, "Kauśika, I shall go before all sentient beings. Which direction is this?"
"It is the south."
"I shall become worthy of the offerings of all beings. Which direction is this?"
"It is the north."
"I shall cross the ocean of existence. Which direction is this?"
"It is the west."
"This will be my last birth."

When the one who puts an end to birth and death was born,
He walked seven steps upon the earth,
And "This has become my final birth,"
So spoke the sole teacher of the three worlds.

It is the nature of things that at the time the bodhisattva Śākyamuni came forth from his mother's womb, this great earth shook violently. This whole world was lit by a vast light. And the darkness between the worlds, gloom upon gloom, pitch-black, where even the sun and moon, so great in magical power and so mighty, cannot give light with their radiance, even those places were at that time lit by a great light. The beings born there, who could not even see their own outstretched hands, saw one another by that light and realized: "Friends, other beings too have been born here! Friends, other beings too have been born here!"

When the Guide was born,
The earth with its mountains shook violently,
And in an instant, throughout the world,
A radiant, illuminating light arose.

Then King Śuddhodana, seeing the great earthquake and the vast light, circumambulated the bodhisattva, bowed at his two feet, and spoke these words: "I have truly gained a great gain, that a son of such great magical power and such might has been born to me."

It is the nature of things that when the bodhisattva Śākyamuni was born from his mother's womb, like a sword drawn from its sheath, he was unsoiled by the impurity of the womb, the impurity of the membranes, the impurity of blood, or any other kind of filth whatever.

Just as a fine, clear, radiant crystal
And clean cloth of Kāśī do not soil each other,
So the Sage, when he came forth from the womb,
Was like refined gold drawn out from a great fire.

It is the nature of things that at the time the bodhisattva Śākyamuni was born from his mother's womb, his mother neither sat nor lay down; standing, that woman of the warrior caste gave birth to one of the warrior caste.

She neither sat nor lay down;
Adorned with the fivefold moral discipline,
She stood, and the supremely virtuous
Youth was received here by Śakra.

It is the nature of things that at the time the bodhisattva Śākyamuni was born from his mother's womb, the gods held white parasols with jeweled handles above his head.

A parasol like the light of the stainless orb of the moon
The chief of the gods holds above his head,
And with hands ablaze with the rays of bracelets,
They fan him with fine yak-tail whisks.

It is the nature of things that the moment the bodhisattva Śākyamuni was born, two streams of water, one cool and one warm, fell from the sky onto the crown of his head, and with them the bodhisattva was bathed.

With stainless, perfectly pure water, cool and likewise warm,
The lord of two-legged beings was bathed,
And to the lineage in which the bodhisattva was born
The nāgas too made offerings.

It is the nature of things that the moment the bodhisattva was born, a great spring appeared before the mother who had given birth and began to flow, and in it his mother performed her ablutions.

When the youth, lord of two-legged beings, was born,
Those gods, their minds filled with joy,
Brought forth fine water there,
And with that water his mother did what was needed.
Divine instruments of sweetest sound
They played in the highest joy;
They scattered garments and garlands,
And others bowed low and likewise sang his praise.

It is the nature of things that the moment the bodhisattva was born, the gods scattered from the sky divine blue lotuses, red lotuses, night lilies, and white lotuses, powdered aloeswood, powdered incense, powdered sandalwood, and tamāla leaves. They scattered divine māndārava flowers as well. Divine cymbals sounded, and they scattered garments too.

When the Buddha, to whom the wise bow down, was born,
A rain of sweet and supreme fragrances fell,
And then, each with the eloquence that was their own,
With overjoyed minds they spoke their praises:
"This one will attain supreme awakening
And bring beings the benefit of suffering's end.
For beings blinded by the cataract of ignorance,
This supreme physician will bestow the gift of sight.
For beings whose eyes are veiled by delusion, who are blind,
This one will become a great lamp.
To those ruined by wrong views, who have entered false paths,
He will show the straight path they had not sought.
He will block the paths to the lower realms entirely
And open the way to the abodes of the gods."
So the gods who dwell as far as Akaniṣṭha
Proclaimed again and again throughout the abodes of the gods.

At the very time the bodhisattva Śākyamuni was born, in Śrāvastī a son was born to Aranemi Brahmadatta. Brahmadatta thought: "When my son was born, the land appeared as bright as brightness itself. Therefore his name shall be Prasenajit." And so he named him.

In the city of Rājagṛha a son was born to King Mahāpadma. Mahāpadma thought: "When my son was born, the world appeared as if the orb of the sun had risen, and he is the son of Bimbī. Therefore his name shall be Bimbisāra." And so he named him.

In Kauśāmbī a son was born to King Śatānīka. Śatānīka thought: "When my son was born, the world appeared as if the sun had risen. Therefore his name shall be Udayana." And so he named him.

In the city of Ujjayinī a son was born to King Anantanemi. He thought: "When my son was born, the world became as if lit by a lamp. Therefore his name shall be Pradyota." And so he named him. But it was not as they thought; rather, all of that was the power of the bodhisattva.

At the very time the bodhisattva Śākyamuni was born, five hundred Śākya boys, headed by Nanda, were born; five hundred Śākya girls, headed by Yaśodharā, were born; five hundred attendants, headed by Chandaka, were born; and five hundred horses, headed by Kaṇṭhaka, were foaled. The gods also revealed the openings of five treasures. The petty kings of the provinces and the kings of the fortified towns bowed down deeply, and all of them paid tribute.

Then King Śuddhodana thought: "Since every wish was fulfilled when my son was born, this boy's name shall be Sarvārthasiddha, 'He Who Accomplishes Every Aim.'" Thus he first gave the bodhisattva his name.

It is the nature of things that the moment the bodhisattva Śākyamuni was born, his father and mother presented him to the brahmins who read bodily marks and interpret omens, and it was declared: "Sirs, a boy who possesses these has two destinies and no other. If, as a householder, he remains at home, he will become a wheel-turning king, victorious over the four quarters, a Dharma king who rules by the Dharma, possessed of the seven precious things. These will be his seven precious things: the precious wheel, the precious elephant, the precious horse, the precious jewel, the precious queen, the precious householder, and seventh, the precious minister. He will have a thousand sons, heroic and valiant, of supreme form in every limb, crushers of hostile armies. He will conquer this entire great earth, as far as the ocean, in accordance with the Dharma, and will dwell ruling over it, where none dares to do harm, free of violence, without punishment, and without the striking of weapons. But if he shaves off his hair and beard, puts on saffron robes, and, out of genuine faith alone, goes forth from home into homelessness, then his name will resound throughout the world as a Tathāgata, an arhat, a perfectly complete buddha. Endowed with the thirty-two marks of a great man, so he truly is, Your Majesty."

From "A boy endowed with the thirty-two marks of a great man has two destinies and no other" up to "he will be known in the world as a perfectly complete buddha": as before, this is the nature of things here.

Concerning this it is said:

When the prince was born, the king questioned
The brahmins who know the marks of a man, and then they spoke:
"If the prince should remain at home,
He will surely become a lord of men,
Valiant, and a wheel-turning king
Holding sway over all other kings.
He will exercise a righteous kingship,
Victorious throughout all the continents.
This precious one, the best of men, will obtain
The seven precious things through the karma of his merit,
And by their power he will rule
The entire earth, his command never broken.

"Well wrought of gold, its hub perfectly round,
With a thousand spokes and a fine, unbroken rim,
That which first conquers the earth:
That wheel he will obtain first.
Well formed in every limb, standing firm upon the ground,
The best of elephants, like a peak of the snowy mountains,
Worthy of a king, traveling through the sky:
That supreme precious thing he will obtain second.
That which at dawn circles all the lands
In every direction and then returns,
With a coat as lovely as a peacock's neck:
That precious horse he will obtain.
A stainless gem whose nature is beryl,
Shedding light a full league all around,
Stainless, making the night just like the day:
That fourth supreme precious thing he will obtain.
She who has fragrance, touch, beauty and radiance,
A noble woman, foremost among the proud,
Endowed, like a goddess, with every virtue:
That precious woman he will obtain here.
And the householder who here offers the king
Pearls and gold and much else besides,
Rich in wealth like the lord of riches:
That precious one he will obtain and enjoy.
Skilled in advancing, in holding firm and in retreating
With the entire army,
The heroic seventh who will appear
Is called the precious minister among men.
A thousand sons will be born to him,
Heroic, valiant, of supreme form in every limb,
Whose splendor and strength are very great,
And to whom other lords of the earth will bow.

"But if, finding the household troubled by many faults,
As a bird forsakes a blazing tree,
He goes forth to the forest for the sake of peace,
Then before long he will become a buddha here.
This son of yours born today,
Such a youth, a unique jewel among beings,
Will go to no destiny other than these two:
So it is seen in the words of the sacred verses."

"Sirs, what are the thirty-two marks of a great man, endowed with which a great man has two destinies and no other," and so on as before, up to "his fame will resound throughout the world"?

"Your Majesty, the prince's feet are well planted. Because his two feet are well planted, they touch the ground evenly. Your Majesty, this is a mark of a great man, the prince's mark of a great man.

"On the soles of both his feet there are wheels with a thousand spokes, with hub and rim, complete in every aspect. Your Majesty, this is a mark of a great man, the prince's mark of a great man.

"Your Majesty, the prince's fingers are long, the heels of his feet are broad, and his hands and feet are soft and tender. His hands and feet are as soft and tender as the down of the silk-cotton tree or the down of cotton. Your Majesty, this is a mark of a great man, the prince's mark of a great man.

"Your Majesty, the prince's hands and feet are webbed. His hands and feet are webbed like those of a young king of geese. Your Majesty, this is a mark of a great man, the prince's mark of a great man.

"Your Majesty, the prince's ankle joints do not protrude, his calves are like those of the eṇeya antelope, his body is well knit, and when he stands upright without bending, his hands reach and touch his two kneecaps. Your Majesty, this is a mark of a great man, the prince's mark of a great man.

"Your Majesty, the prince's private parts are withdrawn into a sheath. His private parts are withdrawn into a sheath like those of a thoroughbred elephant or a thoroughbred horse just born. Your Majesty, this is a mark of a great man, the prince's mark of a great man.

"Your Majesty, the prince's arm span is as great as his height, and his height is as great as his arm span, so that, like a banyan tree, his height and breadth are in perfect proportion. Your Majesty, this is a mark of a great man, the prince's mark of a great man.

"Your Majesty, on the prince's upper body the hairs grow one by one, and the hairs that grow one by one on his body are dark blue and curl to the right. Your Majesty, this is a mark of a great man, the prince's mark of a great man.

"Your Majesty, the prince is golden in color, has a radiance extending a fathom, and his skin is fine and golden in hue. Because his skin is fine, dust and dirt do not cling to his body. Your Majesty, this is a mark of a great man, the prince's mark of a great man.

"Your Majesty, the prince's body has seven prominent parts. The seven prominent parts with which his body was born are his two hands, his two feet, his two shoulders, and the nape of his neck. Your Majesty, this is a mark of a great man, the prince's mark of a great man.

"Your Majesty, the area between the prince's shoulders is full, his upper body is like a lion's, his body is straight and of large frame, his shoulders are perfectly rounded, he has forty teeth, his teeth are even, his teeth are closely set, his canine teeth are very white, his jaw is like a lion's, and he has the supreme sense of taste. Your Majesty, this is a mark of a great man, the prince's mark of a great man.

"Your Majesty, the prince's tongue is very long and thin. Because his tongue is so long and so thin, when he extends it from his mouth it covers the entire circle of his face as far as the hairline. Your Majesty, this is a mark of a great man, the prince's mark of a great man.

"Your Majesty, the prince has the voice of Brahmā, pleasing like the song of the kalaviṅka bird, and resonant as a drum. Your Majesty, this is a mark of a great man, the prince's mark of a great man."

"Your Majesty, the prince's eyes are deep blue, his eyelashes are like a cow's, he has the crown protuberance (uṣṇīṣa), and the tuft of hair between his eyebrows is white as a conch and curls to the right. Your Majesty, this is a mark of a great man, the prince's mark of a great man.

"Your Majesty, these are the thirty-two marks of a great man, endowed with which one has two destinies and no other," and so on as before, up to "his fame will resound throughout the world."

The marks of the Great Sage,
Who has cultivated himself well
And holds in his body
The last of his bodies:
These I shall now declare.
His feet are well planted
And touch the ground evenly.
On the soles of his feet and the palms of his hands
Wheels of perfect shape have appeared.
The heels of his feet are broad,
And his fingers long and beautiful.
His hands and feet are webbed
And soft as cotton down.
His ankle joints do not protrude,
And his calves are like the eṇeya antelope's.
Without bending his body, the best of men
Reaches his kneecaps with his hands.
His private parts are withdrawn into a sheath.
Like a banyan, his height and breadth are in proportion.
The hairs of his upper body grow
One by one, and these hairs curl to the right.
His color is like refined gold,
And he has a radiance a fathom wide all around.
The Blessed One's skin is fine,
And seven parts of his body are prominent.
The Blessed One's shoulders are full between,
His upper body like a lion's,
His body straight and of large frame,
His shoulders perfectly rounded.
His teeth, four times ten,
Are complete, with none missing;
Even and closely set,
His teeth are well placed
And very white. His jaw is like a lion's,
He has the supreme sense of taste,
His tongue is very thin,
And the Blessed One has the voice of Brahmā.
His eyelashes are like a cow's, his eyes deep blue,
And the tuft of hair between his eyebrows
Is white as a conch and curls to the right.
Whoever comes to possess
These thirty-two excellent marks
Will have two destinies;
There will be no third.
If he remains at home,
He will become a wheel-turning king,
With many mindful sons,
With few troubles, and wise.
If he goes forth, a perfect buddha,
A person without equal, he will become.
Food and drink, clothing,
And bedding he will receive,
His retinue will never be divided,
And he will delight the hearts of all embodied beings.
The gods will follow him,
And so will humans.
He will become the supreme lord of the Dharma,
And so too throughout the human lands.
Without equal and without peer,
The Guide will be unmatched.

"Your Majesty, these are the prince's marks of a great man." The words "endowed with which one has two destinies and no other," and the rest, are as before.

It is the nature of things that when seven days had passed after the birth of the bodhisattva Śākyamuni, the mother who bore him passed away, and immediately upon passing she was reborn in splendor among the gods of the Heaven of the Thirty-Three.

Then King Śuddhodana entrusted the bodhisattva to nurses: "Nurses, massage this boy at the proper times, bathe him at the proper times, feed him at the proper times, and at the proper times attend to him with every true comfort." The nurses received him joyfully into their two hands, and at the proper times they massaged him, at the proper times bathed him, at the proper times fed him, and at the proper times attended to him with every true comfort.

Then, at that time, having anointed him with the most delightfully fragrant substances there were and adorned him with every ornament, they presented him to his father Śuddhodana. King Śuddhodana received him, set him joyfully on his lap, gazed at him again and again, and was filled with joy.

It is the nature of things that the bodhisattva Śākyamuni was handsome, beautiful and lovely to behold, having entirely surpassed the complexion of humans, though not attaining the complexion of the gods. When men and women gazed at him, they could never have enough, and they were filled with joy. Just as gold from the Jambū River, worked into an ornament by a skilled smith and placed upon a fresh white woolen cloth, is exceedingly bright, blazing and gleaming, so the bodhisattva Śākyamuni was handsome, beautiful and lovely to behold, having surpassed the complexion of humans, though not attaining the complexion of the gods.

It is the nature of things that the bodhisattva Śākyamuni became dear and delightful to the hearts of a great multitude of people. And this great multitude passed him from shoulder to shoulder, just as an autumn lotus, being dear and delightful to the hearts of a great multitude of people, is passed by that great multitude from hand to hand; and so on as before.

It is the nature of things that from the moment of his birth the bodhisattva looked at forms without blinking. In not blinking his eyes, he was like the gods of the Heaven of the Thirty-Three.

It is the nature of things that from the moment of his birth the bodhisattva, with the divine eye born from the ripening of his former karma, saw for a full league all around, by day and by night.

With his stainless eye, born of karma,
Forms both great and very subtle,
By day and by night, a league all around,
He sees as though they stood before him.

It is the nature of things that from the moment of his birth the bodhisattva's voice was sweet and charming, his voice melodious, his voice delightful. Just as the song of the bird of the snowy mountains is sweet and charming, melodious and delightful, so from the moment of his birth the bodhisattva's voice was sweet and charming, his voice melodious, his voice delightful.

On Mount All-Bearing there lived a sage named Asita, possessed of the five superknowledges, loving by nature, full of compassion, and kind and gentle toward all creatures. He had a nephew named Naradatta. At that time, while the sage had entered a cave and was meditating, the bodhisattva was born. When Naradatta saw the light by which the whole world was lit with a vast radiance, he said to Asita:

"Master, have all the suns
Risen here at once?
For this mountain, with its caves,
Has been lit up with light."

Asita said to him:

"The light of the sun is always harsh.
My son, these lights
Are cool when they touch the limbs,
So surely this is the light of that Sage.
The radiant bodhisattva
Has surely been born from the womb,
And now that he is born, the great being's
Rays, which are like gold,
Stainless as they are,
Have lit up the three worlds."

Naradatta said, "Master, go! See for yourself whether he will become a wheel-turning king or whether he will realize unsurpassed wisdom."

The sage Asita said, "At present the house of King Śuddhodana is thronged all around with countless hundreds of thousands of regional kings, kings of fortified towns, merchants, guild masters, caravan leaders, brahmins and householders. When it has become quiet, then I shall go to King Śuddhodana."

Then King Śuddhodana thought, "Alas, how unfitting it would be if the sage Asita were to come to Kapilavastu on foot!"

Then the sage Asita rose up into the sky above and came to the gate of King Śuddhodana's palace. In due course the gatekeeper announced to King Śuddhodana, "May it please Your Majesty: the sage Asita has come here." When King Śuddhodana heard this, he was glad, delighted, overjoyed and supremely pleased. He honored the sage with the utmost reverence and said, "Welcome, great sage! For what purpose have you come?"

Asita said:

"Great king, I have come here
To see your son.
I long to see that Sage,
The lord of the world, the Guide."

The king said, "If that is so, wait a while until the prince, who is asleep, awakens."

Asita said:

"Just as thoroughbred horses
Sleep only a quarter of the night,
So the sleep of those intent upon a purpose
Does not stay long in their eyes."

Then the prince Śākyamuni, who was sitting on his nurse's lap, was handed to the sage Asita. The sage Asita received him in both hands, examined him for a moment, and said, "Your Majesty, what have the readers of marks prophesied about this prince?"

King Śuddhodana said, "They say that he will become a wheel-turning king possessed of the seven precious things."

Then the sage Asita, looking at the bodhisattva, spoke in verse:

"Lord of the earth, those examiners are mistaken in their judgment:
In the age of strife no wheel-turning king appears.
This treasury of the Dharma, through supreme merit,
Will triumph over all evil and become a buddha.
One whose marks are not clear becomes a lord of men,
The mighty master of the four continents;
But since the marks of this Lord of Sages are perfectly clear,
The best of men will become a buddha and turn the wheel of Dharma."

Then the sage Asita set about examining the certain end of his own life and the bodhisattva's full awakening. He saw that the bodhisattva, at the age of twenty-nine, would leave his home and practice austerities for six years, and then would realize the deathless. And seeing that he himself would die in the meantime, his eyes filled with tears. When King Śuddhodana saw him so, he was alarmed and spoke in verse:

"He whom men and women gaze upon
And can never have enough of, and are filled with joy:
Now that you have seen him here, fair of body, bringer of good,
Why, great sage, do you seem to weep?
Is there not some danger close at hand
To my prince, or that his life will be cut short?
Tell me of it quickly and plainly,
For your tears make me tremble."

The sage Asita also spoke in verse:

"Even if from the sky upon him
A fierce hail of vajras were hurled down,
It could not stir even a single hair
On the body of the Great Sage.
A great fire driven by the force of the wind,
Sharp weapons, utterly unbearable,
Deadly poison and the sharp venom of snakes:
Should these fall upon the prince, he would not fear for an instant.
He who abides in compassion itself
Grants fearlessness to those tormented by fear.
Since he destroys the fears of the whole world,
How could fear arise for this lord of men?
That great being who has become foremost
Among the great beings of the world,
Whom even the gods together with Brahmā always protect:
How could fear arise for that lord of men?
Lord of men, it is my own misfortune that I see,
For I myself shall not attain that peace.
Having met with this supreme treasury of the Dharma,
I shall die without attaining the goal.
Those who will hear the Dharma of this one,
The peerless and excellent speaker, and having heard it
Will go to the far shore of perfect peace:
They are happy and blessed with merit, and free from every plague."

Then, through the power of having touched the bodhisattva, the sage Asita's magical powers waned. When they had waned, Asita said, "I shall have to leave the city on foot alone."

When King Śuddhodana heard this, he had the entire city cleared of stones, gravel and potsherds, and had it strewn everywhere with incense and fragrant powders.

Then the sage Asita, with his great magnificence, came out of the city and gradually made his way to Mount All-Bearing. There he said to Naradatta, "I have seen the prince. When he realizes unsurpassed wisdom, I shall not live to see it, for I shall die in the meantime. You must go forth into homelessness in his presence. Utterly crush pride and arrogance, and practice the pure conduct of celibacy. Do not be arrogant on account of your caste, do not be arrogant on account of your clan, and do not be arrogant on account of your lineage. Before long you too will attain the deathless nectar." Having said this, he spoke again in verse:

"If you wish to seek the Tathāgata,
Stay in the eastern region,
And you will see the perfect Buddha,
The renowned one, so hard to find."

Then, at another time, King Śuddhodana looked again and again at the prince sitting on his nurse's lap, thinking, "There is no one on the face of the earth like my son," and he was overjoyed.

Not long after the bodhisattva was born, as he sat on his nurse's lap eating a meal of rice mixed with curds from a golden bowl, the nurse tried to stop him but could not, and he ate a great deal. She set about taking the bowl away from him, but she was unable to take it. So she told King Śuddhodana, and the king, together with the queen, set about taking it away, but they too were unable. Then the king commanded his ministers and the people living in Kapilavastu, but they too were unable to take it away. Then it was tied to five hundred elephants. The bodhisattva thought, "It would not do for my strength to be overcome by these. I shall hold the golden bowl by crooking my little finger." He crooked his finger, held on to the golden bowl and pulled, and the five hundred elephants were turned around and dragged backward, and even they were unable to take the golden bowl away.

King Śuddhodana thought, "Since the bodhisattva's left hand alone has pulled five hundred elephants, then with both hands he could without doubt match a thousand elephants. Therefore the prince shall be named One with the Strength of a Thousand Elephants." And so he was named.

In the city of Kapilavastu there dwelt from of old a yakṣa called Śākyavardhana. It was the custom among the Śākyas that whenever a boy or girl was born to any Śākya, the child would be brought to bow at the feet of the yakṣa Śākyavardhana. So the king commanded his ministers, "Wise ones, today have the prince bow at the feet of the yakṣa Śākyavardhana."

Then the bodhisattva was mounted on an elephant and, surrounded by countless troops, set out to enter the city of Kapilavastu. The city of Kapilavastu became free of twelve sounds, and when the bodhisattva entered it, it remained still, like a sage. Then King Śuddhodana thought, "This city has become free of twelve sounds: the sound of elephants, the sound of horses, the sound of chariots, the sound of foot soldiers, the sound of conches, the sound of great drums, the sound of kettledrums, the sound of horns, the sound of dancing, the sound of singing, the sound of music, and the sound of the call, 'Give gifts! Make merit! Observe the fast! Take up and keep moral discipline!' Free of these twelve sounds, it remained peaceful, like a sage, when the prince entered it. Therefore the prince shall be named Śākyamuni, Sage of the Śākyas." And so the bodhisattva was named Śākyamuni.

Then the bodhisattva was carried into the presence of the yakṣa Śākyavardhana, and the yakṣa Śākyavardhana saw the bodhisattva drawing near to his shrine. Seeing him, he came out and prostrated, touching the bodhisattva's two feet with his whole body. When King Śuddhodana saw this, he said, "Since even a god touches his feet, he is the god of gods. Therefore the prince shall be called God of Gods." And so the bodhisattva was named God of Gods.

At that time King Śuddhodana, sitting for the purpose, discerned with wisdom words and letters of profound meaning.

Then the bodhisattva Śākyamuni, together with a retinue of five hundred, was sent to the writing master Worm Armor to learn letters. The writing master Worm Armor wrote out a letter and showed it to the bodhisattva, and the bodhisattva said, "This one I know." Then he wrote out two letters, then three, and in the same way up to five hundred, and showed them to him, and the bodhisattva said, "All of these too I know. Tell me others."

Worm Armor said, "Only this many letters are used in the world. Others I do not know."

Then the bodhisattva himself wrote out a letter, gave it to Worm Armor, and said, "Tell me what this letter is called."

He replied, "I do not know."

The bodhisattva said, "It becomes manifest when two have appeared in the world: a bodhisattva and a wheel-turning monarch."

Brahmā, standing in the sky, said, "Worm Armor, it is just as the bodhisattva has said. This script becomes manifest when two have appeared in the world: a bodhisattva or a wheel-turning king."

The bodhisattva read that script aloud with the voice of Brahmā, and because Brahmā, standing in the sky, had mastery over it, it came to be called the Brahmā script.

Then, when the bodhisattva had mastered letters, he and his retinue of five hundred were entrusted to the archery masters called Good Taker and With the Gods, at the archery ground. There the bodhisattva became trained in the five skills: hitting from afar, hitting so that a sound rings out, hitting the vital spot, hitting without missing, and shooting with great force.

Then Good Taker and With the Gods thought, "This bodhisattva is compassionate, so we shall teach him everything there is to teach. But this Devadatta is malicious, and it would not do for him to bring harm to the whole world. So we shall not teach him how to strike the vital spot."

Then, at another time, Devadatta shot a goose with an iron arrow, and it fell into the lap of the bodhisattva, who was sitting on the upper terrace of the palace. The bodhisattva took hold of it, drew out the arrow and anointed it with medicine, and it recovered. Devadatta said, "Bodhisattva, this goose is mine, since I took it. Send it back!"

The bodhisattva said, "I took it long before you. I took it at the very moment I first resolved upon unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening."

At this, Devadatta felt malice toward the bodhisattva, impatience and unease.

Then five hundred Śākya youths, Devadatta among them, came out, and Devadatta girded himself with a cloth and threw down the five hundred Śākya youths. Some time later the bodhisattva went to that place, and Devadatta said, "Bodhisattva, why do you hold back?" So the bodhisattva also girded himself with a cloth and threw down the five hundred Śākya youths. Then Devadatta girded himself, and the bodhisattva seized him, turned his feet toward the sky and threw him flat on his back. At this, Devadatta conceived toward the bodhisattva malice, impatience and unease a second time.

Then the five hundred Śākya youths went outside carrying bows and arrows and set about shooting at palmyra trees. Some pierced one palmyra tree, some pierced two, and Devadatta pierced three. The bodhisattva shot an iron arrow and pierced seven palmyra trees, and so on, to be told at length, up to the point where they were shaken by the wind until they all fell down.

Now on the bank of the Rohitaka River there was a tree called Essence of Virtue, which grew a hundred cubits in a single day. At night it could be cut even with a fingernail, but by day it could not be cut even with a weapon. The gods of the Pure Abodes conjured up a wind and a violent rainstorm of such force that it uprooted the Essence of Virtue tree and cast it into the Rohitaka.

When King Śuddhodana and Suprabuddha heard of this, they thought, "Might this tree not destroy the city?" They went, together with countless hundreds of thousands of beings, to where the Essence of Virtue tree lay. But although it was dragged by countless hundreds of thousands of people, they could not move it even a little.

Udāyin said, "Your Majesty, tell the bodhisattva."

The king said, "I cannot command the prince."

Udāyin said, "Then all of you raise a loud and great uproar. One way or another I shall get the bodhisattva to come."

Then that crowd of people raised a great uproar with loud voices. Udāyin went to the bodhisattva, and the bodhisattva said, "Udāyin, what is this uproar?" Udāyin explained to him in detail all that had happened. Then the bodhisattva said, "If that is so, let us go and pull it out."

So Udāyin and the bodhisattva set out, and after a while a venomous snake came out of a hole. Thinking, "It may seize the bodhisattva," Udāyin cut it through the middle with a sharp sword. The poisonous breath of the snake struck Udāyin and he turned black, and so he came to be called Kālodāyin, Black Udāyin.

Then the bodhisattva went to where the Essence of Virtue tree was, and said to Devadatta, "Let us see how strong you are. Move it!" Devadatta strained and shifted it a little. Nanda dragged it a little. Then the bodhisattva put forth his strength, lifted the Essence of Virtue tree with his hand and threw it up from the bank of the Rohitaka River. The bodhisattva said, "Wise ones, this Essence of Virtue tree is by nature cooling. If it is applied to bile diseases, swellings and blisters, they will be soothed. Cut it into pieces, carry them off and keep them in your homes."

Again, a precious elephant was born in Vaiśālī. The Licchavis of Vaiśālī found an elephant endowed with the auspicious marks, and they thought, "The son of King Śuddhodana has been prophesied by the sign readers to become a wheel-turning king. It is through his power that this precious elephant has appeared. Let us go and offer it to him."

They adorned the elephant with every ornament, led it off and gradually reached Kapilavastu, where they stationed the excellent elephant at the gate of the royal palace and waited. By chance Devadatta, the doer of wicked deeds, came out. When he saw the excellent elephant so adorned with ornaments, great jealousy arose in him, and with jealousy and impatience he asked, "Whose is this excellent elephant?"

They said, "The assembly of Vaiśālī has offered this precious elephant as a gift to Prince Śākyamuni, whom the sign readers have prophesied will become a wheel-turning king."

Devadatta grew all the more impatient and said, "He has not even become a wheel-turner yet, and already you are offering him a precious elephant?" With that, he killed the elephant with a slap of his palm.

After a while Nanda came to that place. Seeing it, he asked, "Wise ones, who killed this fine elephant?"

They said, "Devadatta."

He thought, "Devadatta has surely shown his might upon this. I too shall show my might upon it." He seized it by the tail and flung it seven paces.

After a while the bodhisattva Śākyamuni came out. Seeing it, he asked, "Where did this elephant come from?" They told him what had happened.

"Who killed it?"

"Devadatta."

"Is this how hospitality is shown to guests? A fine thing he has done! Where was it killed?"

"Over there."

"Who dragged it here?"

"Nanda."

He thought, "Those two have surely shown their might upon this. I too shall show my might upon it." Having thought this, he seized it by the tail and flung it over the wall, so that it passed over seven walls and seven moats before it landed. Where it fell it made a hollow, and that hollow came to be called the Elephant Pit. On this it is said:

Devadatta slew the lord of elephants;
Nanda dragged it seven paces;
The bodhisattva, with his hand, through the sky
Hurled it far outside, as if it were a stone.

The sign readers had prophesied: "If the prince does not go forth into homelessness before the age of twelve, he will become a wheel-turning king."

Then King Śuddhodana thought, "What if I were to give him the five qualities of sensual pleasure in abundance? Then, delighting in them, he will not go forth." So King Śuddhodana set heaps of clothing and ornaments before the bodhisattva and said, "Today you are to give these ornaments and clothes to the Śākya maidens." Then King Śuddhodana had it proclaimed by bell throughout the city of Kapilavastu: "Today the prince will give away clothing and ornaments. Let all the Śākya maidens come!"

Then five hundred Śākya maidens came, and the bodhisattva gave clothing and ornaments to them all.

Now the Śākya Daṇḍapāṇi had a daughter called Yaśodharā, and he said to her, "The prince will give clothing and ornaments to you as well, so go." Yaśodharā thought, "Who but the bodhisattva will be my husband?" and went to the bodhisattva. By then all the clothing and ornaments had been given away and nothing at all was left. But she saw that one exceedingly beautiful and precious pearl necklace remained on the bodhisattva's neck. Delighted with it, she untied it herself without the least shyness, fastened it around her own neck, and left the bodhisattva's presence.

Then King Śuddhodana thought, "This girl is confident and steady. She will be able to fall in with the prince's mind, so she shall be taken." And so Yaśodharā, with a retinue of twenty thousand women, was taken as the bodhisattva's wife.

King Śuddhodana said to the bodhisattva, "Go and see how far the working estates extend."

The bodhisattva, heeding King Śuddhodana's command, mounted a fine chariot and went outside to view the working estates of his father King Śuddhodana. When he came to the people at work on the estates, he saw five hundred ploughs tilling the fields. The farmers too, pressing down and pulling the ploughs, were utterly drained of strength, their heads and bodies covered in dust, their bodies rough and their complexions poor from being scorched by wind and heat, their feet and hands cracked. They wore threadbare and filthy clothes, their feet were filthy, and they were sallow with hunger and thirst, their throats, cheeks and the sides of their eyes dried up. And he saw the oxen too: their bodies torn by goads with sharp iron points, so that their backs and hindquarters were covered in blood; panting with hunger and thirst, drawing long, labored breaths from deep in the chest; pus and blood oozing from the sores where the yoke pressed on them, eaten by swarms of flies and insects; their feet wounded by the iron ploughshares; saliva and mucus dripping from their mouths and noses, and covered with flies, gadflies and long-beaked mosquitoes.

Seeing this, with a heart stirred through and through by the compassion that he had generated and gathered as merit from beginningless time, he spoke these words to the farmers: "Good people, to whom do you belong?"

They answered, "We belong to the bodhisattva."

He said, "Good people, from this day on you shall not be slaves, nor shall you be servants. Be free, go wherever you please, and live in the enjoyment of happiness."

He also unyoked the oxen and said, "And you too, from this day on, eat the finest unspoiled grass and drink clear water unmuddied by dust. May cool breezes rise for you from all four directions."

Then the bodhisattva, wearied by what he had seen, looked out over the edge of the fields of his father, the Śākya Śuddhodana, and went to where there stood a rose-apple tree, glossy and green, thick with leaves and giving good shade. Sitting in the shade of the rose-apple tree, he entered into the first meditative concentration: secluded from sensual desires, secluded from evil and unwholesome states, accompanied by initial thought and by investigation, with the joy and bliss born of seclusion, resembling the undefiled.

The great crowd of people did not go near the bodhisattva but stayed apart, beneath other trees.

King Śuddhodana thought, "The bodhisattva has been gone a long time, and the morning is already past. I shall go and look for him." He mounted his chariot and went to the farmers' village, searched here and there for the bodhisattva, and came to the rose-apple tree.

By then the sun had passed midday, and the shadows of the other trees were leaning toward the east, slanting toward the east, and had moved over to the east. But through the bodhisattva's power, the shadow of the rose-apple tree did not leave the bodhisattva's body. King Śuddhodana saw that, although the sun had passed midday and the shadows of the other trees were leaning, slanting and had moved over toward the east, the shadow of the rose-apple tree did not leave the bodhisattva's body. Seeing this, he thought, "The prince has great magical powers and great might. Now that the sun has passed midday, the shadows of the other trees are leaning toward the east, slanting toward the east, and have moved over to the east, yet the shadow of the rose-apple tree does not leave the bodhisattva's body. How great a wonder!"

Then, with joyful reverence, he touched the bodhisattva's two feet and spoke in verse:

"As the earth shook on the day of your birth,
So the rose-apple's shade does not leave your body:
Great-minded one, these are your two marvels.
All-seeing one, I bow down at your feet."

Then the bodhisattva rose from that meditative absorption and mounted his chariot. After a while the guardians of treasure, with long hair, beards and nails, came carrying treasures to where the bodhisattva was, and when they arrived they said to the bodhisattva, "Lord, please accept these treasures."

The bodhisattva said, "Whose are these?"

"Lord, they belonged to men who have passed away."

The bodhisattva said, "Enough of these. What did it profit those fools to hold on to them for a while? I have no need of you. Go!" And he also spoke in verse:

"Those who told me tales of death
Have themselves become tales of the dead.
And I too, not long after I die,
Will become a tale told by others."

They cried out again, "Prince, if you will not accept them, we shall enter the great ocean."

The bodhisattva said, "Enter it as you please," and they entered the great ocean.

Then the gods of the Pure Abodes thought, "Bodhisattvas have the power of the cause, but even so the conditions must be brought about, for even the great power of the cause depends on the power of conditions." So, as the bodhisattva was passing through a charnel ground, the gods showed him a corpse that was bluish, festering, worm-eaten, bloated and gnawed. When the bodhisattva saw it, weariness with the world arose in him.

Then King Śuddhodana had the bodhisattva enter the city of Kapilavastu by another road. Crowds of women of the city of Kapilavastu were watching the bodhisattva from windows, lattice windows and balconies. Gopikā, daughter of the Śākya Good Time, was sitting on the upper terrace of a mansion, and as she gazed at the bodhisattva she dug into the ground with her big toe until it bled, yet she did not feel it. As the bodhisattva in turn gazed at Gopikā, he crushed an arrow to powder in his right hand. The charioteer handed him an iron arrow, and that too he crushed to powder. Then King Śuddhodana thought, "She will be able to fall in with the prince's mind."

Then, as the charioteer drove on, Mṛgajā, daughter of the Śākya Bell Sound, saw the bodhisattva passing by in his chariot. She too was sitting on the upper terrace of a mansion, and she spoke in verse:

"Ah, blissful is his mother,
And his father too is blissful indeed;
Ah, the woman who becomes his wife,
She too has found nirvāṇa."

That supreme being, the meditator,
Hearing the word nirvāṇa,
And knowing nirvāṇa to be peace itself,
Was delighted in his heart by it.

Then the bodhisattva, having heard the word nirvāṇa, was pleased with Mṛgajā. He took a pearl necklace from his neck and threw it to her, and there in the chariot he entered into meditative concentration. The necklace landed and clung around her neck. Then King Śuddhodana thought, "She will be able to fall in with the bodhisattva's mind." So Gopikā, surrounded by twenty thousand women, and Mṛgajā, also surrounded by twenty thousand women, were taken as the bodhisattva's wives. Thus there came to be sixty thousand wives, Yaśodharā, Gopikā, Mṛgajā and the rest.

The sign readers prophesied, "If the bodhisattva does not go forth within seven years, he will become a wheel-turning king." Then King Śuddhodana thought, "What if I were to give the bodhisattva the five qualities of sensual pleasure in abundance?" And he gave them in abundance. The sign readers prophesied, "If the bodhisattva does not go forth within seven months, he will certainly become a wheel-turning king." Then King Śuddhodana gave the bodhisattva the five qualities of sensual pleasure in still greater abundance, thinking, "In this way too, delighting in them, he will not go forth."

It is the way of things that when the bodhisattva wishes to go to the pleasure grove, he says to the charioteer, "O charioteer, quickly make ready a fine carriage, and riding in it we shall go to the pleasure grove." And so the bodhisattva commanded the charioteer, "O charioteer, quickly make ready a fine carriage, and riding in it we shall go to the pleasure grove."

"As my lord commands," said the charioteer, obeying the bodhisattva Śākyamuni. He quickly made ready a fine carriage, went to where the bodhisattva Śākyamuni was, and said to the bodhisattva Śākyamuni, "My lord, the fine carriage is ready. My lord, please consider that the time for it has come."

Then the bodhisattva Śākyamuni mounted the fine carriage and set out for the pleasure grove. And the bodhisattva Śākyamuni saw a man worn out with age, bent and crooked as a rafter, tottering, leaning forward on a staff, trembling as he walked, his hair grey, quite unlike other people. Seeing him, he said to the charioteer, "Charioteer, what is this man worn out with age, bent and crooked as a rafter, tottering, leaning forward on a staff, trembling as he walks, his hair grey, quite unlike other people?"

"My lord, this man is what is called old."

"Charioteer, what is this called old?"

"My lord, this man will die before long, and so he is called old."

"Charioteer, am I too not beyond the condition of aging, the nature of aging?"

"My lord too is not beyond the condition of aging, the nature of aging."

"Charioteer, then turn the chariot around. We shall go back to the palace. For once I have reached the palace, I must reflect on this very matter that has been spoken: that I am not beyond old age."

The charioteer turned the chariot around and set off for the palace. There the bodhisattva Śākyamuni entered the palace and, thinking, "Consider: I too am not beyond old age!", was displeased and brooded unhappily. On this it is said:

Seeing a man past his prime, aged, bent and grey-headed,
Holding a staff in his hand,
And hearing it said, "I am not beyond this old age,"
He then was displeased and brooded unhappily.

Then King Śuddhodana said to the charioteer, "Charioteer, did the prince go out to the pleasure grove glad at heart and content? Did he delight in the pleasure grove?"

"No, Your Majesty."

"Why was that?"

"Your Majesty, as the prince was going to the pleasure grove, he saw a man worn out with age, bent and crooked as a rafter, tottering, leaning forward on a staff, trembling as he walked, his hair grey, quite unlike other people. Seeing him, he said these words to me: 'Charioteer, what is this man worn out with age, bent and crooked as a rafter, tottering, leaning forward on a staff, trembling as he walks, his hair grey, quite unlike other people?' To that I replied, 'My lord, this is what is called old.' He said, 'Charioteer, what is this called old?' To that I replied, 'My lord, this man will die before long, and so, my lord, he is called old.' He said, 'Charioteer, am I too not beyond the condition of aging, the nature of aging?' To that I replied, 'My lord too is not beyond the condition of aging, the nature of aging.' He said, 'Charioteer, then turn the chariot around. We shall go back to the palace. For once I have reached the palace, I must reflect on this very matter that has been spoken: that I am not beyond old age.' Your Majesty, the prince went into the palace, and then, thinking, 'Consider: I am not beyond old age!', he was displeased and brooded unhappily."

Then King Śuddhodana thought, "Surely the words of those brahmin sign readers and soothsayers will not come true? Will the prince shave off his hair and beard, put on saffron robes, and out of genuine faith go forth from home into homelessness? What if I were to give the prince the five qualities of sensual pleasure in abundance, so that one way or another he delights in them and does not go forth?" Then King Śuddhodana gave the bodhisattva Śākyamuni the five qualities of sensual pleasure in abundance, so that one way or another he would delight in them and not go forth. On this it is said:

Hearing the words of the charioteer,
His father, fearful for the prince,
So that he would not go again to the grove of delight,
