Gave him in abundance the five qualities of sensual pleasure.

Then, as is the way of things, when the bodhisattva wished to go to the pleasure grove, he would say to the charioteer, "O charioteer, quickly make ready a fine carriage, and riding in it I shall go to the pleasure grove." And so the bodhisattva said to the charioteer, "O charioteer, quickly make ready a fine carriage. Riding in it I shall go to the pleasure grove."

"As my lord commands," said the charioteer, obeying the bodhisattva Śākyamuni. He made ready a fine carriage, went to where the bodhisattva Śākyamuni was, and said to the bodhisattva Śākyamuni, "My lord, the fine carriage is ready. My lord, please consider that the time for it has come."

Then the bodhisattva Śākyamuni mounted the fine carriage and set out for the pleasure grove. And the bodhisattva Śākyamuni saw a man who was pallid and bloated, emaciated and feeble, his faculties failing, whom most people could not bear to look at. Seeing him, he said to the charioteer, "Charioteer, what is this man, pallid and bloated, emaciated and feeble, his faculties failing, whom most people cannot bear to look at?"

"My lord, this is what is called one stricken with fever."

"Charioteer, what is this called stricken with fever?"

"My lord, this man has reached the point at which he will die. My lord, this is what is called stricken with fever."

"Charioteer, am I too not beyond the condition of sickness, the nature of sickness?"

"My lord too is not beyond the condition of sickness, the nature of sickness."

"Charioteer, then turn the chariot around. We shall go back to the palace. For once I have reached the palace, I must reflect on this very matter that has been spoken: that I am not beyond sickness."

The charioteer turned the chariot around and set off for the palace. There the bodhisattva Śākyamuni entered the palace and, thinking, "Consider: I too am not beyond sickness!", was displeased and brooded unhappily. On this it is said:

Seeing here a man with wasted body, pallid and bloated,
Stricken by an illness beyond his control,
And thinking, "Consider: I am not beyond sickness,"
He then was displeased and brooded unhappily.

Then King Śuddhodana said to the charioteer, "Charioteer, did the prince go out to the pleasure grove glad at heart and content? Did he come to delight in the pleasure grove?"

"No, Your Majesty."

"Why was that?"

"Your Majesty, as the prince was going out to the pleasure grove, he saw a man who was pallid and bloated, emaciated and feeble, his faculties failing, whom most people could not bear to look at. Seeing him, he said to me, 'Charioteer, what is this man, pallid and bloated, emaciated and feeble, his faculties failing, whom most people cannot bear to look at?' To that I replied, 'My lord, this is what is called one stricken with fever.' He said, 'Charioteer, what is this called stricken with sickness?' To that I replied, 'This man has reached the point at which this affliction will kill him. My lord, that is what is called stricken with fever.' He said, 'Charioteer, am I too not beyond the condition of sickness, the nature of sickness?' To that I replied, 'My lord too is not beyond the condition of sickness, the nature of sickness.'"

He said, "Charioteer, therefore turn the chariot back. We shall go to the palace itself. For in this way, once I have gone to the palace, I must reflect on the very meaning of what has been declared: 'I am not beyond sickness.'" The prince went into the palace and, thinking, "Just look: I too am not beyond sickness!", he was displeased and grew dejected.

Then King Śuddhodana thought, "Surely the words of those brahmins, the readers of marks and interpreters of omens, will not come true?" and so on, as before. Here it is said:

Then, so that he would take delight and not set his mind on leaving,
Forms and sounds, smells and tastes,
And pleasing objects of touch, the very finest,
The five qualities of sensual pleasure, he provided as well.

As the nature of reality would have it, the bodhisattva Śākyamuni came to wish to go out to the grounds of the pleasure garden, and he gave the command to the charioteer, and so on, as before. The bodhisattva Śākyamuni saw a canopy made of cloth of many colors stretched out, a bier being carried, lamps held up in front, a great crowd of people going on ahead, and women with disheveled hair following behind, weeping.

Seeing this, he said to the charioteer, "Charioteer, what is this, with a canopy made of cloth of many colors stretched out, a bier being carried, lamps held up in front, a great crowd of people going on ahead, and women with disheveled hair following behind, weeping?"

"My lord, this is what is called one who has died."

"Charioteer, what is this called dead?"

"My lord, from now on this man will not see his beloved father and mother, nor his sons and daughters, his male and female servants, his workers, and the people who live on his wages; and from now on they too will not see him. My lord, that is what is called one who has died."

"Charioteer, am I too not beyond the condition of death, the nature of death?"

"My lord too is not beyond the condition of death, the nature of death."

"Charioteer, therefore turn the chariot back. We shall go to the palace itself. For in this way, once I have gone to the palace, I must reflect on the very meaning of what has been declared: 'I am not beyond death.'"

The charioteer turned the chariot back and set out for the palace itself. There the bodhisattva Śākyamuni went into the palace and, thinking, "Just look: I too am not beyond death!", he was displeased and grew dejected. Here it is said:

Seeing the corpse of a man without mind,
Parted from perception, his life span exhausted,
He thought, "Just look: I am not beyond death!"
And then he was displeased and grew dejected.

Then King Śuddhodana said to the charioteer, and so on; this is as before. Here it is said:

In that excellent city, glorious and delightful,
He who had become a god of gods, like a lamp,
Was gladdened in every way by the five qualities of sensual pleasure,
Like the Thousand-Eyed (Indra) dwelling in the Nandana Grove.

Then the gods of the Pure Abodes thought, "Although the bodhisattva possesses the power of the cause, still the condition must be brought about, for even a great power of the cause depends upon the power of the condition." With this thought, they emanated a man with shaven head, wearing saffron robes and holding an alms bowl in his hand, wandering and going from house to house and from dwelling to dwelling.

As the nature of reality would have it, the bodhisattva came to wish to go out to the grounds of the pleasure garden, and so on; this is as before. When he had come out to the grounds of the pleasure garden, the bodhisattva Śākyamuni saw a man with shaven head, wearing saffron robes and holding an alms bowl in his hand, wandering and going from house to house and from dwelling to dwelling.

Seeing him, he said to the charioteer, "Charioteer, what is this man, with shaven head and an alms bowl in his hand, wandering and going from house to house and from dwelling to dwelling, whose clothing is of a different color, in no way like that of other people?"

"My lord, that is what is called one who has gone forth."

"Charioteer, what is this called one who has gone forth?"

"My lord, that one who has gone forth, knowing that it is good to be disciplined, good to be perfectly restrained, good to act for the benefit of others, good to practice the Dharma, good to practice virtue, and good to practice what is wholesome, shaved off his hair and beard, put on saffron robes, and out of genuine faith alone went forth from home into homelessness. My lord, this is what is called one who has gone forth."

"Charioteer, therefore turn this chariot aside to where that one who has gone forth is."

"As my lord commands," said the charioteer, and, heeding the bodhisattva Śākyamuni, he turned the chariot to where the one who had gone forth was. Then the bodhisattva Śākyamuni spoke thus to the one who had gone forth: "O man, why do you have a shaven head and an alms bowl in your hand, wandering and going from house to house and from dwelling to dwelling, and why is your clothing of a different color, in no way like that of other people?"

He replied, "Prince, I am what is called one who has gone forth."

"O man, in what way are you called one who has gone forth?"

"Prince, knowing that it is good to be disciplined, good to be perfectly restrained, good to act for the benefit of others, good to practice the Dharma, good to practice virtue, and good to practice what is wholesome, I shaved off my hair and beard, put on saffron robes, and out of genuine faith alone went forth from home into homelessness. In that way I am called one who has gone forth."

"O man, knowing that it is good to be disciplined, good to be perfectly restrained, good to act for the benefit of others, good to practice the Dharma, good to practice virtue, and good to practice what is wholesome, you have shaved off your hair and beard, put on saffron robes, and out of genuine faith alone gone forth from home into homelessness. Excellent!"

Then the bodhisattva Śākyamuni said to the charioteer, "Charioteer, therefore turn the chariot back. We shall go to the palace itself. For in this way, once I have gone to the palace, I must reflect on just this matter."

The charioteer turned the chariot back and set out for the palace itself. When he had arrived there at the palace, the bodhisattva Śākyamuni reflected on just this matter, thinking, "Ah! I shall shave off my hair and beard, put on saffron robes, and out of genuine faith alone go forth from home into homelessness."

Then King Śuddhodana spoke to the charioteer, and so on; this is as before.

Then the great Mahāprajāpatī dreamed a dream: Indra's banner fell; the Śākyas were filled with sweepings; the earth turned over; glory departed from the house; outside, she saw herself weeping and laughing for an instant; and the sun set in the very place where it had risen. When the bodhisattva heard this, he reflected, "I am the place where the Śākyas' banner of Indra stands, and if I leave the city, glory will depart."

King Śuddhodana too dreamed a dream: the bed of glory broke, and the father's lineage was cut off. When the bodhisattva heard this, he thought, "That is just how it is. I have become the ground of Yaśodharā's glory and the ground of Śuddhodana's paternal lineage, so if I go forth, the lineage of the Śākyas will be cut off."

In the presence of King Śuddhodana, the readers of marks had prophesied, "If the prince does not go forth within seven days, he will become a wheel-turning monarch." They also said this:

"If you wish to see his good fortune,
Then guard him for seven days,
And, O lord of men, just as the parasol is unfurled,
There is no doubt the seven precious things will appear.

Your son will become a king,
The lord of the seven precious things.
The earth bounded by the ocean
He will protect without punishment.

But if he abandons the earth
And goes, unafraid, into the forest,
He will attain omniscience
And bring about the liberation of beings."

So it was heard. Having heard it, the four, Śuddhodana, Śuklodana, Droṇodana and Amṛtodana, gathered together in one place and began to take counsel: "Wise friends, the brahmins who read marks and interpret omens have prophesied of the bodhisattva Śākyamuni that if he does not go forth within seven days he will become a wheel-turning monarch. Therefore we must guard the bodhisattva closely for seven days, and we must guard the city well too."

Then they surrounded the city of Kapilavastu with seven ramparts and seven moats. The doors of the city gates were made of iron, and bells with a very clear ring were fastened to the doors, so that if a gate were opened its sound would be heard for a league all around. In the bodhisattva's mansion they had captivating and beautiful women attend on him with dancing, song and music. They had a very large ladder made that could be moved, which five hundred men were needed to lift. The place where the steed Kaṇṭhaka stood was covered with copper plates and surrounded by a net of golden bells hung around it.

The bodhisattva went into the upper story of the mansion, where there were no men, and there he stayed in great splendor, taking up lutes, flutes, bronze drums, sweet-sounding instruments and other kinds of musical instruments.

At night King Śuddhodana stayed outside the eastern gate with the fourfold army. In the south was Droṇodana, in the west Śuklodana, and in the north Amṛtodana. The one making the rounds of the center of the city was Mahānāman the Śākya, with the fourfold army.

He went to the eastern gate and said, "Who keeps watch? Who keeps watch?"

King Śuddhodana said, "I keep watch."

"My lord, keep watch! Keep watch, and it will go well for you," he said, and he spoke in verse:

"The sleeping world is like a corpse;
The one who sleeps has fallen into Māra's power.
The one who does not sleep is ever wise,
So do not sleep: keep watch!"

He went to the southern gate and said, "Who keeps watch? Who keeps watch?"

Droṇodana said, "I keep watch."

"My lord, keep watch! Keep watch, and it will go well for you," he said, and he spoke in verse:

"The sleeping world is like a corpse;
The one who sleeps has fallen into Māra's power;
The one who sleeps has fallen into the power of the Lord of Death.
So do not sleep: keep watch!"

He went to the western gate and said, "Who keeps watch? Who keeps watch?"

Śuklodana said, "I keep watch."

"My lord, keep watch! Keep watch, and it will go well for you," he said, and he spoke in verse:

"The one who sleeps is always drunk;
He lies like one who has drunk a measure of liquor,
Like one asleep in a lonely forest.
So do not sleep: keep watch!"

He went to the northern gate and said, "Who keeps watch? Who keeps watch?"

Amṛtodana said, "I keep watch."

"My lord, keep watch! Keep watch, and it will go well for you," he said, and he spoke in verse:

"Like one asleep on a mountain or asleep in a mountain gorge,
Or one asleep in the waters of the ocean,
Like one asleep at the top of a tree:
So do not sleep: keep watch!"

He went to the crossroads and the three-way junctions in the center and said, "Who keeps watch? Who keeps watch?"

The guards said, "We keep watch."

"Keep watch! Keep watch, and it will go well," he said, and he spoke in verse:

"Practice the Dharma; do not practice what is not Dharma.
Speak the truth; do not speak falsehood.
Otherwise you will enter into great darkness.
So do not sleep: keep watch!"

Then, when night had turned to dawn, Mahānāman the Śākya said to King Śuddhodana, "My lord, day has broken. Of the seven days, one day has passed and six days remain."

King Śuddhodana said, "Excellent! When six days have passed and the prince has become a wheel-turning monarch, we shall travel across the face of the sky and follow him to the four continents."

It was the same, up to "one day remains."

Then Śakra, lord of the gods, thought:

"This sage, the son of the Śākya lord of men,
The great being who has perfected the six perfections,
Wishes to go to the grove of austerities that gladdens the heart,
The forest in which the noble ones delight."

Then the bodhisattva thought, "Others may come to say of me, on this basis, 'Prince Śākyamuni is not a man; that is why he abandons Yaśodharā, Gopikā, Mṛgajā and sixty thousand women and goes forth.' So I shall make love with Yaśodharā." With that thought, he made love with Yaśodharā, and Yaśodharā conceived a child. She thought, "When dawn comes, I shall tell the bodhisattva."

Then the bodhisattva, in keeping at that moment with the way things arise in dependence, spoke in verse:

"This night is the last time
That I sleep at home with a woman.
From now on I shall not dwell
At home together with a woman."

Then those women, worn out and heavy with fatigue from dancing, singing and music, fell asleep, saliva dripping from their mouths, their hair disheveled, their clothes and arms flung loose, blurting out whatever senseless words came to them. The bodhisattva saw all those women asleep, saliva dripping from their mouths, their hair disheveled, their clothes and arms flung loose, blurting out whatever senseless words came to them. Seeing this, there arose in him the perception of his circle of consorts as a charnel ground. Then disenchantment arose in the bodhisattva, and he spoke in verse:

"Like a bed of lotuses struck by the force of the wind, here on the ground
They have fallen, their arms and legs sprawled out,
Their hair disheveled, their private parts and buttocks exposed.
Seeing them, the son of the Śākyas is filled with disenchantment by their very nature.

Seeing this throng of young women, devoid of all decorum,
As like the frightful nature of a charnel ground,
If one should still delight today in the domain of childish beings,
Would one not be bereft of mind, intelligence and mindfulness?

Base desires are like a swamp, like arrows and spears, like poison and fire.
They are like a dream, and like the taste of salt water.
Desire brings ruin and has become the cause of strife and evil.
It ends badly, like the mouth of a snake. I shall abandon it."

Then, as the bodhisattva lay on his great couch, that very night he dreamed five dreams. He made the great earth his bed and placed Sumeru, king of mountains, as his pillow; his left hand entered the great eastern ocean, his right hand entered the great western ocean, and his two feet entered the great ocean. A kind of grass called Upright sprang from his navel and rose up, reaching into the sky. Birds that were completely white with black heads touched his two feet and could be seen up as far as his kneecaps. Birds of many kinds came from the four directions and, in front of him, all became a single color. And he walked upon a heap of filth without being soiled by the filth.

Having dreamed thus, he was glad at heart and thought, "Since I have dreamed such dreams as these, before long I shall attain unsurpassed wisdom."

Yaśodharā too saw in a dream that all her teeth fell out, that her conch-shell armlet broke, that the moon fell to the ground, and that the stars wept. When Yaśodharā told the bodhisattva the dream she had dreamed, the bodhisattva said:

"Your teeth are still in your mouth,
And the moon is still in the sky.
So clear away the torment in your mind,
And sleep happily, without fear."

Then Yaśodharā thought:

"Since I have dreamed fearful dreams,
My mind is filled with apprehension.
Tonight, surely I shall not
Be parted from my beloved?"

Then Śakra, lord of the gods, thought, "Although bodhisattvas possess the power of the cause, still it must be accomplished through the condition, for even a great power of the cause depends upon the power of the condition." With this thought, he went to where the bodhisattva was, and when he had arrived, he stayed in the mansion and said this to the bodhisattva:

"Like a monkey of the forest roaming its domain,
Your mind is like a colt left to run free.
If you turn away from the qualities of sensual pleasure,
You will see a light far brighter than these.

Rise up, rise up, you of excellent mind!
Abandon the earth and depart,
And once you have attained omniscience,
Bring beings across to liberation."

The bodhisattva replied:

"Kauśika, do you not see?
Like the lord of beasts encircled by a net am I.
Powerful men bearing bows, weapons and swords,
With horses, elephants, chariots and a great host of troops,
Have encircled the city of Kapilavastu on every side.

This host of Śākyas, Śuddhodana and the rest,
Mounted on elephants and horses, their bodies well armored,
And grim men of every kind with weapons in hand,
Are stationed within the courtyards, the stables and the mansion.

The palace and the doors of the house,
And likewise the gates of the city,
Have been shut and firmly bolted,
And so are guarded on every side.

With the sound of horns, of paṭaha drums and of drums,
Stationed within the courtyards, stables and mansion,
Throngs of people, striving with elephants and cavalry,
Keep constant guard so as not to let me go."

Śakra said:

"Remember the prophecy of the gentle Dīpaṃkara,
And this world tormented by suffering,
And keep in mind your former vow:
Abandon your household and go forth with resolve.

So that you may go, without obstacles,
To the forest this very day,
We gods, together with Brahmā and the rest,
Shall bring it about."

The bodhisattva said, "Excellent!" and was pleased.

Then Śakra, lord of the gods, made everyone drunk with sleep and commanded Pāñcika, the great general of the yakṣas: "Friend, the bodhisattva is going to come down from the upper story of the mansion. Bring the ladder!" He fetched the ladder, and the bodhisattva came down.

Then, as soon as Śakra gave the signal, the bodhisattva went to where his attendant Chandaka was. When he found Chandaka overcome by deep sleep, the bodhisattva roused him with effort and spoke to him in the melody of verse:

"Get up, Chandaka! Quickly, from the stable,
Bring my steed, the precious Kaṇṭhaka.
To the grove of austerities enjoyed by the Victors of old,
Which gladdens the sages, I am going."

Chandaka opened his sleepy eyes and said:

"My lord, it is not the hour for the pleasure garden.
Gentle one, you have no quarrel with anyone.
Since you have no enemy whatever,
What need have you of a mount at midnight?"

The bodhisattva declared:

"Chandaka, if never once
Have you disobeyed my word,
Then now, at this my final hour,
Do not disobey this word of mine."

Chandaka said, "Prince, this night is full of fear and terror, so I will not fetch the mount."

The bodhisattva thought, "If I ask him again and again, people will surely wake up. I shall go myself and make ready Kaṇṭhaka, the king of horses." With that thought, he himself opened the door of the palace and set about taking hold of Kaṇṭhaka, the king of horses. But the horse twisted its neck, shied away this way and that, and would not stand still.

Then the bodhisattva stroked its head with his hand, a hand produced by many hundreds of merits, bearing the wheel, the auspicious mark and the swastika, a hand that brings relief to the frightened, and he said:

"Kaṇṭhaka, take up this final burden,
And carry me without hindrance to the forest of austerities.
Once I have attained awakening, I shall swiftly satisfy all beings
With the rain of meditative concentration and liberation."

As is the nature of things, even an animal does not balk once it has been instructed, so Kaṇṭhaka, the king of horses, stood without moving. Then joy arose in the bodhisattva, and he saddled Kaṇṭhaka, the king of horses.

At that moment Mahānāman the Śākya, who was making the rounds, inspecting the people, came to that spot and saw the bodhisattva preparing to depart. Then, his voice choking and his eyes full of tears, he said, "Prince, what are you doing?"

"Mahānāman, I am going."

"Prince, this is not right."

The bodhisattva said, "Mahānāman, this is exactly what is right. For three incalculable eons, through many hundreds of thousands of hardships, I have striven perfectly for awakening for the sake of sentient beings. How, then, could I bear to remain at home now? This is certain: I am going to the grove of austerities.

When the wise swiftly escape
From a great house that is ablaze,
It is not fitting to call this wrong;
It is indeed worthy of praise.

Likewise, when one wishes to escape
From the cage of existence, blazing fiercely
With old age, sickness and death,
What do you see in this that is not right?"

When he rebuked him thus with forceful words, tears streamed like rain from Mahānāman's face, and he broke into lament: "Alas!

Today the hope of King Śuddhodana
And of all the Śākyas,
Pondered on again and again,
Has come to nothing.

Today King Śuddhodana,
Tormented by grief for his son,
Will suffer, raising up his arms,
And utter unbearable cries.

Gopikā, Yaśodharā, Mṛgajā,
And the great circle of consorts as well,
Through being parted from Siddhārtha,
Will undergo terrible suffering."

Having said this, he woke Yaśodharā in alarm and said:

"Know this: Siddhārtha is going!
Lest you be tormented by grief for your husband
And come to be wretched with suffering,
Turn your beloved husband back!

Once your husband has gone, he will be hard to see.
Look at him: this is the last time you will see your husband.

Alas, wretched me! Though I cried out in the night,
No one answered me, so the fault is not mine."

But though he cried out in this way,
Through the blessing of the gods,
Like one shouting in the wilderness,
No one at all awoke.

Then the bodhisattva thought, "To go without seeing my father would not be in keeping with my way." With this thought, he went to where King Śuddhodana was, and when he arrived, King Śuddhodana was overcome by sleep. Then he thought, "Although I have become the father of the whole world, the teacher of the world and the eldest of the world, even so, father and mother are a person's teachers; they are as gods and as Brahmā to him, and they are his great benefactors." With that thought, he bowed at his father's two feet, circled him three times, and spoke these words:

"I too have love in my heart,
And I too take joy in my kin;
But saṃsāra is evil by its very nature,
And having seen this, I am leaving.

If there were no harmful suffering
That comes from old age, sickness and death
And from parting with beloved kin,
Who would ever leave you?

For, though we do not wish it,
The great circle of our kin,
Our friends, our wives and our wealth
Before long will pass from us.

Therefore, with a mind free of attachment,
In order to be fully freed from saṃsāra,
I have left my loving kin
And set out for the forest where no people are.

Since every gathering of embodied beings
Must surely end in separation,
Think of me with love, and do not
Grieve in your bewilderment."

Having said this, he set out. Then Mahānāman woke King Śuddhodana and said:

"Siddhārtha is departing!
Lord of the earth, turn him back!
Stricken with grief for your son,
Tormented by suffering, you will weep."

So he said, but the king too did not wake.

Then Śakra, lord of the gods, spoke to four sons of the gods, Sprout, Maṅkura, Radiant and Leaf-Clad: "Friends, are you able to lift up the bodhisattva?"

They said, "We are well able indeed."

"How much strength do you have?"

Sprout said, "I have such strength that I could carry all the mountains there are on the earth on one shoulder and go along with little fatigue."

Maṅkura said, "I too could carry all the water there is on the earth on one shoulder and go along."

Radiant said, "I too could carry all the dust there is on the earth on my shoulder and go along with little fatigue."

Leaf-Clad said, "I too could carry all the grass, trees, branches and leaves there are on the earth on one shoulder and go along with little fatigue."

Śakra said, "Friends, since you have such strength, you are able to lift the bodhisattva."

Then the four, Sprout, Maṅkura, Radiant and Leaf-Clad, took the precious steed Kaṇṭhaka and went to where the bodhisattva was. When they arrived they said, "Bodhisattva, why do you wait?"

Then the bodhisattva thought, "These will grow proud, thinking, 'We lifted up the bodhisattva and carried him through the sky.'" With that thought, the bodhisattva blessed his body to become a vajra body, and they were not able even to move him. Filled with wonder, they said, "Prince, we did not know that bodhisattvas had such strength."

Then the bodhisattva mounted the precious steed Kaṇṭhaka, and thus, through the bodhisattva power of the bodhisattvas and the divine power of the gods, the precious steed Kaṇṭhaka and Chandaka rose up into the sky above.

Then the gods, Śakra, Brahmā and the rest, surrounded by many hundreds of thousands of gods, went to where the bodhisattva was. When they arrived, they went along encircling the bodhisattva. On the right went the gods who dwell in the form realm, with peaceful bearing; on the left went those who dwell in the desire realm, raising victory banners and pennants, with many kinds of special divine music; and in front went Śakra, Brahmā and the others, each in his own manner.

Many hundreds of thousands of gods stationed in the expanse of the sky scattered divine blue lotuses, red lotuses, night lilies and white lotuses over the bodhisattva. They scattered powdered aloeswood, powdered sandalwood and powdered tamāla leaves, and divine mandārava flowers as well. They played many kinds of divine musical instruments, and they scattered garments too. Here it is said:

In the sky the gods raised great sounds of laughter,
And with joyful minds they waved their garments.
Some sang the praises of the bodhisattva,
And in the sky they played hundreds of instruments.

Some opened the gates,
And strewed mandārava flowers.
Some held the hooves of his mount,
And, gazing at the Sugata, followed after him.

Some followed after on the right,
And those going on the left followed after too.
Śakra, likewise, together with Brahmā,
And Kubera himself, showed him the way.

Like the full moon amid the hosts of stars,
The great being, with the hosts of gods going before him,
Went, glad at heart, to the forest of austerities that gladdens the sages,
The forest in which the noble ones delight.

Then the bodhisattva, to encourage Kaṇṭhaka, said:

"Do not tire, do not tire; hold fast to your strength.
Take up today this one chance to serve beings.
Before long I shall attain awakening,
And satisfy beings with meditative concentration and liberation."

The bodhisattva traveled twelve leagues and the next morning arrived at the city of Peerless.

Then, as the gods were wondering, "Now that the bodhisattva has gone forth, will he not enter his own city again?", he turned his whole body to the right, looked back with the gaze of an elephant, and said:

"Until I have attained the supreme path
That the buddhas have taught,
I shall not enter again
The city of Kapilavastu."

Then they circled the bodhisattva's feet three times and went back to their own abodes.

The bodhisattva dismounted, took off his ornaments, and told Chandaka, "Chandaka, take the ornaments and the horse, and go back." Here it is said:

"Charioteer, give this horse of mine
And these ornaments to my kin.
Having abandoned vast desires,
I shall go forth right here, now.

These desires are like a razor's edge,
Like a blazing pit of fire, and they end badly.
Out of compassion, to benefit the world, I have abandoned them.
I have gone to the wilderness and will not turn back home."

Then Chandaka, tears streaming like rain down his face, said to the bodhisattva:

"In a forest teeming with lions and tigers,
And full of herdsmen,
All alone, parted from your kin,
How will the noble one get by?"

The bodhisattva said:

"When beings are born, they are born alone.
Likewise, when they die, they die alone.
Alone they undergo suffering.
In saṃsāra there is no companion."

Chandaka said:

"Alas! The prince has never seen suffering.
With tender feet, used to riding elephants and horses,
How will he go on foot over ground that is harsh, full of grass and stones,
And wander in the forest?"

The bodhisattva said:

"The terror of the Lord of Death is never once
Absent for the fortunate, nor for those used to comfort,
Nor for those who have protectors, nor for those of noble birth,
Nor for the brave and the venerable, nor for people who rejoice.

It is said that old age
Brings on the terrors of the Lord of Death.
With one's purpose not done, not even begun,
One goes on to death as well."

Chandaka said, "The aged king will die of grief for his son." But because the bodhisattva had gathered the accumulations for awakening, Chandaka's words did not move his heart.

Then the bodhisattva drew from its scabbard his sword, which was like a blue lotus, cut off his topknot, and tossed it up into the sky. Śakra, lord of the gods, caught it and, showing it great honor, carried it among the gods of the Heaven of the Thirty-Three, where he instituted a great festival in honor of the topknot.

Then Śakra, lord of the gods, thought, "Because of the pride of his royal caste, the prince will not be able to turn back from this," and he rejoiced.

Then the bodhisattva said to Chandaka, "Do not lead Kaṇṭhaka into the palace." When he had said this, Chandaka, crying aloud, his eyes full of tears, led away Kaṇṭhaka, king of horses, looking back again and again at the bodhisattva as he went, and came to Kapilavastu. There an uproar arose, and at length people said to King Śuddhodana, "Your Majesty, the prince has come; please rejoice." In the city of Kapilavastu, too, a great proclamation rang out. When Kaṇṭhaka, king of horses, entered the palace, the whole company of royal women clung to Kaṇṭhaka's neck and began to weep. Kaṇṭhaka's breath went inward, and he died on that very spot. After his death he took rebirth in the family of a renowned brahmin devoted to the six duties.

Then the bodhisattva, traveling by stages, came to the hermitage of the sage Shunner of Evil. At that time the sage Shunner of Evil's son was sitting with his cheek resting on his hand, brooding. Seeing him like that, the bodhisattva said, "Great sage, why are you sitting with your cheek on your hand, brooding?"

He replied, "In this hermitage of mine the tāla trees bore golden leaves, flowers, and fruit, but they have not remained as they were before."

The bodhisattva said, "Great sage, the one by whose power the tāla trees in this hermitage bore golden leaves, flowers, and fruit has truly seen old age, sickness, and death, has grown weary of the world, and now dwells in the forest of austerities. Had he not truly seen old age, sickness, and death, grown weary, and come to dwell in the forest of austerities, this pleasure grove would have been his."

When he had said this, the son of the sage Shunner of Evil began to examine the bodhisattva. Seeing his handsome form and peaceful bearing, he examined him for a long time, and, filled with wonder, said, "O renunciant, could that one be you?"

The bodhisattva said, "Great sage, it is certainly so."

Then the son of Shunner of Evil, staring with eyes wide in amazement, first invited the bodhisattva to a seat and offered him flowers and fruit.

From "In the city of Peerless there was a householder, rich, wealthy, and of great possessions," up to "ten sons were born to him," all those sons went forth and realized the awakening of a pratyekabuddha. In time, their old mother gave those great beings a pair of hempen garments, and they said, "Mother, please understand this. The son of King Śuddhodana, Prince Sarvārthasiddha, having seen old age, sickness, and death, has gone to the forest with a mind thoroughly weary of the world, and he will fully and manifestly awaken to unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening. Therefore, since we cannot remain in a buddha's field, we shall pass into final nirvāṇa. Offer these hempen garments to the son of King Śuddhodana. That great being will realize unsurpassed wisdom, and you too will obtain a great fruit." Having said this, they performed the miracles of blazing, shining, sending down rain, and flashing lightning, and passed into final nirvāṇa in the element of nirvāṇa without remainder of the aggregates.

When the old woman was at the point of death, she entrusted those garments to her daughter and told her all that had happened. Her daughter in turn was struck by illness, and, knowing she would die, she too placed them at the foot of a tree and prayed to the god dwelling in that tree, "Please offer these to the son of King Śuddhodana."

Since the knowledge and vision of Śakra (Indra), lord of the gods, reaches down to the realms below, he learned of them and took them. Then, magically transforming himself into what looked like a decrepit old hunter, he put them on, took a bow and arrows in his hand, and sat down in the middle of the road the bodhisattva would travel. The bodhisattva, traveling by stages, came to that road and saw a hunter holding a bow and arrows and wearing ochre garments. Seeing him, he said to the man, "Good man, those hempen garments are fitting for one who has gone forth. Take these fine Kāśī cloths and give those to me."

He replied, "Prince, others would say of me, 'You killed the young prince and took these garments of fine Kāśī cloth,' and on that ground I would be put to death. So I will not give them to you."

The bodhisattva said, "Good man, the whole world knows what power I possess. Who could kill me? Who would believe that you had killed me? Give them without fear."

Then Śakra, lord of the gods, touched his head to the bodhisattva's two feet, offered him the hempen garments, and took the fine Kāśī cloths. The hempen garments did not fit the bodhisattva's body, and he thought, "Ah, may these hempen garments fit my body." No sooner had he spoken the words than, by the bodhisattva power of bodhisattvas and the divine power of the gods, those hempen garments came to fit the bodhisattva's body.

Then the bodhisattva thought, "Now that I have gone forth, I should take beings into my care." Then the bodhisattva, wandering by stages, arrived at Rājagṛha. Since the bodhisattva was skilled in every craft and trade, he made an alms bowl from the leaves of a karavīra tree, and with calm deportment entered Rājagṛha for alms.

At that time King Bimbisāra, who was on the upper terrace of his palace, saw him going on his alms round, graceful in going and returning, in looking ahead and looking about, in bending and stretching, and in bearing his outer robe and alms bowl. Seeing him, he thought, "Of all the many renunciants who live in Rājagṛha, there is not one with deportment like this renunciant's."

On this it is said:

I shall proclaim the going forth,
How the one with vision went forth,
How he, having examined it well,
Chose the going forth with full resolve.
This household life is suffering;
It is a place of dust,
While going forth is like the open sky:
Knowing this, he then went forth.
Having gone forth, with his body
He abandoned deeds of evil,
And having abandoned evil of speech,
He made his livelihood wholly pure.

Then to the foremost city of Magadha,
Rājagṛha, he went,
And when the protector, vigilant and mindful,
Went there on his round for alms,
The skilled lord of Magadha,
Seated in his palace, saw him.
Seeing him, he reflected, and then
He spoke thus to his ministers:
"Richly adorned with the supreme marks,
Perfect in his bodily frame,
Looking only a yoke's length ahead:
Look at this man, you wise ones!
His eyes are lowered, his mind upright;
He was not born of a lowly family.
Where will he go to stay?"
The king sent messengers to follow him.
Then he commanded the messengers:
"Wherever that monk goes,
And wherever he makes his dwelling,
Follow close behind him."

He, going on his round for alms,
His six doors well restrained,
Vigilant and mindful,
Swiftly filled his alms bowl.
And when he had completed his alms round,
The sage went out from the city,
Went on to Mount Pāṇḍava,
And there he made his dwelling.
Knowing that he had made his dwelling,
One messenger stayed behind,
While the other swiftly returned
And spoke these words to the king:
"Great king, that monk,
Like the cub of a lion or a tiger
Dwelling in a mountain cave,
Dwells upon Mount Pāṇḍava."

Hearing the messengers' words,
The lord of the earth mounted his chariot,
And, surrounded by his ministers,
He set out with all speed.
He rode as far as the ground would allow,
Then he alighted from his carriage,
Went close on foot,
And then he saw the Tathāgata.
The king sat down, and having exchanged
Pleasant and agreeable words,
Neither haughty nor coarse,
He spoke these words:
"Monk, you are in the very bloom
Of your first youth;
For one of such splendor as this,
Wandering for alms is not becoming.
Delightful houses,
Women adorned with ornaments,
And the finest enjoyments, if you would partake of them:
Tell me, I ask, your lineage."

"O king, near the snowy mountains
Lies a country full of wealth
And full of grain,
Renowned by the name of Kosala.
There dwell the Śākyas,
Of the Ikṣvāku line, of the solar clan.
O king, I am of the warrior caste,
And I do not seek the objects of desire.

Though one possessed this earth with its mountains and its oceans,
Rich with every kind of jewel,
A man would never be sated,
Like a fire fed with wood in the scorching heat of summer.
Desires are said to be bound up with enmity;
O lord of men, they are hard to cross, like a swamp of mire.
They are the chief root of suffering, sorrow, and fear,
And those of foolish mind indulge in them.
O king, since even as soon as I came forth
I conceived a distaste for desires,
How could I take back again what I have cast away,
Those terrifying foes of the Dharma?
The vast snowy mountain, the Meru of many peaks,
Might perhaps be shaken by the wind,
But my mind, which rests on liberation,
Cannot be shaken by the force of desire.
This world, sunk in desire,
Wanders round on the wheel of saṃsāra;
O lord of men, I am its refuge, since there is no other,
And I shall free it from its great fear.

Knowing the dangers of desires,
Seeing nirvāṇa as peace,
For the sake of abandoning them,
I shall go wherever my mind delights."

King Bimbisāra said, "O renunciant, what aim do you pursue by this discipline?"

He said, "Unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening."

The king said, "O renunciant, when you have fully and manifestly awakened to unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening, please think of me too."

The bodhisattva said, "I shall consider it so." Having said this, he left Rājagṛha and went to a hermitage of sages not very far from Vulture Peak Mountain. There he dwelt, applying himself to meditative concentration in his conduct and in his abiding. When those sages stood on one leg for a single watch of the day, the bodhisattva remained so for two watches. If they sat for a single watch of the day in the practice of the five fires, the bodhisattva too sat in that practice for two watches. Then, filled with wonder, they said, "This śramaṇa is a great one," and so he came to be called "the Great Śramaṇa, the Great Śramaṇa."

The bodhisattva said to them, "Sirs, what aim do you pursue by this discipline?"

Some of them said, "To be Śakra himself." Some said, "We seek to be Brahmā himself." Some said, "We seek to be Māra himself."

The bodhisattva thought, "These sages will fall once again; they have entered a wrong path. This path cannot bring knowing, cannot bring seeing, and cannot bring awakening to unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening."

Then the bodhisattva, knowing that path to be powerless, went to where Ārāḍa Kālāma was, and when he arrived he said to Ārāḍa Kālāma, "Ārāḍa, if it is no trouble to you, I would practice the holy life in your presence."

He said, "Venerable Gautama, it is no trouble to me; stay just as you please."

The bodhisattva said, "Ārāḍa, how many dharmas have you realized through your own direct knowledge?"

He replied, "Venerable Gautama, up to the sphere of nothingness."

Then the bodhisattva thought, "In what Ārāḍa Kālāma has faith, I too have faith. Just as Ārāḍa Kālāma has diligence, mindfulness, samādhi, and wisdom, I too have diligence, mindfulness, samādhi, and wisdom. Ārāḍa Kālāma has directly realized all those dharmas up to the sphere of nothingness; why should I too not directly realize these dharmas?" Then, in order to attain those very dharmas not yet attained, to understand what was not yet understood, and to realize directly what was not yet directly realized, the bodhisattva dwelt alone and secluded, heedful, ardent, and resolute. As he dwelt alone and secluded, heedful, ardent, and resolute, before long he directly realized those dharmas.

Having directly realized those dharmas, he went to where Ārāḍa Kālāma was, and when he arrived he said to Ārāḍa Kālāma, "Ārāḍa, did you directly realize these dharmas up to the sphere of nothingness through your own direct knowledge?"

He replied, "Indeed, Venerable Gautama, I directly realized those dharmas up to the sphere of nothingness."

The bodhisattva said, "Venerable Ārāḍa, I too have directly realized those dharmas up to the sphere of nothingness through my own direct knowledge."

"Venerable Gautama, in that case, as much as you have, I have as well, and as much as I have, you have as well. In this matter we are equal, the same, and alike. Come here, and the two of us together shall lead this assembly."

Then Ārāḍa Kālāma, who had at first been the bodhisattva's teacher, himself honored the bodhisattva with the highest honor and revered him with the highest reverence, and he was delighted and pleased with his realizations.

Then the bodhisattva thought, "This path cannot bring knowing, cannot bring seeing, and cannot bring awakening to unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening." Then the bodhisattva, understanding that path to be powerless, went to where Udraka Rāmaputra was, and when he arrived he said to Udraka Rāmaputra, "Udraka, if it is no trouble to you, I would practice the holy life in your presence."

He said, "Venerable Gautama, it is no trouble to me; stay just as you please."

"How many dharmas have you directly realized?"

"Venerable Gautama, up to the sphere of neither perception nor non-perception."

Then the bodhisattva thought, "In what Udraka Rāmaputra has faith, I too have faith. If I have such diligence, mindfulness, samādhi, and wisdom as Udraka Rāmaputra has, and Udraka Rāmaputra has directly realized all these dharmas up to the sphere of neither perception nor non-perception, why should I too not directly realize these dharmas?" Then, in order to attain those very dharmas not yet attained, to understand what was not yet understood, and to realize directly what was not yet directly realized, the bodhisattva dwelt alone and secluded, heedful, ardent, and resolute. As he dwelt alone and secluded, heedful and resolute, before long he directly realized those dharmas.

Having directly realized those dharmas, he went to where Udraka Rāmaputra was, and when he arrived he said to Udraka Rāmaputra, "Udraka, did you yourself directly realize these dharmas up to the sphere of neither perception nor non-perception?"

"Indeed, Venerable Gautama, I directly realized these dharmas up to the sphere of neither perception nor non-perception."

The bodhisattva said, "Venerable Udraka, I too have myself directly realized these dharmas up to the sphere of neither perception nor non-perception."

"Venerable Gautama, in that case, as much as you have, I have as well, and as much as I have, you have as well. In this matter we are equal, the same, and alike. Come here, and the two of us together shall lead this community."

Then Udraka Rāmaputra, who had at first been the bodhisattva's teacher, honored the bodhisattva with the highest honor and revered him with the highest reverence, and he was delighted and pleased with his understanding.

Then the bodhisattva thought, "This path cannot bring knowing, cannot bring seeing, and cannot bring awakening to unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening." Then the bodhisattva, understanding that this path too was powerless, departed. Then five sages, three from the following of Ārāḍa Kālāma and two from the following of Udraka Rāmaputra, followed after the bodhisattva.

Then the bodhisattva, together with the five, wandered on and came to the market town of the community of Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, south of the Ganges. As he walked about there, he saw a delightful place: a beautiful, dense grove; the river Nairañjanā flowing cool, with sandy banks and easy fords, broad on its shores; adorned with blue grasses and many kinds of trees, altogether delightful.

Seeing it, he thought, "Ah, this place is truly delightful: a beautiful, dense grove; the river Nairañjanā flowing cool, with sandy banks and easy fords, broad on its shores; adorned with blue grasses and many kinds of trees, altogether delightful. Ah, if a son of good family who seeks to strive resorts to this forest, he will be able to strive. Since I seek to strive, come now, I too shall resort to this forest and strive."

Then the bodhisattva entered the forest, sat down at the foot of a tree, set his teeth against his teeth, pressed his tongue to his palate, and with his mind he gripped his mind, restrained it, and restrained it utterly. As with his mind he gripped his mind, restrained it, and restrained it utterly, sweat broke out from all the pores of his body. Just as, when a strong man seizes a very weak man, binds him upside down, restrains him, and restrains him utterly, sweat breaks out from all that man's pores, so at that time the bodhisattva set his teeth against his teeth, pressed his tongue to his palate, and with his mind gripped his mind, restrained it, and restrained it utterly, and so on, as before.

Then the bodhisattva thought, "Come now, I shall enter into the meditations of swelling (āsphānaka)." He began to enter into the meditations of swelling, and as he entered into the meditations of swelling, he stopped the in-breath and the out-breath through his mouth and nose. When he had stopped the in-breath and out-breath through his mouth and nose, all the wind went up into the crown of his head, and in his head there arose an extremely unbearable pain in the head. Just as, when a strong man drives a sharp iron spike into the crown of a very weak man, an extremely unbearable pain in the head arises in that man's head, so, when the bodhisattva entered into the meditations of swelling, an extremely unbearable pain arose in his head. Yet he did not lose heart; his diligence remained aroused. His body was not untrained but trained. He was not dull but had mindfulness closely established; his mind was settled one-pointedly in equipoise. Although the bodhisattva experienced such feelings, fierce, unbearable, harsh, burning, and disagreeable suffering, because his body had been thus cultivated, they did not seize hold of his mind and remain.

He thought, "Come now, I shall enter still further into the meditations of swelling." He began to enter still further into the meditations of swelling, and as he entered into the meditations of swelling, he stopped the in-breath and out-breath through his mouth and nose. Because he had stopped the in-breath and out-breath through his mouth and nose, all the wind went out through the openings of his two ears. Just as, when a smith blows his bellows, the whole stream of air goes out through the two openings at its corners, so, when the bodhisattva entered into the meditations of swelling, the whole stream of wind went out through the openings of his two ears. Yet he did not lose heart; his diligence remained aroused, and so on, as before.

Then the bodhisattva thought, "Come now, I shall enter yet further into the meditations of swelling." He began to enter further into the meditations of swelling, and as he entered into the meditations of swelling, he stopped the in-breath and out-breath through his mouth and nose. Because he had stopped the in-breath and out-breath through his mouth and nose, his whole belly swelled up, swelled up utterly. Just as, when a skilled smith blows his bellows, it becomes full, utterly full, so at that time, when the bodhisattva entered into the meditations of swelling, his whole belly swelled up, swelled up utterly. Yet he did not lose heart; his diligence remained aroused, and so on, as before.

Then the bodhisattva thought, "Come now, I shall enter into the meditations of swelling." He began to enter further into the meditations of swelling, and as he entered into the meditations of swelling, he stopped the in-breath and out-breath through his mouth and nose. When he had stopped the in-breath and out-breath through his mouth and nose, at that time an extremely unbearable pain arose in his head. Just as, when a strong man binds the head of a very weak man again and again with a cord, an extremely unbearable pain in the head arises in that man's head, so at that time, when the bodhisattva entered into the meditations of swelling, an extremely unbearable pain arose in his head. Yet he did not lose heart; his diligence remained aroused, and so on, as before.

Then the bodhisattva thought, "Come now, I shall enter into the meditations of swelling." He began to enter further into the meditations of swelling, and as he entered into the meditations of swelling, he stopped the in-breath and out-breath through his mouth and nose. When he had stopped the in-breath and out-breath through his mouth and nose, at that time an unbearable pain arose within his belly. Just as, when a skilled butcher of cattle, or a butcher's skilled apprentice, slits open a cow's belly with a sharp cattle-killing knife, an extremely unbearable pain arises within its belly, so at that time, when the bodhisattva entered into the meditations of swelling, an extremely unbearable pain arose within his belly. Yet he did not lose heart; his diligence remained aroused, and so on, as before.

Then the bodhisattva thought, "Come now, I shall enter into the meditations of swelling."

He began to enter still further into the meditations of swelling, and as he entered into the meditations of swelling, he stopped the in-breath and out-breath through his mouth and nose. When he had stopped the in-breath and out-breath through his mouth and nose, at that time an extremely unbearable burning arose in his body. Just as, when two very strong men seize a very weak man, hold him upside down, and roast him in a fire pit, an extremely unbearable burning arises in that man's body, so at that time, when the bodhisattva entered into the meditations of swelling, an extremely unbearable burning arose in his body. Yet he did not lose heart; his diligence remained aroused, and so on, as before.

Then the bodhisattva thought, "Come now, I shall practice not eating food at all, in any way whatsoever." Then very many gods, knowing the bodhisattva's mind with their minds, went to where the bodhisattva was, and when they arrived they said to the bodhisattva, "Friend, if you shun human food, are ashamed of it, and despise it, we shall infuse divine radiance through all the pores of your body; please accept it."

Then the bodhisattva thought, "If, having vowed not to eat human food, I were to accept the divine radiance that the gods infuse into all my pores, that would be a falsehood on my part, and whatever is a falsehood on my part is a wrong on my part. On account of whatever is a wrong on my part, some beings here, at the breaking up of the body after death, would be reborn in the lower realms, the bad destinies, the places of ruin, the hells. Come now, I shall reject the gods' words entirely, in every way, and I shall eat only a little food, a mere handful, or drink broth of mung beans, or broth of peas, or broth of hareṇuka fruit."

Then at that time the bodhisattva rejected the gods' words entirely, in every way, and took little food, scant, meager, a mere handful, or else broth of mung beans, or broth of peas, or broth of hareṇuka fruit. Then, as the bodhisattva took little food, scant, meager, a mere handful, all his limbs and lesser limbs shrank and shrank completely, and he became withered, his bodily splendor diminished. Just as the stems of the asita creeper and of the kālika creeper shrink and shrink completely, so that they become thin and dry, so at that time, as the bodhisattva took little food, scant, meager, a mere handful, all his limbs and lesser limbs shrank and shrank completely, and he became withered, his bodily splendor diminished.

Then at that time, as the bodhisattva took little food, scant, meager, a mere handful, the skin of his head withered and withered completely, and became shriveled and wrinkled. Just as a gourd cut from its stalk withers and withers completely, becoming shriveled and wrinkled, so at that time, as the bodhisattva took little food, scant, meager, a mere handful, the skin of his head withered and withered completely, and became shriveled and wrinkled. Yet he did not lose heart; his diligence remained aroused, and so on, as before.

Then the bodhisattva thought, "As I take little food, scant, meager, a mere handful, the eyeballs of my two eyes have sunk in, sunk deep in, gone deep, very deep, far down, so that they look as if they had been plucked out." Just as one speaks of the reflection of the stars in the water of a deep well as sunk in, sunk deep in, gone deep, very deep, far down, so at that time, as the bodhisattva took little food, scant, meager, a mere handful, the eyeballs of his two eyes sank in, sank deep in, went deep, very deep, far down, so that they looked as if they had been plucked out. Yet he did not lose heart; his diligence remained aroused, and so on, as before.

Then, as the bodhisattva took little food, scant, meager, a mere handful, at that time the spaces between his ribs became ridged and hollowed. Just as the rafters of a grass hut two or three years old become uneven, so at that time, as the bodhisattva took little food, scant, meager, a mere handful, the spaces between his ribs became ridged and hollowed. Yet he did not lose heart; his diligence remained aroused, and so on, as before.

Then, as the bodhisattva took little food, scant, meager, a mere handful, the joints of his spine became like a braid of hair, and his buttocks became like a camel's foot. At times, when he wished to rise, he fell forward on his face, and at times, when he wished to sit, he fell over on his back. He supported his lower body by leaning on his upper body, and supported his upper body by leaning on his lower body. He rubbed his body with his two hands, and when he rubbed and rubbed his body all over with his two hands, the hairs, rotted at the roots, dropped to the ground.

Then the bodhisattva thought, "Come now, I shall take a meal of a single sesame seed," and he took a meal of a single sesame seed. As he took a meal of a single sesame seed, all his limbs and lesser limbs shrank and shrank completely, and he became withered, his bodily splendor diminished. As with a single sesame seed, so too with a single grain of rice, a single chickpea, a single pea, a single lentil, and a single mung bean; and he slept on a bed of grass.

When King Śuddhodana heard of that state of austerity, he sent out two hundred and fifty scouts every day, and King Suprabuddha too sent two hundred and fifty. Every day they carried news to those two kings with all possible speed.

"The bodhisattva is practicing hardships such as these: he takes a meal of a single sesame seed, a single grain of rice, a single chickpea, a single pea, a single lentil, and a single mung bean, and he sleeps on a bed of grass," they reported. When King Śuddhodana heard this, he was overwhelmed by grief for his son, pining for him all the more, his eyes brimming with tears; in his anguish he too lay down on a bed of grass, together with his retinue of queens. Yaśodharā also heard of it, and she was overwhelmed by grief for her husband. She ate only a single sesame seed, and the child within her womb, ill at ease, dwindled away right there.

Then the bodhisattva thought, "By this path, too, one cannot know, one cannot see, one cannot awaken to unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening."

Then that bodhisattva thought, "Come, let me eat extremely coarse food," and so he took extremely coarse food. The base food in this case was the urine and dung of calves that, not long born, were still drinking milk. As long as its sustaining radiance had not faded from his body, he would not take a second meal; only when it had faded did he take the next one. He also went to the great charnel ground, placed a corpse beneath his head as a pillow, set one foot upon the other, and, attending in mind to the perception of light and to a perception endowed with mindfulness and vigilance, lay down to sleep on his right side.

At that time the young men and the young women of the town would come and push sticks into the openings of his two ears and draw them out through both ears, saying, "Hey, look at the piśāca of the rubbish heap! Hey, look at the piśāca of the rubbish heap!" They would also scatter sweepings, donkey dung, and gravel over him. Yet the bodhisattva did not think to give rise to ill will toward those young men or young women of the town, or to utter a harsh word. Because the bodhisattva practiced hardship in great austerity, he came to be called "the Great Ascetic, the Great Ascetic." In this way he practiced austerities for six years.

Then three gods came to where the bodhisattva was, and one god said, "The ascetic Gautama is black." The second said, "He is not black; rather, he is dark blue." The third said, "He is neither black nor dark blue; he is dusky red." The fine complexion that had shone in the bodhisattva's body had vanished altogether.

Then, at that time, three similes never heard before came to the bodhisattva, namely these.

Suppose a piece of wet, sappy wood were taken out of the water and placed close by on dry ground, and then a man who wanted fire, seeking fire, came along, made it into a lower fire stick, set the upper fire stick upon it, and rubbed. Because that wood is wet and sappy, it would not be possible to produce fire or make it blaze. In the same way, there are certain ascetics or brahmins who do not live with body and mind held far away from sensual pleasures; for them, their desire for sensual pleasures, their attachment to sensual pleasures, their delight in sensual pleasures, their clinging to sensual pleasures, their infatuation with sensual pleasures, and their intense clinging to sensual pleasures have not been brought fully to an end in their minds. Such is the case of those who do not live with body and mind held far away. Although they undergo such feelings of self-inflicted suffering, unbearable, harsh, burning, and disagreeable, nevertheless they cannot know, they cannot see, they cannot awaken to unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening. This was the first simile, never heard before, that came to the bodhisattva at that time.

Suppose, for example, a piece of wet, sappy wood were taken out of the water and placed close by on dry ground, and then a man who wanted fire, seeking fire, came along, made it into a lower fire stick, set the upper fire stick upon it, and rubbed. Because that wood is wet and sappy, it would not be possible to produce fire or make it blaze. In the same way, there are certain ascetics or brahmins who live withdrawn from sensual pleasures in body but not in mind; for them, their desire for sensual pleasures, their attachment to sensual pleasures, their delight in sensual pleasures, their clinging to sensual pleasures, their infatuation with sensual pleasures, and their intense clinging to sensual pleasures remain, not brought fully to an end in their minds. Although they undergo such feelings of self-inflicted suffering, unbearable, harsh, burning, and disagreeable, nevertheless they cannot know, they cannot see, they cannot awaken to unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening. This was the second simile, never heard before, that came to the bodhisattva at that time.

Suppose a piece of dry, hollow wood without sap were placed on dry ground far from water, and then a man who wanted fire, seeking fire, came along, made it into a lower fire stick, set the upper fire stick upon it, and rubbed. Because that wood is dry and hollow, it would be possible to produce fire and make it blaze. In the same way, there are certain ascetics or brahmins who live with body and mind held far away from sensual pleasures; for them, their desire for sensual pleasures, their attachment to sensual pleasures, their delight in sensual pleasures, their clinging to sensual pleasures, their infatuation with sensual pleasures, and their intense clinging to sensual pleasures have been brought fully to an end in their minds and no longer remain. Although they undergo such feelings of self-inflicted suffering, unbearable, harsh, burning, and disagreeable, nevertheless they can know, they can see, they can awaken to unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening. This was the third simile, never heard before, that came to the bodhisattva at that time.

Then the bodhisattva thought, "All those who live striving to abandon suffering go no further than this. There is nothing beyond it, and nothing higher than it; and yet by this path, too, one cannot know, one cannot see, one cannot awaken to unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening."

Then the bodhisattva reflected, "What, then, is the path by which one can know, by which one can see, by which one can awaken to unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening?" And he thought this: "When I was living in the household of my father, the Śākya Śuddhodana, I went out to the edge of the fields where the plowing was being done and sat in the shade of a rose-apple tree. There, secluded from sensual desires, secluded from evil, unwholesome dharmas, I entered and dwelled in the first meditative concentration, with its joy and bliss born of seclusion, accompanied by thought and accompanied by examination." Recalling this, he thought, "That is the path; that is the practice. By it one can know, one can see, one can awaken to unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening. Yet it is not easy for me to bring that about while I am this emaciated, weak, and feeble. Come, let me breathe in at ease and breathe out at ease, let me eat coarse food of rice and warm porridge as I please, anoint my body with butter and sesame oil, and bathe my body with soft water." So he breathed in at ease and breathed out at ease, took coarse food of rice and warm porridge as he pleased, anointed his body with sesame oil and butter, and bathed his body with soft water.

Then those attendants of his thought, "Friends, this ascetic Gautama has grown lax in his restraint, given to abundance, and has fallen away from renunciation. Now he eats plenty of food, rice and warm porridge, anoints his body with clarified butter and sesame oil, and bathes his body with soft water. Now he will not be able to attain anything at all." Having thought this, they abandoned the bodhisattva and went away, and in stages they reached Vārāṇasī and stayed in the Deer Park at Ṛṣivadana.

Then the bodhisattva gradually regained his bodily strength and vigor, and his diligence, and in stages he came to the town of Senānī. He stayed on the bank of the Nairañjanā River near the town called Senānī. A brahmin named Deva, who had formerly been a friend of the bodhisattva, lived in the town of Senānī. Seeing the bodhisattva practicing hardships, he said to two village girls, Nandā and Nandabalā, "Have you heard the prophecy of the soothsayers that if the son of King Śuddhodana did not go forth, he would become a universal monarch?" The two of them said, "We too made the aspiration, 'If he becomes a universal monarch, may we become his queens.'" The brahmin Deva said, "When that great being, his body tormented by austerities, rises from his renunciation, you two must be the very first to offer him alms. That will bring you both benefit and happiness for a long time."

When the girls Nandā and Nandabalā heard this, they milked a thousand cows. Having milked them, they fed that milk to five hundred cows. Having milked the five hundred cows, they fed the milk to two hundred and fifty. Having milked the two hundred and fifty, they fed it to a hundred. Having milked the hundred, they fed it to fifty. Having milked the fifty, they fed it to twenty. Having milked the twenty, they kindled a fire, poured the milk into a vessel made of crystal, and began to boil a milk porridge. The brahmins thought, "Surely there is going to be a bride taken or a bride given away here," and many brahmins gathered. As the thousandfold-enriched milk porridge boiled, there appeared in it the signs of an auspicious vase, a śrīvatsa, and a swastika. A wandering ascetic called Upaga saw this and thought, "Whoever drinks this milk porridge will attain unsurpassed wisdom. Come, let me beg for it."

Then Śakra thought, "The bodhisattva's body has been weakened by austerities, and he is tormented by hunger and thirst. If the bodhisattva takes the milk porridge, he will realize unsurpassed wisdom. But these brahmins, greedy by nature, have gathered here, and they might do the bodhisattva harm." So he went up Mount Gandhamādana, took a divine medicine, and, emanating himself as a brahmin of great beauty, he sat over the boiling milk porridge, and Śakra, lord of the gods, poured into it the medicine called "Strength-Giving."

Then, having boiled the thousandfold-enriched milk porridge, sweet as honey, Nandā and Nandabalā said to Śakra, lord of the gods, "Are you the son of King Śuddhodana?" Śakra said, "He is my preceptor; I am his student." Then Śakra, lord of the gods, took the thousandfold-enriched milk porridge, sweet as honey. Having taken it, he said to the two of them, Nandā and Nandabalā, "What do you two seek by this gift? For what purpose should the merit of the offering be dedicated?" The two of them said:

"Whatever merit there may be in offering this savory food,
may all the aims of that renowned one, who has accomplished every aim,
who has become supreme among humans, endowed with supreme understanding,
be swiftly and perfectly accomplished!"

Then the bodhisattva entered the Nairañjanā River and bathed. He wished to cross, but because his body was so weak he was unable to cross. The gods bent down the branch of an arjuna tree, and holding on to it the bodhisattva crossed over. He dried his robes and sat on the bank of the Nairañjanā River.

Then Śakra, lord of the gods, knowing that the bodhisattva was seated at ease, poured the thousandfold-enriched milk porridge, sweet as honey, into a bowl and offered it to the bodhisattva. The bodhisattva ate it and dedicated the merit of the offering.

Then the bodhisattva searched along the bank of the Nairañjanā River, here and there, for a place to stay. At length he saw a great rocky mountain with flowers and all kinds of fruit. He climbed it, and in a secluded and clear spot he sat down with his legs crossed. No sooner had he sat down than that very mountain crumbled.

Then the bodhisattva thought, "What could be the cause that this mountain has crumbled? Are the gods displeased at my coming, or is some obstacle about to arise?" The gods, knowing the bodhisattva's thought, said to him, "Bodhisattva, there are two whom the whole earth cannot bear to hold up: one whose roots of virtue are severed, and one whose roots of virtue are fully refined. You are one whose roots of virtue are fully refined. This is not a place for bodhisattvas. Go on ahead instead; there lies the vajra seat. Seated there, all the bodhisattvas of the past, the future, and the present have attained and will attain unsurpassed wisdom."

Then, as the bodhisattva went along the way the gods had shown him, lotuses sprang up wherever he set his feet. Water came down from the four great oceans, and lotus ponds appeared. When he took a step and pressed down, this great earth began to ring out like a bronze vessel. Jays and wild animals circled around the bodhisattva. The young gods who send the winds sent the wind ever so gently, and the young gods who send the rain let fall a light rain.

Then the bodhisattva thought, "Since such auspicious signs as these have appeared for me, this very day I shall attain unsurpassed wisdom."

On the bank of the Nairañjanā River there lived a blind nāga king called Kālika. A dwelling there had come to him, born of the ripening of his karma: when a buddha appears, he gains his sight, and when that buddha passes into parinirvāṇa, he becomes blind once more. At the sound of the earth ringing out, he came out of his dwelling, and from far away he saw the bodhisattva, his body entirely adorned with the thirty-two marks of a great being and embellished with the eighty excellent features, adorned with a halo a fathom wide, with rays of light surpassing a thousand suns, altogether beautiful like a mountain of jewels in motion. As soon as he saw him, he began to praise the bodhisattva in a chant of verses:

"Hearing the sound of the earth,
all filled with joyful song,
he came out from the nāga dwelling
and looked around in every direction.

The chief of those that move upon their breasts,
the nāga king of great power,
saw on the bank of the Nairañjanā
the guide of the world approaching.

By the power of a buddha's appearing,
the nāga gained his sight,
and with his mind joyful and heart delighted,
he spoke these words:

'When I see you, O Sage,
there is no difference to tell
between you and those buddhas of great power
whom I have seen before.

From the way, O Sage,
you first lift your right foot,
today you will become a buddha
and the field of offerings for the world.

From the way you dried your robes
and the way you entered
the cool water of the Nairañjanā,
today you will become a buddha.

Since you go with a steady gait,
moving majestically like a lordly bull,
O bull among men, supreme among the upholders of the Dharma,
today, here, you will become a supreme buddha.

Since hundreds of jays in the sky
soar about and circle
your vast, vast body,
today, O best of men, you will become a supreme buddha.

Since a fragrant breeze arises,
the herds of deer and flocks of birds are unafraid,
and the trees are not shaken by the wind,
today, O best of men, you will become a supreme buddha.

Since, venerable one, the light of your face,
like the blazing light of stainless gold,
is radiantly beautiful and full of color,
today, O best of men, you will become a supreme buddha.'"

Then the bodhisattva, having been fully praised by the nāga king Kālika, went toward the vajra seat. Then the bodhisattva thought, "I shall sit on a mat of grass." When he had need of grass, Śakra, lord of the gods, knowing in his mind the bodhisattva's intention, took from Mount Gandhamādana a load of grass as pleasant to the touch as cotton down, emanated himself in the guise of the grass seller Svastika, and stood before the bodhisattva. The bodhisattva said, "Good sir, give me grass." Śakra, lord of the gods, touched the bodhisattva's two feet and offered it with reverence.

The bodhisattva accepted the grass from the grass seller Svastika, and with a bearing that captivated the minds of gods and humans, he inspired faith in beings. Along the path the gods had shown, he went to where the seat of awakening was. Taking his place at the vajra seat, which is like the hub of the wheel of the great trichiliocosm world system, he resolutely spread the grass himself, unruffled and neither too thick nor too thin. Then he made a resolve in his mind and spoke it aloud as well:

"Until I have attained the exhaustion of defilements,
the end of all suffering,
even though my life should come to an end,
I shall not break this cross-legged posture."

Having said this, the bodhisattva mounted the vajra seat, sat down cross-legged like the coiled body of a sleeping serpent king, held his body straight and upright, set mindfulness before him, and remained there. The very moment he sat down, the abode of Māra, the Evil One, was shaken.

Then Māra, the Evil One, thought, "Why has my abode been shaken?" At length he saw the bodhisattva seated on the vajra seat. Then Māra, the Evil One, thought, "The son of the Śākya Śuddhodana is sitting on the vajra seat. As long as he has not passed beyond my domain, I shall make obstacles for him." Having thought this, he emanated himself in the form of a messenger carrying a letter, his hair standing on end and wearing a lower garment of hemp, and went to where the bodhisattva was. Arriving, he said in haste, "The city of Kapilavastu has been overrun by Devadatta, the palace has been destroyed, and the Śākyas have been subjugated. Why are you sitting here, settled in this place?"

Then three evil, unwholesome thoughts arose in the bodhisattva, namely: the thought of desire, the thought of ill will, and the thought of harming. The thought of desire was toward Yaśodharā, Mṛgajā, and Gopikā. The thought of ill will was toward Devadatta. The thought of harming was toward the Śākyas who lived as his followers. Then the bodhisattva reflected, "Why have three evil, unwholesome thoughts arisen in me?" At length he saw that Māra, the Evil One, had come near in order to stir up trouble. Then three wholesome thoughts arose in the bodhisattva, namely: the thought of renunciation, the thought free of ill will, and the thought free of harming.

Again Māra said, "Why are you sitting at the seat of awakening?" The bodhisattva said, "In order to attain unsurpassed wisdom." Māra said, "How could you attain unsurpassed wisdom?" The bodhisattva said, "Evil One, if you, by performing just one sacrifice, became the lord of the desire realm in all his splendor, then what need is there to say that I, who for three incalculable eons have performed many hundreds of thousands of koṭis of niyutas of sacrifices, and for the sake of beings have given away my head, hands, feet, eyes, flesh, blood, sons, wife, wealth, gold, and so on, shall attain unsurpassed wisdom? Your hope that I shall not attain unsurpassed wisdom is vain."

When he had said this, Māra said, "As for how I attained lordship of the desire realm through my one sacrifice, you are my witness. But who is your witness that, in order to attain unsurpassed wisdom, you performed many hundreds of thousands of koṭis of niyutas of sacrifices over three incalculable eons, and gave away your head, hands, feet, eyes, flesh, blood, sons, wife, wealth, gold, and so on, for the sake of beings?"

Then the bodhisattva touched the earth with his hand, the hand of a Blessed One, marked with the wheel, the auspicious emblem, and the swastika, joined by a web, produced by many hundreds of merits, and bringing relief to the frightened. He said, "This earth is my witness that I performed many hundreds of thousands of koṭis of niyutas of sacrifices over three incalculable eons, and gave away my head, hands, feet, eyes, flesh, blood, sons, wife, wealth, gold, and so on, for the sake of beings." Then the goddess of the earth broke up through the ground, joined her palms, and cried out, "Evil One, it is just as the Blessed One has said." When she had said this, Māra, the Evil One, fell silent; he became dejected, became abashed, hung his head, lost his confidence, and sat there brooding.

Then Māra, the Evil One, thought, "Though I did that to the son of Śuddhodana, I found no opening. Come, let me make one more obstacle for him." So, together with thirty-six koṭis of troops with mighty forces, resembling elephants, horses, camels, goats, deer, buffalo, pigs, dogs, owls, mongooses, monkeys, foxes, and the like, terrifying, brandishing weapons such as swords, great arrows, spikes, discs, short spears, bows, nooses, axes, and iron clubs, he completely surrounded the bodhisattva and took up position, and Māra, the Evil One, himself stood there with his bow drawn back to his ear.

Then the bodhisattva saw them, of so many forms and equipped with so many kinds of weapons, and this thought arose in him: "Even when one begins to fight with an ordinary person, one applies oneself to the remedy against him; what need then to speak of this lord of the desire realm? Come, let me apply myself to the remedy." Having thought this, he began to consider the means by which they might be pacified.
