Māra, together with thirty-six koṭis of bhūtas, began to rain down weapons upon the bodhisattva from above: discs, short spears, great arrows, spikes, arrows, and the like. Then the bodhisattva emanated a celestial palace made of crystal, and all the weapons fell upon that celestial palace. At the sound of the celestial palace, the whole army of Māra was terrified and scattered in every direction.

Then, in the first watch of the night, the bodhisattva remained endowed with constant practice and wakefulness, diligently pursuing the yoga of meditating on the dharmas that accord with awakening. In the first watch of the night, when he set his mind on the higher knowledge that realizes the domain of miraculous powers, he experienced the many kinds of miraculous powers: being one, he became many, and being many, he became one; he appeared and disappeared; all this he experienced through the vision of wisdom. With his body he passed straight through walls, mountains, and fences, as a bird does through the sky. He rose up out of the earth and plunged into it, as a bird does in water. He went upon the flowing water without sinking, as a bird does upon the ground.

He traveled through the sky in the cross-legged posture, like a winged bird. Even the sun and moon, so mighty in miraculous power and so great in splendor, he touched and stroked with his hand, and with his body he exercised mastery as far as the world of Brahmā.

Then Māra, the Evil One, thought, "Sound is the thorn of meditative concentration. Come, let me stay far off and raise a din." Having thought this, he, together with thirty-six koṭis of bhūtas, raised a terrifying sound of great dread. To block it out, the bodhisattva emanated a grove of kadamba trees twelve leagues across, and so the sound was stifled and did not resound.

Then the bodhisattva thought, "Come, let me set my mind on the higher knowledge that realizes the knowledge of the divine ear, and so listen to whatever sounds of gods and humans I wish to hear." Having thought this, when he set his mind on the higher knowledge that realizes the knowledge of the divine ear, with his divine ear, purified and surpassing that of humans, he heard both kinds of sound, those of humans and those of nonhumans, both those far away and those nearby.

Then the bodhisattva thought, "Of the thirty-six koṭis of bhūtas in Māra's retinue, how am I to know which of them intend me harm and which do not?" Then that bodhisattva thought, "It is through the higher knowledge that realizes the knowledge of the varieties of mind." Having thought this, in the middle watch of the night, when he set his mind on the higher knowledge that realizes the knowledge of the varieties of mind, with his own mind he fully knew, just as they were, the minds of other beings and other persons, with their thinking and examining. A mind with desire he fully knew, just as it was, as "a mind with desire." A mind free of desire he fully knew, just as it was, as "a mind free of desire." Minds with aversion and free of aversion, with delusion and free of delusion, collected and scattered, sluggish and exerted, agitated and not agitated, not calmed and calmed, not composed and composed, undeveloped and developed, minds not liberated and liberated: these he fully knew just as they were. A mind thoroughly liberated he fully knew, truly and just as it was, as "a thoroughly liberated mind."

Then the bodhisattva thought, "Among these servants of Māra, how am I to know which of them were related to me as mother or as father in other lives, and which were my executioners, my adversaries, or my enemies?" Then the bodhisattva thought, "It is through the higher knowledge that realizes the knowledge of recollecting former existences." Having thought this, in the middle watch of the night, when he set his mind on the higher knowledge that realizes the knowledge of recollecting former existences, he recollected many kinds of former existence, namely: one birth, two, three, four, five, six, seven, eight, nine, ten, twenty, thirty, forty, fifty births, a hundred births, a thousand births, a hundred thousand births, many hundreds of births, many thousands of births, many hundreds of thousands of births; an eon of dissolution, an eon of formation, an eon of dissolution and formation, many eons of dissolution, many eons of formation, many eons of dissolution and formation. He recollected: "When I formerly lived among these beings, my name was such, my caste was such, my clan was such. I ate such food. I experienced such pleasure and pain. I lived for so long a life. I remained for such a length of time. I lived out a life of such a span. Then I died, passed on, and was born in such and such a place. From there too I died, passed on, and was born in such and such a place. From there too I died, passed on, and was born here." In this way he recollected many kinds of former existence, with their details and their locations.

Then the bodhisattva thought, "Among these servants of Māra, how am I to know which of them will go to the lower realms and which will not go to the lower realms?" Then the bodhisattva thought, "It is through the higher knowledge that realizes the knowledge of death, transmigration, and birth." Having thought this, in the last watch of the night, when he set his mind on the higher knowledge that realizes the knowledge of death, transmigration, and birth, he looked with his divine eye, purified and surpassing that of humans, and he fully knew, just as it is, the death, transmigration, and birth of beings, beings of good complexion and of bad complexion, lowly and excellent, going to the happy destinies and to the unhappy destinies, faring just as they actually fare. He fully knew: "Friends, these beings are possessed of misconduct of body, of speech, and of mind. They disparage the noble ones and hold wrong views. Because of having taken up the dharmas and actions of wrong view, by that cause and that condition, when the body breaks up, after death they will be born in the lower realms, the unhappy destinies, fallen into ruin, among the beings of the hells. Friends, these beings are possessed of good conduct of body, of speech, and of mind. They do not disparage the noble ones and hold right view. Because of having taken up the dharmas and actions of right view, by that cause and that condition, when the body breaks up, after death they will be born in the happy destinies, in the heavenly world, among gods and humans."

Then the bodhisattva, seated at the foot of the tree of awakening, constantly applying himself with diligence and vigilance, remained in a yoga of meditation in accord with the dharmas that are factors of awakening. In the last watch of that night, when he directed his mind toward the knowledge that brings about direct realization of the knowledge of the exhaustion of the defilements, he fully understood, just as it is: "This is the noble truth of suffering." He fully understood, just as it is: "This is the origin of suffering. This is the cessation of suffering. This is the noble truth of the path leading to the cessation of suffering." As he knew thus and saw thus, his mind was liberated from the defilement of desire, and his mind was liberated from the defilement of existence and the defilement of ignorance. Liberated, he realized: "My birth is exhausted. I have lived the holy life. What was to be done has been done. I know no further existence beyond this." And right there he realized: "I have fully awakened into unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening." Thus in the last watch of the night the Blessed One, by the weapon of loving-kindness, was victorious over Māra together with his thirty-six koṭis of bhūtas, and attained unsurpassed wisdom.

Then the bow slipped from the hand of Māra the Evil One, his banner turned its tip downward, and his abode was shaken. Then Māra the Evil One, together with his retinue of thirty-six koṭis of bhūtas, suffering, unhappy and distressed at heart, vanished from that very spot and went to his own abode. Suffering, unhappy and defeated, he sank into brooding, and remained there scratching the ground with an arrow.

Then the daughters of Māra, named Tṛṣṇā, Delight-Maker and Delight-Enjoyer, went to where Māra was, and having arrived, spoke to Māra the Evil One as follows:

"Father, why are you not pleased?
Why do you sit like this?
What man is it who harms you,
That you keep brooding on him?
Tell us, and with the noose
Of desire, like an elephant from the forest,
We shall bind him and bring him here:
He will fall into your power."

Māra said:

"That man is utterly free of desire;
He cannot be lured by attachment.
He has passed beyond the domain of Māra:
That is why I grieve so deeply."

Then Tṛṣṇā, Delight-Maker and Delight-Enjoyer, the three daughters of Māra, blazing with splendor like lightning issuing from a cloud, went from their own abode toward where the Blessed One was. Having arrived, they bowed their heads at the Blessed One's feet and sat down to one side. Seated to one side, the three daughters of Māra the Evil One addressed the Blessed One as follows: "O ascetic, we shall honor your feet; Sugata, we shall serve you." But the Blessed One, having attained the unsurpassed state in which craving is completely exhausted and liberated, and the mind is completely and perfectly liberated, paid no attention to them.

A second and a third time the three daughters of Māra addressed the Blessed One as follows: "O ascetic, we shall honor your feet; Sugata, we shall serve you." A second and a third time, the Blessed One, having attained the unsurpassed state in which craving is completely exhausted and liberated, and the mind is completely and perfectly liberated, paid no attention to them.

Then the three daughters of Māra went off to one side, and talking among themselves, they consulted together: "Wise sisters, since men's craving for women differs from one to another, let us conjure ourselves up as hundreds of young women of every complexion, with garments in hundreds of colors; let us conjure ourselves up as women who have never given birth, who have given birth once, twice, three times and four times; let us conjure ourselves up in hundreds of forms of women of middle age, and approach the ascetic Gautama. Having arrived, let us say to the ascetic Gautama: 'O ascetic, we shall honor your feet; Sugata, we shall serve you.'"

Then the three daughters of Māra conjured themselves up as many hundreds of thousands of young women of every complexion, with garments in hundreds of colors, and so on up to hundreds of forms of middle-aged women, and went to where the Blessed One was. Having arrived, they bowed their heads at the Blessed One's feet and sat down to one side. Seated to one side, they addressed the Blessed One as follows: "O ascetic, we shall honor your feet; Sugata, we shall serve you." But the Blessed One, having attained the unsurpassed state in which craving is completely exhausted and liberated, and the mind is completely and perfectly liberated, paid no attention to them. Though the three daughters of Māra addressed the Blessed One thus a second and a third time, a second and a third time the Blessed One paid no attention to them.

Then the three daughters of Māra went off to one side and said to one another: "Sisters, if men who are not free of desire are approached and attended in so many ways as these, it can happen that their minds are thrown into turmoil, or their hearts burst, or they vomit hot blood from their mouths. Yet this ascetic Gautama pays us no attention. But since men's craving for women differs from one to another, let us conjure ourselves up in hundreds of forms of old women and go before the ascetic Gautama. If we do that, it may be that the ascetic Gautama will develop desire for us." Having said this, the three daughters of Māra conjured themselves up in hundreds of forms of old women and went to where the Blessed One was. They addressed him as before, up to "we shall serve you," but the Blessed One, having attained the unsurpassed state in which craving is completely exhausted and liberated, and the mind is completely and perfectly liberated, paid no attention to them.

Then the Blessed One thought: "These women have made themselves into old women of their own accord. So I too, in order to make them thoroughly disenchanted, shall bless them to remain in this very old age." And the Blessed One blessed the daughters of Māra to remain old women.

Then the three daughters of Māra wished to make that old age disappear, but they were unable to make it disappear. Then the three daughters of Māra, their purpose unfulfilled and as if ashamed of their forms, aged, decrepit and worn out, hunched and bent like a crooked rafter, leaning forward on staffs, their bodies trembling, went to where Māra the Evil One was, and having arrived, spoke to Māra the Evil One as follows: "Father, what you told us is true indeed:

'That man is utterly free of desire;
He cannot be lured by attachment.
He has passed beyond the domain of Māra:
That is why I grieve so deeply.'

There is no means of clouding another's mind
That we did not employ,
And even so he did not fall under our power.
At last, when we took forms like these
And went up close to him,
He blessed us
To remain in old age just as we are.
Therefore, Father, please make
This old age of ours disappear."

Māra said:

"Proud of your forms,
Like lightning from a mass of clouds,
Blazing with splendor,
You went, puffed up with eager pride.
When you reached that great hero,
You were scattered like cotton by the wind.
Whoever would do harm here
To the Buddha, who has put an end to all attachment,
Wants to dig a mountain with fingernails,
To chew iron with teeth,
To topple a mountain
With the stalk of a young lotus.
Whoever wishes to do harm here
To the Buddha, who has put an end to all attachment,
Wants to dig out the ocean
And search for its deepest floor.
Whoever wishes to do harm here
To the Buddha, who has put an end to all attachment,
Wants to bind the wind with a noose,
To bring the moon down from the sky,
To scoop out the ocean with his hands,
To leap across the snowy mountains,
And to embrace a fire
Blazing bright and burning.
Whoever wishes to do the impossible,
This is how it will turn out.
Now then, go for refuge
To that very Sage,
And this form of old age
Will be turned to something else.
Since he has great compassion,
He will surely remove it for you.

In the world with its gods, its māras and its Brahmās, among beings with their ascetics and brahmins, in the world with its gods, humans and asuras, I see no one other than that very Blessed One, the Tathāgata, the arhat, the perfectly and completely awakened Buddha, who could make your state of old age disappear. Go, and win the favor of that Blessed One."

Then the three daughters of Māra went toward where the Blessed One was. Having arrived, they bowed their heads at the Blessed One's feet and sat down to one side. Seated to one side, the three daughters of Māra addressed the Blessed One as follows: "Venerable Blessed One, like children, like fools, like the undiscerning, like those unskilled in means, we failed to please the Tathāgata, the arhat, the perfectly and completely awakened Buddha, as he ought to be pleased. That was our transgression. Blessed One, please accept our confession of this transgression. Sugata, please forgive us. Blessed One, please make our state of old age disappear. Sugata, please make our state of old age disappear." Then the Blessed One made their state of old age disappear. Then the three daughters of Māra, having regained their own forms, bowed their heads at the Blessed One's feet, gained faith in the Blessed One, and vanished from that very spot.

Then King Śuddhodana's own men announced to him: "Your Majesty, rejoice! The prince has attained unsurpassed wisdom." When he heard that, on that very same day Yaśodharā also gave birth to a boy, and to Amṛtodana too a boy was born. On that same day the moon was seized by Rāhu. Then King Śuddhodana, seeing such good fortune, was filled with joy, delight, gladness, great elation and happiness of mind.

Then the city of Kapilavastu was cleared of stones, gravel and potsherds, sprinkled with sandalwood water, and set about with the most fragrant incense. Many silken streamers were hung, banners and flags were raised, and all kinds of flowers were strewn as pleasingly as could be. At the four gates of the city and at the crossroads, almshouses were built. Then at the eastern gate of the city gifts were given to ascetics, brahmins, wandering mendicants (carakas and parivrājakas), the wretched, the friendless, the poor, the destitute and beggars. At the crossroads of the south, the west, the north and the center too, gifts were given and merit was made.

When it came to naming Yaśodharā's son, the people of the queen's retinue said: "When he was born, the moon was seized by Rāhu, so the boy shall be named Rāhula." And so he was named.

Amṛtodana likewise gave gifts and made merit, and then, asking, "What shall the boy be named?", set about naming his son. His relatives said: "When he was born, the whole city rejoiced, so the boy shall be named Ānanda." And so he was named.

King Śuddhodana said: "This son of Yaśodharā is not the son of Śākyamuni." When Yaśodharā heard this, she was cast into despair.

Then she placed him on a stone the size of the bodhisattva's lap, sat down at the edge of the pleasure pond, and made a declaration of truth: "If this child was born of the bodhisattva, then let him float on this pond together with the stone. If not, let him sink." Having said this, she cast him into the water, and like a tuft of cotton he floated together with the stone.

King Śuddhodana, hearing of this marvel, went to the edge of the bodhisattva's pleasure pond surrounded by a great crowd of people, and there he saw the good Rāhula riding on the stone the size of the bodhisattva's lap, floating on the water like a tuft of cotton. Seeing this, he was filled with joy, delight, gladness, great elation and happiness of mind. At once he entered the pond, took the young prince, the good Rāhula, up onto his lap, and stood up.

When the Blessed One, by the weapon of loving-kindness, had subdued Māra's retinue of thirty-six koṭis of bhūtas and attained unsurpassed wisdom, at that moment this great earth shook violently. This whole world too was lit up by a great radiance. Even the spaces between the worlds, in pitch-black darkness, where the light of the sun and moon, so magically potent and so mighty, is never felt, were at that time lit up by a great radiance. The beings who had been born there, seeing one another by that light, recognized one another, saying, "Friends, look, other beings have been born here too! Friends, look, other beings have been born here too!"

The Buddha, the Blessed One, entered the absorption of the fire element and spent seven days in a single cross-legged posture. Then two gods of the Brahmakāyika class, dwelling in the Brahmā world, thought: "The Buddha, the Blessed One, is dwelling in Uruvilvā on the bank of the Nairañjanā River at the foot of the tree of awakening. Not long after his perfect and complete awakening, he has entered the absorption of the fire element and is spending seven days in a single cross-legged posture, and no one has offered him alms. Let us go there and praise him with a verse each."

Then, in the time it takes a strong man to stretch out his bent arm or bend his stretched arm, the two Brahmakāyika gods vanished from the Brahmakāyika world, came before the Blessed One, and sat down to one side. One of the two Brahmakāyika gods addressed him in verse:

"Caravan leader, victor in battle,
Free of evil, arise, seer of the world!
Here are those who seek the precious Dharma:
Sugata, please teach the supreme Dharma."

The other also addressed him in verse:

"Destroyer of pride, victor in battle,
Free of evil, arise, seer of the world!
Like the full moon on the fifteenth night,
Your mind is utterly pure."

Having said this, the two Brahmakāyika gods vanished from that very spot. Then the Blessed One arose from that samādhi, and at that time he spoke these verses:

"Whatever pleasure of the senses in the world,
And whatever bliss arises in the realm of the gods,
They are not worth even a sixteenth part
Of the bliss of craving's exhaustion.
Carrying a burden is suffering;
Laying the burden down is happiness.
Having laid down the heavy burden,
Take up no other.
Having abandoned all craving
And exhausted all conditioned things,
When one fully knows all things,
One does not come into existence again."

While the Buddha, the Blessed One, experiencing in his body the joy and bliss of liberation, spent seven days in a single cross-legged posture, no one offered him alms. At that time the two merchants Trapuṣa and Bhallika, traveling with some five hundred wagons, set out on that very road. Then a god who had once been a friend, minister, relative and companion of the merchants Trapuṣa and Bhallika thought: "The Buddha, the Blessed One, is dwelling in Uruvilvā on the bank of the Nairañjanā River at the foot of the tree of awakening. Not long after his perfect and complete awakening, experiencing in his body the joy and bliss of liberation, he has spent seven days in a single cross-legged posture, and no one has offered him alms. If I lead the two merchants Trapuṣa and Bhallika to be the first to offer him alms there, it will be for their long-lasting good, benefit and happiness. So I myself shall strive to have them offer alms to the Blessed One."

So that god lit up the wagons and all the merchants with a great radiance and spoke to the merchants Trapuṣa and Bhallika as follows: "Merchants, merchants, this Buddha, the Blessed One, is dwelling in Uruvilvā on the bank of the Nairañjanā River at the foot of the tree of awakening. Not long after his perfect and complete awakening, experiencing in his body the joy and bliss of liberation, he has spent seven days in a single cross-legged posture, and no one has offered him alms. If you are the first to offer him alms there, it will be for your long-lasting good, benefit and happiness." Having said this, the god vanished from that very spot.

Then the two merchants Trapuṣa and Bhallika thought: "The Buddha is surely supreme, and his teaching of the Dharma is surely supreme too, since even a god strives in this way to have alms offered to that Blessed One." So they rose early in the morning, took plenty of honey and barley meal, and went to where the Blessed One was. Having arrived, they bowed their heads at both the Blessed One's feet and sat down to one side. Seated to one side, the merchants Trapuṣa and Bhallika addressed the Blessed One as follows:

"Venerable One, we have brought plenty of honey and barley meal here for the Blessed One. Out of compassion, Blessed One, please accept it."

Then the Blessed One thought: "If I accept alms in my hands, I will be like the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas). That is not fitting for me. Let me consider in what the perfectly and completely awakened buddhas of the past accepted alms for the benefit of living beings." As he thought this, gods said to the Blessed One: "Venerable One, the perfectly and completely awakened buddhas of the past accepted alms in a bowl in order to benefit living beings." When the Blessed One too reflected and brought forth the vision of wisdom, he saw that the buddhas, the blessed ones of the past, had accepted alms in a bowl for the benefit of living beings, and so the thought of a bowl arose in the Blessed One.

Then the Four Great Kings, knowing that the thought of a bowl had arisen in the Blessed One, took from a certain rocky mountain four bowls made of stone, made by nonhumans and kept by nonhumans, clear, clean and free of any foul smell, and went to where the Blessed One was. Having arrived, they bowed their heads at both the Blessed One's feet and sat down to one side. Seated to one side, the Four Great Kings addressed the Blessed One as follows: "Venerable One, knowing that the Blessed One has need of a bowl, we have brought here four stone bowls from a certain rocky mountain, made of stone by nonhumans and kept by nonhumans, clear, clean and free of any foul smell. Out of compassion, Blessed One, please accept them."

Then the Blessed One thought: "If I accept one bowl from one Great King, three will be displeased. If I accept from two, two will be displeased. If I accept from three, one will be displeased. So let me accept four bowls from the Four Great Kings and bless them into a single bowl." Then the Blessed One accepted the four bowls from the Four Great Kings and blessed them into a single bowl.

Then, for the benefit of living beings, the Blessed One accepted alms from the merchants Trapuṣa and Bhallika. Then the Blessed One spoke to the merchants Trapuṣa and Bhallika as follows: "Merchants, come, go for refuge to the Buddha, go for refuge to the Dharma, and go for refuge also to what will arise in the future, called the Saṅgha."

"Venerable One, we go for refuge to the Buddha. We go for refuge to the Dharma. We go for refuge also to the Saṅgha that will arise in the future."

Then the Blessed One rejoiced in the gift of the merchants Trapuṣa and Bhallika:

"For whatever purpose a gift is given,
It serves that very purpose:
If a gift is given for the sake of happiness,
It turns into happiness.
The ripening of merit is happiness,
And one's aspirations too are fulfilled;
Swiftly one realizes nirvāṇa,
The supreme peace.
As for harm arising from others,
The hosts of gods and māras
Cannot obstruct
One who does nothing but merit.
If one who is generous
Strives for the sake of noble wisdom,
He will attain what is called the clear seeing
That puts an end to suffering."

Then the merchants Trapuṣa and Bhallika, delighting and rejoicing in what the Blessed One had said, bowed their heads at both the Blessed One's feet and departed from the Blessed One's presence.

Then the Blessed One, having accepted the alms from the merchants Trapuṣa and Bhallika, went to where the Nairañjanā River was. Having arrived, he sat on the bank of the Nairañjanā River and took his meal. The honey stirred up wind, and the barley meal too stirred up wind, and so the Blessed One fell ill with a wind disorder.

Then Māra the Evil One, knowing that the Blessed One had fallen ill with a wind disorder, went to where the Blessed One was. Having arrived, he addressed the Blessed One as follows: "Blessed One, the time has come for you to pass into parinirvāṇa. Sugata, the time has come for you to pass into parinirvāṇa."

Then the Blessed One thought: "This Māra the Evil One has come here to make trouble." Having thought this, he spoke to Māra the Evil One as follows: "Evil One, as long as there have not arisen monks, nuns, laymen and laywomen who are my hearers, learned, clear-minded and steady, able to refute in accord with the Dharma the arguments of opponents whenever they arise, and able to make their own teaching clear, and as long as my holy life has not been taught widely, truly and well, extensively, to many people, among gods and humans, I shall not pass into parinirvāṇa."

Then Māra the Evil One thought, "Alas! The ascetic Gautama will not pass into parinirvāṇa." Thinking this, suffering, unhappy and full of regret, he vanished from that very spot.

Then Śakra, lord of the gods, knowing that the Blessed One had fallen ill with a wind disorder, went to a great grove of myrobalan trees not very far from the tree called jambu, after which the continent of Jambudvīpa is named. Taking from there myrobalan fruit endowed with fine color, fragrance and taste, he went to where the Blessed One was. Having arrived, he bowed his head at both the Blessed One's feet and sat down to one side. Seated to one side, Śakra, lord of the gods, addressed the Blessed One as follows:

"Venerable One, knowing that the Blessed One has fallen ill with a chill, I went to a great grove of myrobalan trees not far from the tree called jambu, after which the continent of Jambudvīpa is named, and from there I have brought myrobalan fruit endowed with fine color, fragrance and taste. Blessed One, please eat it. When the Blessed One eats it, it will counter the chill, and the chill will be relieved."

When the Blessed One ate it, it countered the wind, and the wind disorder was relieved. Then the Blessed One became quite at ease and thrived. Then the Blessed One, having stayed at the foot of the tree of awakening for as long as he pleased, went to the abode of Mucilinda, the nāga king. Having arrived, he took his seat at the foot of a tree, crossed his legs, held his body upright, set his mindfulness before him, and remained there.

At that time unseasonable clouds gathered over the abode of Mucilinda, the nāga king, for seven days. Then Mucilinda, the nāga king, knowing that unseasonable clouds had gathered for seven days, rose up out of his abode, thinking, "During these seven days the Blessed One might grow cold, and heat, flies, mosquitoes, wind, sun and the touch of snakes and scorpions might harm his body." So he wrapped his body seven times around the body of the Blessed One and stayed there, spreading the great hood of his head above him.

Then Mucilinda, the nāga king, knowing that the unseasonable clouds of the seven days had gone, unwound his body from the body of the Blessed One. Anointed with unguents and adorned with armlets, earrings, garlands and all kinds of ornaments, he bowed with palms joined toward where the Blessed One was and addressed the Blessed One as follows: "Blessed One, during these seven days did you not grow cold? Did heat, flies, mosquitoes, chill, sun, or the harm of snakes and scorpions not touch your body?"

Then the Blessed One at that time spoke these verses:

"The joy of truth is happiness,
And so is looking to hearing the Dharma.
With restraint toward living beings,
Being free of harm in the world is happiness.
Being free of desire for the world
And going beyond sense pleasures is happiness.
Whoever has subdued the pride of 'I am':
That is the supreme happiness."

Then the Blessed One, having stayed in the abode of Mucilinda, the nāga king, for as long as he wished, went to the seat of awakening. Having arrived, he sat on the grass mat that had been spread there, crossed his legs, held his body upright, set his mindfulness before him, and spent seven days in a single cross-legged posture, contemplating dependent origination with its twelve links in forward and reverse order, as follows:

When this exists, that exists; from the arising of this, that arises. That is to say: conditioned by ignorance are formations; conditioned by formations is consciousness; conditioned by consciousness are name and form; conditioned by name and form are the six sense bases; conditioned by the six sense bases is contact; conditioned by contact is feeling; conditioned by feeling is craving; conditioned by craving is grasping; conditioned by grasping is becoming; conditioned by becoming is birth; conditioned by birth arise aging and death, sorrow, lamentation, suffering, unhappiness and distress. Thus does this whole great mass of suffering arise.

When this does not exist, that does not exist; from the cessation of this, that ceases. That is to say: from the cessation of ignorance, formations cease; from the cessation of formations, consciousness ceases; from the cessation of consciousness, name and form cease; from the cessation of name and form, the six sense bases cease; from the cessation of the six sense bases, contact ceases; from the cessation of contact, feeling ceases; from the cessation of feeling, craving ceases; from the cessation of craving, grasping ceases; from the cessation of grasping, becoming ceases; from the cessation of becoming, birth ceases; from the cessation of birth, aging and death, sorrow, lamentation, suffering, unhappiness and distress cease. Thus does this whole great mass of suffering cease.

Then the Blessed One, having let those seven days pass, arose from that meditative absorption, and at that time he spoke these verses:

When these dharmas arise
For the brahmin, diligent and meditating,
When he knows phenomena together with their cause,
Then all his doubts depart.

When these dharmas arise
For the brahmin, diligent and meditating,
When he knows suffering together with its cause,
Then all his doubts depart.

When these dharmas arise
For the brahmin, diligent and meditating,
When he has attained the exhaustion of feelings,
Then all his doubts depart.

When these dharmas arise
For the brahmin, diligent and meditating,
When he has attained the exhaustion of conditions,
Then all his doubts depart.

When these dharmas arise
For the brahmin, diligent and meditating,
When he has attained the exhaustion of the defilements,
Then all his doubts depart.

When these dharmas arise
For the brahmin, diligent and meditating,
He abides illuminating the whole world,
Like the sun risen in the sky.

When these dharmas arise
For the brahmin, diligent and meditating,
He stands, having crushed the hosts of Māra;
The Buddha is freed from every fetter.

Then the Blessed One thought, "I have realized a Dharma that is profound and profound in appearance, hard to see and hard to comprehend, not within the scope of the reasoning of logicians, subtle, to be understood by the wise, the learned and the knowing. Were I to teach it to others and they did not understand it, that would only weary me, exhaust me, and leave my mind without enthusiasm. So let me dwell alone in the depths of a forest wilderness, devoted to the yoga of dwelling at ease in this very life."

So the Blessed One inclined his mind toward remaining at ease, inactive, and not toward teaching the Dharma.

Then Brahmā Sahāṃpati, lord of this world of endurance, thought, "Alas, this world is lost! Alas, this world is lost! For only rarely, once in a long while, does a Tathāgata, an arhat, a perfectly complete buddha appear in the world, like the udumbara flower; and now that Blessed One is inclining his mind toward remaining inactive, and not toward teaching the Dharma. Let me go to him and entreat him."

Then, in the time it takes a strong man to stretch out his bent arm or to bend his outstretched arm, Brahmā Sahāṃpati vanished from the Brahmā world and stood before the Blessed One. Then Brahmā Sahāṃpati at that time addressed him in verse:

In Magadha there arose before a Dharma
That is not pure, conceived by the stained.
Open, I pray, the door of the deathless Dharma;
Teach what the stainless one has realized.

Then the Blessed One at that time spoke in verse:

Brahmā, what I have realized
With great hardship, having destroyed the faults,
This Dharma, those bound by existence and attachment
Will not rightly realize.
The path that runs against the stream,
Profound and very hard to see,
Those veiled by a mass of darkness,
Clinging to desire, will not see.

"Venerable One, among the beings born in the world and grown old in the world there are those of sharp faculties, of middling faculties and of dull faculties, those easy to set straight, easy to train, of few afflictions and of little dust; if they do not hear the sublime Dharma, they will be utterly lost. Venerable One, it is like blue lotuses, red lotuses, white water lilies or white lotuses, which are born in the water and grow large in the water: some rise up above the water, some stand level with the water, and some remain sunk within the water. Just so, Venerable One, among the beings born in the world and grown old in the world there are those of sharp faculties, of middling faculties and of dull faculties, those easy to set straight, easy to train, of few afflictions and of little dust; if they do not hear the sublime Dharma they will be utterly lost, and so there will be those who come to understand the precious Dharma. Blessed One, please teach the Dharma! Sugata, please teach the Dharma!"

Then the Blessed One thought, "Let me look upon the world with my own buddha eye." Then the Blessed One looked upon the world with his own buddha eye, and as he looked upon the world with his own buddha eye, he saw that among the beings born in the world and grown old in the world there are those whose nature is of sharp faculties, of middling faculties and of dull faculties, easy to set straight, easy to train, of few afflictions and of little dust, and that if they do not hear the sublime Dharma, they will be utterly lost. And having seen this, he turned his great compassion toward sentient beings. Then the Blessed One at that time spoke in verse:

I shall open the doors of the deathless.
Whoever wishes to listen, cast off your doubt!
Brahmā, to humankind the excellent Dharma
I shall teach in brief, not seeking to find fault.

Then Brahmā Sahāṃpati thought, "Oh, the Blessed One will teach the Dharma! How wonderful, the Sugata will teach the Dharma!" Knowing this, he was joyful, delighted, overjoyed, and filled with vast joy and gladness; he bowed his head at the Blessed One's feet, circumambulated the Blessed One three times, and vanished right there.

Then the Blessed One thought, "To whom shall I first teach the Dharma?" Then the Blessed One thought, "Let me first teach the Dharma to Ārāḍa Kālāma. He was my very first teacher, and yet he showed me the highest respect and honored me with the highest honor; and he too rejoiced in and had faith in the sublime realizations."

The gods said to the Blessed One, "Venerable One, it is seven days since the son of Kālāma died."

The Blessed One, too, entered into contemplation, and when he brought forth the vision of wisdom, he saw that seven days had passed since Ārāḍa Kālāma had died. Then the Blessed One thought, "Ah, Ārāḍa Kālāma, who has died, has been unfortunate! Ah, Ārāḍa Kālāma, who has died, has been most unfortunate, for he did not hear this Dharma and Vinaya. Had he heard this Dharma and Vinaya, he would have understood it."

Then the Blessed One thought, "To whom, then, shall I first teach the Dharma?" Then the Blessed One thought, "Let me first teach the Dharma to Udraka Rāmaputra. He was my very first teacher, and yet he showed me the highest respect and honored me with the highest honor; and he too rejoiced in and had faith in the sublime realizations."

The gods said to the Blessed One, "Venerable One, Udraka Rāmaputra died yesterday evening." The Blessed One entered into contemplation, and when he brought forth the vision of wisdom, he saw that Udraka Rāmaputra had died in the evening. Then the Blessed One thought, "Ah, Udraka Rāmaputra, who has died, has been unfortunate! Ah, Udraka Rāmaputra, who has died, has been most unfortunate, for he did not hear this Dharma and Vinaya. Had he heard this Dharma and Vinaya, he would certainly have understood it."

Then the Blessed One thought, "To whom, then, shall I first teach the Dharma?" Then he thought, "Let me first teach the Dharma to the monks of the group of five, for when formerly I dwelt devoted to the yoga of abandoning suffering, they stayed with me with reverence, respect and affection."

Then the Blessed One thought, "Where are the monks of the group of five staying now?" And with his divine eye, purified and surpassing that of humans, the Blessed One saw the monks of the group of five staying at Vārāṇasī, in Ṛṣivadana, in the Deer Park. Having seen them, he remained at the foot of the tree of awakening for as long as he wished, and then set out, wandering, toward Vārāṇasī, the town of Kāśī.

At that time Upaka the Ājīvika had set out on that very road, and Upaka the Ājīvika saw the Blessed One coming along the road. Seeing him, he said this: "Śramaṇa Gautama, your faculties are serene, the color of your face is utterly pure, and your complexion is radiant. Venerable Gautama, who is your teacher? Under whom did you go forth? Whose Dharma do you delight in?"

Then the Blessed One at that time spoke in verse:

There is none who is like me;
I have no teacher whatsoever.
In the world I alone am a buddha;
I have attained true and supreme awakening.

Overcoming all, knowing all the world,
I am not stained by any of these phenomena.
Having abandoned all, free of craving, fully liberated,
Having directly known it by myself, on whom should I rely?

The Tathāgata, teacher of gods and humans,
Possessed of all the powers of omniscience,
Having realized awakening himself, proclaims it well;
Unequalled and without peer, on whom should he rely?

I am the arhat of the world;
Since I am unsurpassed in the world,
In the world together with its gods
I am the Victor who overcomes Māra.

"Venerable Gautama, do you call yourself a Victor?"

Those who have attained the exhaustion of the defilements
Should be known as victors like me.
Since I have conquered evil things,
Therefore, Upaka, I am a Victor.

"Venerable Gautama, where are you going?"

Having gone to Vārāṇasī,
I shall beat the drum of the Dharma
And set fully turning the wheel of the Dharma
That has not been turned in the world.

The omniscient Buddha, gone to peace,
Who has crossed beyond attachment to the world:
Unless he carries the world across,
The holy being cannot be renowned.

Upaka the Ājīvika said, "Venerable Gautama, Victor, excellent!" and went on along the road.

Then the Blessed One, wandering through the land of Kāśī, arrived at Vārāṇasī. At that time the monks of the group of five were staying at Vārāṇasī, in Ṛṣivadana, in the Deer Park. The monks of the group of five saw the Blessed One from a long way off. Seeing him, they deliberately took counsel with one another, and when they had sat down properly, they made this agreement: "Friends, the śramaṇa Gautama is unsteady, busy with many affairs, living in abundance, fallen from his renunciation. He eats much rice and warm porridge, anoints his body with butter, cream and sesame oil, and bathes his body in gentle water, and now here he comes. When he comes to us, we will not speak to him, we will not pay him homage, we will not rise, and we will not stand up to offer him a seat. Instead, pointing to a seat set out just as it is, we will say these words: 'Venerable Gautama, there is a seat; sit down if you wish.'" So they agreed.

But the nearer the Blessed One came in order to see the monks of the group of five, the less the monks of the group of five could bear the Blessed One's splendor, glory and dignity, and they rose from their seats. Some prepared a seat for the Blessed One; some set water for washing his feet and a footstool to one side; and some went to meet him, took his robe and his Dharma robes, and said, "Come, friend Gautama! Welcome, friend Gautama! Friend Gautama, sit on this very seat that has been prepared."

Then the Blessed One, knowing, "Alas, these foolish men made a rule and then broke it themselves," sat on the seat that had been prepared.

Then the monks of the group of five went on addressing the Blessed One by his name, by his clan name, and as "Venerable." Then the Blessed One said to the monks of the group of five, "Monks, do not go on addressing the Tathāgata by his name, by his clan name, or as 'Venerable.' That will bring you harm, disadvantage and suffering for a long time."

"Why is that? Monks, whoever goes on addressing the Tathāgata, the arhat, the perfectly complete Buddha, by his name, by his clan name, or as 'Venerable,' that foolish man will meet with harm, disadvantage and suffering for a long time."

They said this: "Venerable Gautama, if by your former way of life, your conduct and your austerities you were unable to attain any distinction of a noble state beyond human qualities, and did not attain even the least knowledge, or seeing, or dwelling in direct contact, then now that you are unsteady, busy with many affairs, living in abundance and fallen from your renunciation, eating much rice and warm porridge, anointing your body with butter, cream and sesame oil, and bathing your body in gentle water, what need is there even to speak of it?"

"Monks, do you see the Tathāgata's complexion and the clarity of his faculties to be otherwise, different from before?"

They said this: "Venerable Gautama, it is so."

Then the Blessed One said to the monks of the group of five, "Monks, one who has gone forth should not resort to these two extremes, should not cultivate them, and should not honor them. What are the two? Devotion to the pleasures of desire among desirable things, which is low, base, vulgar and common; and devotion to the exhaustion of oneself, which is painful, ignoble and harmful. When you do not rely on these two extremes, there is the middle path, which produces vision and produces wisdom, and leads to peace, to direct knowledge, to perfect awakening and to nirvāṇa. And what is the middle path? It is this noble eightfold path: right view, right thought, right speech, right action, right livelihood, right effort, right mindfulness and right meditative absorption."

As the Blessed One went on making the monks of the group of five understand what was to be understood in this way, when the Blessed One gave instruction to two of the monks of the group of five in the morning, three would go into the city for alms, and whatever the three obtained sustained all six of them. When the Blessed One gave instruction to three of the monks of the group of five in the afternoon, two would go into the city for alms, and whatever the two obtained sustained the group of five; for the Tathāgata ate only in the morning.

Then the Blessed One said to the monks of the group of five:

"Monks, with regard to things not heard before, as I attended properly to 'This is the noble truth of suffering,' vision arose in me, and knowledge, insight, understanding and realization arose.

"Monks, with regard to things not heard before, as I attended properly to 'This is the origin of suffering; this is the cessation of suffering; this is the path that leads to the cessation of suffering,' vision arose, and knowledge, insight, understanding and realization arose.

"Monks, with regard to things not heard before, as I attended properly to 'This noble truth of suffering is to be fully understood by me through direct knowledge,' vision arose, and knowledge, insight, understanding and realization arose.

"Monks, with regard to things not heard before, as I attended properly to 'This noble truth of the origin of suffering is to be abandoned by me through direct knowledge,' vision arose, and knowledge, insight, understanding and realization arose.

"Monks, with regard to things not heard before, as I attended properly to 'This noble truth of the cessation of suffering is to be actualized by me through direct knowledge,' vision arose, and knowledge, insight, understanding and realization arose.

"Monks, with regard to things not heard before, as I attended properly to 'This noble truth of the path that leads to the cessation of suffering is to be cultivated by me through direct knowledge,' vision arose, and knowledge, insight, understanding and realization arose.

"Monks, with regard to things not heard before, as I attended properly to 'This noble truth of suffering has been fully understood by me through direct knowledge,' vision arose, and knowledge, insight, understanding and realization arose.

"Monks, with regard to things not heard before, as I attended properly to 'This noble truth of the origin of suffering has been abandoned by me through direct knowledge,' vision arose, and knowledge, insight, understanding and realization arose.

"Monks, with regard to things not heard before, as I attended properly to 'This noble truth of the cessation of suffering has been actualized by me through direct knowledge,' vision arose, and knowledge, insight, understanding and realization arose.

"Monks, with regard to things not heard before, as I attended properly to 'This noble truth of the path that leads to the cessation of suffering has been cultivated by me through direct knowledge,' vision arose, and knowledge, insight, understanding and realization arose.

"Monks, as long as the vision of these four noble truths, thus repeated three times and turned in twelve aspects, had not arisen in me, as long as there was no knowledge, no insight, no understanding, and realization had not arisen, for that long I did not dwell much with a mind released from this world with its gods, its Māras and its Brahmās, from this generation with its śramaṇas and brahmins, its gods and humans, a mind gone forth from it, detached from it, fully liberated and free of distortion; and, monks, I did not recognize that 'I have fully and perfectly awakened to unsurpassed, true and complete awakening.'"

"Monks, once the vision of these four noble truths, thus repeated three times and turned in twelve aspects, had arisen in me, and knowledge, insight, understanding and realization had arisen, from then on I dwelt much with a mind released from this world with its gods, its Māras and its Brahmās, from this generation with its śramaṇas and brahmins, its gods and humans, a mind gone forth from it, detached from it, fully liberated and free of distortion. And, monks, from then on I recognized that 'I have fully and perfectly awakened to unsurpassed, true and complete awakening.'"

When this discourse on the Dharma had been spoken, there arose in the venerable Kauṇḍinya and in eighty thousand gods the eye of Dharma regarding phenomena, free of dust and free of stain.

Then the Blessed One said to the venerable Kauṇḍinya, "Kauṇḍinya, have you understood the Dharma?"

"Blessed One, I have understood."

"Kauṇḍinya, have you understood the Dharma? Have you understood?"

"Sugata, I have understood. I have understood."

Because the venerable Kauṇḍinya had understood the Dharma, the venerable one was therefore given the name Ājñāta Kauṇḍinya, "Kauṇḍinya Who Has Understood."

The earth-dwelling yakṣas raised the cry, "The venerable Kauṇḍinya has understood the Dharma!" and they proclaimed aloud, "Friends, at Vārāṇasī, in Ṛṣivadana, in the Deer Park, the Blessed One has turned the wheel of the Dharma, the wheel in accord with the Dharma, repeated three times in twelve aspects, which no one in the world, whether śramaṇa, brahmin, god, Māra or Brahmā, has turned in accord with the Dharma. He has turned it for the benefit of many people, for the happiness of many people, out of compassion for the world, and for the welfare, benefit and happiness of gods and humans. Therefore the families of the gods will flourish, and the ranks of the asuras will dwindle away."

When the yakṣas who move through the sky heard the cry of the earth-dwelling yakṣas, the cry passed on through them, the gods of the Four Great Kings, the gods of the Heaven of the Thirty-Three, the Yāma gods, the Tuṣita gods, the Nirmāṇarati gods and the Paranirmitavaśavartin gods, and in that instant, that moment, that very second, in an instant, a moment, a second, it resounded all the way to the Brahmā world. The Brahmakāyika gods too raised the cry and proclaimed aloud, "Friends, at Vārāṇasī, in Ṛṣivadana, in the Deer Park, the Blessed One has turned the wheel in accord with the Dharma, repeated three times in twelve aspects, which no one in the world, whether śramaṇa, brahmin, god, Māra or Brahmā, has ever even once turned in accord with the Dharma. He has turned it for the benefit of many people, for the happiness of many people, out of compassion for the world, and for the welfare, benefit and happiness of gods and humans. Therefore the ranks of the gods will flourish, and the ranks of the asuras will dwindle away."

Because the Blessed One turned the wheel of the Dharma, the wheel in accord with the Dharma, repeated three times in twelve aspects, at Vārāṇasī, in Ṛṣivadana, in the Deer Park, this discourse on the Dharma was therefore given the name "The Turning of the Wheel of the Dharma."

Then the Blessed One spoke to the monks of the group of five a second time: "Monks, there are these four noble truths. What are the four? The noble truth of suffering, the noble truth of the origin of suffering, the noble truth of the cessation of suffering, and the noble truth of the path that leads to the cessation of suffering.

"What is the noble truth of suffering? Birth is suffering, aging is suffering, sickness is suffering, death is suffering, separation from what is dear is suffering, meeting with what is not dear is suffering, seeking what one wants and not finding it, that too is suffering; in short, the five aggregates of grasping are suffering. In order to understand it fully, one should cultivate the noble eightfold path.

"What is the noble truth of the origin of suffering? It is the craving for renewed existence, accompanied by delight and passion, that longs for this and that. In order to abandon it, one should cultivate the noble eightfold path.

"What is the noble truth of the cessation of suffering? It is the complete abandoning, without remainder, of that very craving for renewed existence, accompanied by delight and passion, that delights in this and that; its relinquishing, its removal, its exhaustion, freedom from passion for it, its cessation, its pacification and its disappearance. In order to actualize it, one should cultivate the noble eightfold path.

"What is the noble truth of the path that leads to the cessation of suffering? It is the noble eightfold path, namely: right view, right thought, right speech, right action, right livelihood, right effort, right mindfulness and right meditative absorption; and that too should be cultivated."

When this discourse on the Dharma had been spoken, the mind of the venerable Ājñāta Kauṇḍinya was liberated from the defilements, without grasping. In the remaining monks of the group of five there arose the eye of Dharma regarding phenomena, free of dust and free of stain. At that time there were in the world two arhats: one, and the second was the Blessed One.

Then the Blessed One spoke again to the remaining monks of the group of five: "Monks, form is not the self. Monks, if form were the self, form would not lead to harm and suffering, and with regard to form one would obtain what one wished: 'Let my form be like this; let it not be like that.' Monks, because form is not the self, form leads to harm and suffering, and with regard to form one does not obtain what one wishes: 'Let my form be like this; let it not be like that.'

"Monks, feeling, perception, formations and consciousness are not the self. Monks, if consciousness were the self, consciousness would not lead to harm and suffering, and with regard to consciousness one would obtain what one wished: 'Let my consciousness be like this; let it not be like that.'"

"Monks, because consciousness is not the self, consciousness leads to harm and suffering, and with regard to consciousness one does not obtain what one wishes: 'Let my consciousness be like this; let it not be like that.'

"Monks, what do you think? Is form permanent or impermanent?"

"Venerable One, it is impermanent."

"Is what is impermanent suffering, or is it not suffering?"

"Venerable One, it is suffering."

"Would a learned noble śrāvaka regard what is impermanent, suffering and subject to change as the self, thinking, 'This is the self; the self is this; this is my self'?"

"No, Venerable One."

"Monks, what do you think? Are feeling, perception, formations and consciousness permanent or impermanent?"

"Venerable One, they are impermanent."

"Is what is impermanent suffering, or is it not suffering?"

"Venerable One, it is suffering."

"Would a learned noble śrāvaka regard what is impermanent, suffering and subject to change as the self, thinking, 'This is the self; the self is this; this is my self'?"

"No, Venerable One."

"Therefore, monks, whatever form there is, of whatever kind, whether past, future or present, internal or external, coarse or subtle, inferior or excellent, far or near, all of it should be seen as it really is, with right wisdom, thus: 'This is not the self; the self is not this; this is not my self.' Therefore, monks, whatever feeling, perception, formations and consciousness there are, of whatever kind, whether past, future or present, internal or external, coarse or subtle, inferior or excellent, far or near, all of them should be seen as they really are, with right wisdom, thus: 'This is not the self; the self is not this; this is not my self.'

"Monks, in this way the learned noble śrāvaka sees these five aggregates of grasping as not the self and not belonging to the self. When he sees them in this way, he grasps at nothing whatsoever in the world. When there is no grasping, there is no torment. When there is no torment, he himself attains complete nirvāṇa, and he knows: 'My birth is exhausted; I have lived the holy life; I have done what was to be done; I know no other existence than this.'"

When this discourse on the Dharma had been spoken, the minds of the remaining monks of the group of five were liberated from the defilements, without grasping. At that time there were five arhats, and the sixth was the Blessed One.

Then the Blessed One stayed on the bank of the Varaṇā River at Vārāṇasī. At that time there lived in Vārāṇasī a young man named Yaśas, the son of the one known as the guild master. Day after day he amused himself with music played by women and indulged in every pleasure he liked, until, his body languid, weary and spent, he quickly fell asleep. Those women too, their bodies languid, weary and spent, fell asleep. When Yaśas, the guild master's son, awoke in the night, he saw all those women lying sprawled, naked, their hair disheveled, their arms flung out, slack and drooling. When he saw them, the perception of a charnel ground arose in him with regard to his wives.

Then Yaśas, the guild master's son, came down from the great upper chamber, put on a pair of jeweled sandals worth a hundred thousand, and went to the door of the bedchamber. Having arrived, he cried out in distress, "Friends, I am oppressed! Friends, I am ruined!" Nonhuman beings opened its door for him and made it so that his voice was not heard. Then Yaśas, the guild master's son, went to the door of the house. Having arrived, he cried out in distress, "Friends, I am oppressed! Friends, I am ruined!" Nonhuman beings opened its door for him and made it so that his voice was not heard. Then Yaśas, the guild master's son, went to the gate of the city. Having arrived, he cried out in distress, "Friends, I am oppressed! Friends, I am ruined!" Nonhuman beings opened its gate for him and made it so that his voice was not heard.

Then Yaśas, the guild master's son, went toward the Varaṇā River. At that time, on the bank of the Varaṇā River, outside his dwelling, in the open air, the Blessed One, awaiting the coming of Yaśas, the guild master's son, was walking up and down, rising again and again to pace. Yaśas, the guild master's son, saw the Blessed One from a long way off, walking up and down on the bank of the Varaṇā River. Seeing him, he cried out in distress, "Śramaṇa, I am oppressed! Śramaṇa, I am ruined!"

Then the Blessed One said this to Yaśas, the guild master's son: "Young man, come here. In this place there is no oppression for you; here there is no ruin."

Then Yaśas, the guild master's son, took off his pair of jeweled sandals worth a hundred thousand on the bank of the Varaṇā River, crossed the Varaṇā River, and went to where the Blessed One was. Having arrived, he bowed his head at the Blessed One's feet and sat down to one side. Then the Blessed One led Yaśas, the guild master's son, to his own dwelling, and when he arrived there he sat on the seat that had been prepared. Having sat down, the Blessed One, with talk on the Dharma, instructed Yaśas, the guild master's son, led him to take it up, inspired him and filled him with delight. He explained at length the talk on the Dharma that the buddhas, the blessed ones, give at the outset, namely: talk on generosity, talk on moral discipline and talk on the higher realms; the meager satisfaction and the drawbacks of sensual pleasures; defilement and purification; praise of renunciation and of solitude; and the qualities that belong to the side of purification.

When the Blessed One saw that he had a joyful mind, a wholesome mind, a contented mind, a mind free of obscurations, that he was fortunate, and that he was able to understand if the exalted teaching of the Dharma were explained, he then explained at length the exalted teaching of the Dharma that belongs to the buddhas, the blessed ones, namely the Four Noble Truths: suffering, origin, cessation and the path. Just as a clean cloth without any dark stain, when dipped into a suitable dye, takes the dye well, in the same way Yaśas, the guild master's son, while still seated on that very seat, realized the Four Noble Truths, namely suffering, origin, cessation and the path.

Then Yaśas, the guild master's son, having seen the Dharma, attained the Dharma, understood the Dharma and fathomed the depths of the Dharma; having crossed beyond uncertainty and crossed beyond doubt; no longer relying on others and no longer led by others; and having gained confidence in the teachings the Teacher had taught, rose from his seat, draped his upper garment over one shoulder, joined his palms, bowed toward the Blessed One, and said this to the Blessed One: "Venerable One, I have gone beyond; I have gone utterly beyond. I go for refuge to the Blessed One, to the Dharma and to the Saṅgha. Please accept me as a layman. From this day forth, for as long as I live, even at the cost of my life, I go for refuge with full faith."

That same night a maidservant woke from sleep and saw that young Yaśas was not on his great bed. Seeing this, she went to where the householder, the guild master, was, and when she arrived she said to the householder, the guild master: "Master, please be aware: young Yaśas is not on his great bed."

Then the householder, the guild master, thought: "Surely bandits or rogues have not led the young man away for the sake of his wealth?" Thinking this, he sent messengers on horseback in the four directions, and he himself, together with men carrying lamps in their hands, went toward the Varaṇā River. On the bank of the Varaṇā River the householder, the guild master, saw a pair of jeweled sandals, worth a hundred thousand, that had been taken off. Seeing them, he thought: "Bandits or rogues have not led the young man away for the sake of his wealth. Since a pair of jeweled sandals worth a hundred thousand has been taken off here on the bank of the Varaṇā River, surely the young man has crossed the Varaṇā River from this very bank. That is why a pair of jeweled sandals worth a hundred thousand has been left here on the bank of the Varaṇā River." Having thought this, he crossed the Varaṇā River from that very bank and went to where the Blessed One was.

The Blessed One saw the householder, the guild master, from far away. Seeing him, he thought: "I shall bring about a feat of miraculous power of such a kind that the householder, the guild master, will not see young Yaśas even while he sits on this very seat." Then the Blessed One brought about a feat of miraculous power such that the householder, the guild master, did not see young Yaśas, even though he was sitting on that very seat.

Then the householder, the guild master, went to where the Blessed One was, and when he arrived he said to the Blessed One: "Blessed One, have you perhaps seen young Yaśas?"

"Householder, in that case, sit down. There is a chance that you will see young Yaśas sitting on this very seat."

Then the householder, the guild master, thought: "Without doubt the Blessed One has seen young Yaśas, for the Blessed One has said, 'Householder, in that case, sit down. There is a chance that you will see young Yaśas sitting on this very seat.'" Understanding this, he felt joy, contentment, supreme joy, vast joy and gladness of mind; he bowed his head at the Blessed One's feet and sat down to one side. When he had sat down to one side, the Blessed One instructed the householder, the guild master, with talk on the Dharma, and so on, as before. When this discourse on the Dharma had been taught, the stainless, immaculate eye of Dharma regarding phenomena arose in the householder, the guild master. And the venerable Yaśas, though still adorned with all his ornaments, through non-grasping had his mind liberated from the defilements.

Then the Blessed One withdrew the feat of miraculous power he had brought about, and on that occasion he spoke this verse:

Though adorned with ornaments, one who practices the Dharma,
Tamed and perfectly restrained, living the pure life,
Who has laid down punishment toward all beings,
That one is a brahmin, a renunciant, a monk.

At that time there were six arhats in the world, and the seventh was the Blessed One.

The householder, the guild master, saw young Yaśas sitting on that very seat. Seeing him, he said to young Yaśas: "Come here, young man. Your mother is weary in mind and exhausted in body and is crying out in lament. Let us go home."

Then the Blessed One said to the householder, the guild master: "Householder, what do you think? Would one who, by the knowledge of one beyond training and the view of one beyond training, has fully realized the Four Noble Truths, namely suffering, origin, cessation and the path, live as a householder in a home and enjoy sensual pleasures with hoarded wealth?"

"No, Venerable One."

"Householder, just as you, by the knowledge of one in training and the view of one in training, have fully realized the Four Noble Truths, namely suffering, origin, cessation and the path, in the same way young Yaśas, by the wisdom of one beyond training and the view of one beyond training, has fully realized the Four Noble Truths, namely suffering, origin, cessation and the path."

"Venerable One, it is a gain well gained for young Yaśas that, by the knowledge of one beyond training and the view of one beyond training, he has fully realized the Four Noble Truths, namely suffering, origin, cessation and the path. Blessed One, it would be good if, with the renunciant Yaśas following behind you as your attendant, you would, out of compassion, come to where the guild master lives."

The Blessed One consented to the householder, the guild master, by remaining silent. Then the householder, the guild master, understanding that the Blessed One had consented by remaining silent, bowed his head at the Blessed One's feet and left the Blessed One's presence.

Then, when that night had passed, in the morning the Blessed One put on his lower robe, took his bowl and robes, and, with the venerable Yaśas following behind as his attendant renunciant, went to where the house of the householder, the guild master, was. At that time the mother and the former wife of the venerable Yaśas were staying at the inner gate in order to welcome the Blessed One again and again. The mother and the former wife of the venerable Yaśas saw the Blessed One from far away. Seeing him, they prepared a seat for the Blessed One and said this: "Come, Blessed One. Welcome, Blessed One. Blessed One, please be seated on the seat that has been prepared." When they had said this, the Blessed One sat on the seat that had been prepared.

Then the mother and the former wife of the venerable Yaśas bowed their heads at the Blessed One's feet and sat down to one side. When they had sat down to one side, the Blessed One, with talk on the Dharma, instructed the mother and the former wife of the venerable Yaśas, led them to take it up, inspired them and filled them with delight. He explained at length the talk on the Dharma that the buddhas, the blessed ones, give at the outset, namely: talk on generosity, talk on moral discipline and talk on the higher realms; the meager satisfaction and the drawbacks of sensual pleasures; defilement and purification; praise of renunciation and of solitude; and the qualities that belong to the side of purification.

When the Blessed One saw that the two of them had joyful minds, contented minds, minds free of obscurations, that they were fortunate, and that they were able to understand if the exalted teaching of the Dharma were explained, he then explained at length the exalted teaching of the Dharma that belongs to the buddhas, the blessed ones, namely the Four Noble Truths: suffering, origin, cessation and the path. Just as a clean cloth without any dark stain, when dipped into a suitable dye, takes the dye well, in the same way the mother and the former wife of the venerable Yaśas, while still seated on those very seats, fully realized the Four Noble Truths, namely suffering, origin, cessation and the path.

Then the mother and the former wife of the venerable Yaśas, having seen the Dharma, attained the Dharma, understood the Dharma and fathomed the depths of the Dharma; having crossed beyond uncertainty and crossed beyond doubt; no longer relying on others and no longer led by others; and having gained confidence in the teachings the Teacher had taught, rose from their seats, joined their palms, bowed toward the Blessed One, and said this to the Blessed One: "Venerable One, we have gone beyond; we have gone utterly beyond. We go for refuge to the Blessed One, to the Dharma and to the Saṅgha of monks. Please accept us as lay followers. From this day forth, for as long as we live, even at the cost of our lives, we go for refuge with full faith. Venerable One, therefore, please take your meal here."

The Blessed One consented to the mother and the former wife of the venerable Yaśas by remaining silent. Then the mother and the former wife of the venerable Yaśas, understanding that the Blessed One had consented by remaining silent, and seeing the Blessed One comfortably seated, served him with their own hands pure and delicious food, both hard and soft, and satisfied him. Having served him with their own hands, in many ways, pure and delicious food, both hard and soft, and having satisfied him, and knowing that the Blessed One had finished eating, had washed his hands and had put away his bowl, they took much lower seats and sat before the Blessed One in order to listen to the Dharma.

Then the Blessed One, with talk on the Dharma, instructed the mother and the former wife of the venerable Yaśas, led them to take it up, inspired them and filled them with delight. Having in many ways instructed them with talk on the Dharma, led them to take it up, inspired them and filled them with delight, he rose from his seat and departed.

In Vārāṇasī, the second, third, fourth and fifth sons of householder guild masters, namely Pūrṇa, Vimala, Gavāṃpati and Subāhu, these sons of guild masters, heard that Yaśas, the guild master's son, had shaved off his hair and beard, put on saffron robes, and, out of genuine faith alone, gone forth from home into homelessness. When they heard this, they thought: "Oh! The Buddha can surely be no ordinary one, and his teaching of the Dharma can surely be nothing ordinary either, since now even Yaśas, the guild master's son, who is so very young and so fond of comfort, has shaved off his hair and beard, put on saffron robes, and, out of genuine faith alone, gone forth from home into homelessness. Come, let us too shave off our hair and beards, put on saffron robes, and, out of genuine faith alone, go forth from home into homelessness."

Then those sons of guild masters, the second, third, fourth and fifth, namely Pūrṇa, Vimala, Gavāṃpati and Subāhu, went out from Vārāṇasī and went to where the Blessed One was. When they arrived, they bowed their heads at the Blessed One's feet and sat down to one side.

When they had sat down to one side, those sons of guild masters, the second, third, fourth and fifth, namely Pūrṇa, Vimala, Gavāṃpati and Subāhu, said this to the Blessed One: "Venerable One, if you would grant us the going forth and full ordination, the state of a monk, in the well-spoken Dharma and Vinaya, we would live the pure life in the presence of the Blessed One."

Those venerable ones received the going forth and full ordination, the state of a monk, in the well-spoken Dharma and Vinaya. Having gone forth in this way, those venerable ones dwelt alone, secluded, heedful, ardent and resolute. Dwelling alone, secluded, heedful, ardent and resolute, they directly realized in this very life, through their own higher knowledge, and accomplished, the unsurpassed culmination of the pure life, for the sake of which sons of good family shave off their hair and beard, put on saffron robes, and, out of genuine faith alone, go forth from home into homelessness. They understood: "Our births are exhausted. We have lived the pure life. We have done what was to be done. We know no existence beyond this one." And those venerable ones became arhats, endowed with complete knowledge, their minds thoroughly liberated. At that time there were ten arhats in the world, and the eleventh was the Blessed One.

In Vārāṇasī, fifty youths, sons of the foremost townsmen, heard that the sons of guild masters, the first, second, third, fourth and fifth, namely Yaśas, Pūrṇa, Vimala, Gavāṃpati and Subāhu, had shaved off their hair and beards, put on saffron robes, and, out of genuine faith alone, gone forth from home into homelessness. When they heard this, they thought: "Oh! The Buddha can surely be no ordinary one, and his teaching of the Dharma can surely be nothing ordinary either, since now even the sons of guild masters, the first, second, third, fourth and fifth, namely Yaśas, Pūrṇa, Vimala, Gavāṃpati and Subāhu, who are so very young and so fond of comfort, have shaved off their hair and beards, put on saffron robes, and, out of genuine faith alone, gone forth from home into homelessness. Come, let us too shave off our hair and beards, put on saffron robes, and, out of genuine faith alone, go forth from home into homelessness."

Then those fifty youths, sons of the foremost citizens, went out from Vārāṇasī and went to where the Blessed One was. When they arrived, they bowed their heads at the Blessed One's feet and sat down to one side. When they had sat down to one side, the fifty youths, sons of the foremost townsmen, said this to the Blessed One: "Venerable One, if you would grant us the going forth and full ordination, the state of a monk, in the well-spoken Dharma and Vinaya, we would live the pure life in the presence of the Blessed One."

Those venerable ones received the going forth and full ordination, the state of a monk, in the well-spoken Dharma and Vinaya. Having gone forth in this way, those venerable ones dwelt alone, secluded, heedful, ardent and resolute. Dwelling alone, secluded, heedful, ardent and resolute, they, as before, for the sake of which sons of good family, and so on, up to: they became arhats, endowed with complete knowledge, their minds thoroughly liberated. At that time there were sixty arhats in the world, and the sixty-first was the Blessed One.

Then the Blessed One was staying in the Deer Park at Ṛṣivadana near Vārāṇasī. Then the Blessed One said to the monks: "Monks, I am freed from all the bonds of gods and humans. Monks, you too are freed from all the bonds of gods and humans. Monks, for the benefit of many beings, for the happiness of many beings, out of compassion for the world, and for the benefit and happiness of gods and humans, set out wandering today, and do not go two of you by one way. I too shall go wandering to where the town of Senānī at Uruvilvā is."

Then Māra the Evil One thought: "This renunciant Gautama, staying in the Deer Park at Ṛṣivadana near Vārāṇasī, is teaching the Dharma to his śrāvakas in this way: 'Monks, I am freed from all the bonds of gods and humans. Monks, you too are freed from all the bonds of gods and humans. Monks, for the benefit of many beings,' and so on, up to 'set out wandering today,' and he says, 'I too shall go wandering to where the town of Senānī at Uruvilvā is.' Come, I shall approach him in order to do something to stir up trouble."

Then Māra the Evil One magically transformed himself into the guise of a brahmin youth, went to where the Blessed One was, and when he arrived, he stood before the Blessed One and addressed him in verse:

"If, though not free, you perceive yourself as free,
How can you think yourself free?
Renunciant, you have not freed yourself,
And you are bound by a great bond."

Then the Blessed One thought: "Ah, this is Māra the Evil One; he has come here to stir up trouble." Realizing this, he spoke in verse:

"I am freed from all the bonds,
Whatever they may be, of gods and humans.
You who make obstacles are defeated:
Evil One, know that this is so."

Then Māra the Evil One thought: "The renunciant Gautama has recognized me with his mind." Realizing this, he was miserable, unhappy and filled with regret, and he vanished on the spot.

Then the Blessed One said to the monks a second time: "Monks, I am freed from all the bonds of gods and humans."

"Monks, you too are freed from all the bonds of gods and humans. Monks, for the benefit of many beings," and so on, up to "set out wandering today," as above. "I too shall go to where the town of Senānī at Uruvilvā is."

"Venerable One, we shall do as you say," the monks replied, having listened to the Blessed One, and they set out wandering through the countryside.

Then the Blessed One, having stayed in Vārāṇasī for as long as he wished, set out wandering toward the town of Senānī at Uruvilvā, and, wandering by stages, he arrived at the town of Senānī. Then the Blessed One went to a dense cotton grove, and when he arrived, he sat at the foot of a tree for his daytime rest.

At that time a company of sixty Bhadravargīyas would amuse themselves every day outside the town of Senānī with music made by women, delighting and indulging themselves. One of those women broke her agreement and ran away. Then the company of sixty Bhadravargīyas, searching for that woman, went to the dense cotton grove, and the company of sixty Bhadravargīyas saw the Blessed One sitting at the foot of a tree: handsome, inspiring faith, his senses calm, his mind calm, his mind supremely tamed, endowed with calm and with discipline, blazing with splendor like a golden pillar. Seeing him, they went to where the Blessed One was, and when they arrived, they said this to the Blessed One: "Blessed One, have you perhaps seen a woman?"

"Young men, what is a woman to you?"

"Venerable One, we, the company of sixty Bhadravargīyas, amuse ourselves here outside the town of Senānī with music made by women, delighting and indulging ourselves. While we were amusing ourselves with them, delighting and indulging ourselves, one woman broke her agreement and ran away, and we are searching for her."

"Young men, what do you think? Which would you choose: searching for a woman, or searching for yourselves?"

"Venerable One, what use is a woman to us? We would choose searching for ourselves."

"Young men, in that case, sit down, and I shall teach you the Dharma."

Then the company of sixty Bhadravargīyas bowed their heads at the Blessed One's feet and sat down to one side. When they had sat down to one side, the Blessed One instructed the company of sixty Bhadravargīyas with talk on the Dharma, and so on, as before. Then the company of sixty Bhadravargīyas, while still seated on those very seats, fully realized the Four Noble Truths, namely suffering, origin, cessation and the path.
