Then the company of sixty Bhadravargīyas, having seen the Dharma, attained the Dharma, understood the Dharma and fathomed the depths of the Dharma; having crossed beyond uncertainty and crossed beyond doubt; no longer relying on others and no longer led by others; and having gained confidence in the teachings the Teacher had taught, rose from their seats, draped their upper garments over one shoulder, joined their palms, bowed toward the Blessed One, and said this to the Blessed One: "Venerable One, we have gone beyond; we have gone utterly beyond. We go for refuge to the Blessed One, to the Dharma and to the Saṅgha of monks. Please accept us as lay followers. From this day forth, for as long as we live, even at the cost of our lives, we go for refuge with full faith."

Then the company of sixty Bhadravargīyas, delighting in and rejoicing at what the Blessed One had said, bowed their heads at the Blessed One's feet and left the Blessed One's presence.

Then, when that night had passed, in the morning the Blessed One put on his lower robe, took his bowl and robes, and entered the town of Senānī for alms. As the Blessed One entered the town of Senānī for alms, he thought: "Now then, to whom in the town of Senānī should I teach the Dharma first?" Then the Blessed One thought: "I shall teach the Dharma first to the village girls Nandā and Nandabalā, for when I was formerly dwelling devoted to the discipline of abandoning suffering, they served me with fresh butter and milk porridge as sweet as honey."

Then the Blessed One went to where the house of the village girls Nandā and Nandabalā was. The village girls Nandā and Nandabalā saw the Blessed One from far away. Seeing him, they prepared a seat for the Blessed One and said this: "Come, Blessed One. Welcome, Blessed One. Blessed One, please be seated on the seat that has been prepared." When they had said this, the Blessed One sat on the seat that had been prepared.

Then the village girls Nandā and Nandabalā bowed their heads at the Blessed One's feet and sat down to one side. When they had sat down to one side, the Blessed One instructed the village girls Nandā and Nandabalā with talk on the Dharma, and so on, up to "with full faith they went for refuge," as above.

Then the village girls Nandā and Nandabalā said this to the Blessed One: "Venerable One, therefore, please take your meal here." The Blessed One consented to the village girls Nandā and Nandabalā by remaining silent. Then the village girls Nandā and Nandabalā, understanding that the Blessed One had consented by remaining silent, and seeing the Blessed One comfortably seated, served him with their own hands pure and delicious food, both hard and soft, and satisfied him. Having served him with their own hands, in many ways, pure and delicious food, both hard and soft, they satisfied him. Knowing that the Blessed One had finished eating, had washed his hands and had put away his bowl, they took much lower seats and sat before the Blessed One in order to listen to the Dharma.

Then the Blessed One, with talk on the Dharma, instructed the village girls Nandā and Nandabalā, led them to take it up, inspired them and filled them with delight; then he rose from his seat and departed.

Then, as the Blessed One was leaving the town of Senānī, he thought: "Here in the land of Magadha, who is there, among the renunciants or brahmins honored for their good character, such that if I were to win him over, then through his being won over a great multitude of people would be won over with little trouble?"

At that time there was Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, a hundred and twenty years old, aged and decrepit. The people of Magadha honored, respected, revered and worshipped him, holding him in esteem as an arhat, and with a following of five hundred he practiced austerities at a hermitage on the bank of the Nairañjanā River.

Then the Blessed One thought: "This Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, a hundred and twenty years old, aged and decrepit, whom the people of Magadha," and so on as above, up to "practices austerities. Come, I shall win over Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic; if he is won over, a great multitude of people will be won over with little trouble."

Then the Blessed One went to where the hermitage of Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, was. Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, saw the Blessed One from far away. Seeing him, he prepared a seat for the Blessed One and said this: "Come, great renunciant. Welcome, great renunciant. Great renunciant, it has been a long time; from where have you come to these parts? Great renunciant, please be seated on the seat that has been prepared." The Blessed One sat on the seat that had been prepared.

Then Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, and the Blessed One exchanged many kinds of pleasant and agreeable talk face to face, and Kāśyapa sat down to one side. When he had sat down to one side, the Blessed One said this to Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic: "Kāśyapa, if it is no inconvenience to you, I would stay one night in your fire hall."

"Great renunciant, it is no inconvenience to me. However, a fierce nāga lives there, and he will do you harm."

"Kāśyapa, just give your permission; he will not harm me."

"Great renunciant, if it does not trouble you, great renunciant, stay just as you please."

Then the Blessed One went to where the fire hall of Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, was. When he arrived, he washed his feet outside the fire hall and went inside. He spread a mat of grass himself, held his body upright, crossed his legs, set his mindfulness before him, and sat.

The fierce nāga saw the Blessed One from far away. Seeing him, through the power of his anger he sent forth smoke. The Blessed One, too, through the power of his miraculous ability, sent forth smoke. The fierce nāga, through the power of his anger, burst into flame. The Blessed One, too, entered the meditative absorption of the fire element. Then, through the power of the fierce nāga's anger and the power of the Blessed One's miraculous ability, the whole fire hall blazed, blazed fiercely, blazed fiercely all around, and became a single mass of flame, burning.

When Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, looked at the stars in the night, he saw the whole fire hall blazing, blazing fiercely, blazing fiercely all around, a single mass of flame, burning. Seeing this, he thought: "Surely this handsome great renunciant Gautama has not been burned to ashes by the fierce nāga? Alas, the poor man did not heed my words."

Then the Blessed One thought: "Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, together with his following, has treated me with disdain. Come, I shall set my mind in concentration on producing such a miraculous display that my fire overpowers the fierce nāga's fire, yet does not harm his body; and having tamed him, I shall place him in my bowl and make a gift of this kind to Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic."

Then the Blessed One set his mind in concentration on producing such a miraculous display, and he brought it about that his fire overpowered the fierce nāga's fire, yet did not harm the nāga's body. Knowing that the nāga was tamed, he placed him in his bowl and, carrying it, went to where Uruvilvā Kāśyapa was.

Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, saw the Blessed One from far away. Seeing him, he said this to the Blessed One: "Great renunciant, you are still alive, I see!"

"Kāśyapa, I am alive."

"Great renunciant, what is it that you have brought in your bowl?"

"Kāśyapa, the fierce nāga by whose power your fire hall became a blaze of fire, who would give no room to anyone lacking miraculous power: I have tamed him, put him in my bowl and brought him here."

Then Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, thought: "This great renunciant truly has great miraculous power and great might; it is very wonderful. Yet we too are arhats."

Then the Blessed One stayed in the dense grove of the hermitage of Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic. At that time in the hermitage of Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, there were about five hundred brahmin youths who tended the fires, each with three fire altars, so that there were fifteen hundred fire altars. Then, when those brahmin youths wanted to tend the fires, they were unable to kindle them.

Then those brahmin youths went to where Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, was, and when they arrived they said this to Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic: "Master, please be aware: here, when we wish to tend the fires, we cannot manage to kindle them."

Then Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, thought: "Since the great renunciant is staying near me, might this not be his power at work?"

Then Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, went to where the Blessed One was, and when he arrived he said this to the Blessed One: "Great renunciant, please be aware: here, when my five hundred or so brahmin youths wish to tend the fires, they cannot manage to kindle them, and so I think: 'Since the great renunciant is staying near me, might this not be his power at work?'"

"Kāśyapa, do you wish the fires to be kindled?"

"Great renunciant, please kindle them."

Then, through the buddha-power of the buddhas and the divine power of the gods, those fires blazed up of their own accord.

Then Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, thought: "This great renunciant truly has great miraculous powers and great might; it is most wondrous, and yet I too am an arhat."

Then, when those brahmin youths had tended the fires and wished to put them out, they were unable to put them out.

Then those brahmin youths went to where Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, was, and when they arrived they said this to Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic: "Master, please be aware: here, when we have tended the fires and wish to put them out, we cannot manage to put them out."

Then Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, thought: "Since the great renunciant is staying near me, might this not be his power at work?"

Then Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, went to where the Blessed One was, and when he arrived he said this to the Blessed One: "Great renunciant, please be aware: here, when my brahmin youths have tended the fires and wish to put them out, they cannot manage to put them out, and so I think: 'Since the great renunciant is staying near me, might this not be his power at work?'"

"Kāśyapa, shall those fires be put out?"

"Great renunciant, please put them out."

Then, through the buddha-power of the buddhas and the divine power of the gods, those fires died out of their own accord.

Then Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, thought: "This great renunciant truly has great miraculous powers and great might; it is most wondrous, and yet I too am an arhat."

Then again, at another time, when Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, himself wished to tend the fire, he was unable to kindle it; and so on, as above, up to "I too am an arhat." Likewise, from "when he had tended it and wished to put it out" up to "I too am an arhat," it is as above.

Then, at another time, all the fire houses of Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, blazed, blazed up fiercely, blazed up fiercely on every side, and, become a single mass of flame, were burning; and though Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, together with his retinue, and a great crowd of other people as well, strove hard, they were unable to put it out.

Then Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, thought: "Since the great renunciant is staying near me, might this not have come about through his power?"

Then Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, went to where the Blessed One was, and when he arrived he said this to the Blessed One: "Great renunciant, please be aware: great renunciant, here all my fire houses are blazing, blazing up fiercely, blazing up fiercely on every side, and, become a single mass of flame, are burning; and though I with my retinue, and a great crowd of other people as well, strive hard, we cannot manage to put the fire out, and so I think: 'Since the great renunciant is staying near me, might this not have come about through his power?'"

"Kāśyapa, shall that fire be quelled?"

"Great renunciant, please quell it."

Then, through the buddha-power of the buddhas and the divine power of the gods, those fire houses were quelled of their own accord.

Then Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, thought: "This great renunciant truly has great miraculous powers and great might; it is most wondrous, and yet I too am an arhat."

Then the Blessed One stayed in the dense forest by the hermitage of Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic. Then, when night had fallen, the Four Great Kings, of surpassing radiance, came to where the Blessed One was, and when they arrived they bowed their heads at the Blessed One's feet. They were seen as four masses of fire, as it were, blazing, blazing up fiercely, blazing up fiercely on every side, and, become a single mass of flame, burning. And on seeing them he thought: "The great renunciant also tends fire, for in front of him there are four masses of fire, blazing, blazing up fiercely, blazing up fiercely on every side, and, become a single mass of flame, burning."

Then Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, rose early in the morning, went to where the Blessed One was, and when he arrived he said this to the Blessed One: "Great renunciant, please be aware: here, last night, as I was looking at the stars, I saw in front of the great renunciant four masses of fire, blazing, blazing up fiercely, blazing up fiercely on every side, and, become a single mass of flame, burning. And on seeing them I thought: 'The great renunciant also tends fire, for in front of him there are four masses of fire, blazing, blazing up fiercely, blazing up fiercely on every side, and, become a single mass of flame, burning.'"

"Kāśyapa, do I tend fire? I do not tend fire.

The wise turn the mind
Wholly away from the afflictions;
What merit is there in devotedly
Serving fire or water?

Nevertheless, last night, when night had fallen, the Four Great Kings, of surpassing radiance, came to where I was, and when they arrived they bowed their heads at my feet and sat to one side, with bodies like four masses of fire, blazing, blazing up fiercely, blazing up fiercely on every side, and, become a single mass of flame, burning."

Then Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, thought: "This great renunciant truly has great miraculous powers and great might; it is most wondrous, and yet I too am an arhat."

Then, when night had fallen, Śakra, lord of the gods, of surpassing radiance, went to where the Blessed One was, and when he arrived he bowed his head at the Blessed One's feet and sat to one side, with a body like a mass of fire, blazing, blazing up fiercely, blazing up fiercely on every side, become a single mass of flame, yet far greater, far more noble and far more excellent than those four. That same night, Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, as he was looking at the stars, saw in front of the Blessed One a mass of fire blazing; and so on, as above, up to: "This great renunciant truly has great miraculous powers and great might; it is most wondrous, and yet I too am an arhat."

Then, when night had fallen, Brahmā, lord of the Sahā world, of surpassing radiance, went to where the Blessed One was, and when he arrived he bowed his head at the Blessed One's feet and sat to one side, with a body like a mass of fire; as before, from "those very four masses of fire burning" up to "those five great masses of fire, become a single mass of flame, were burning," his body was even far greater, far more noble and far more excellent than those. That same night, Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, as he was looking at the stars, saw in front of the Blessed One a great mass of fire blazing; and so on, as above, up to: "This great renunciant truly has great miraculous powers and great might; it is most wondrous, and yet I too am an arhat."

At that time the occasion came for a seven-day sacrifice to be held at the dwelling of Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic. Then Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, thought: "If the people of Magadha learn that this great renunciant has such great miraculous powers and such great might, they will think of honoring him alone, and it will no longer be so for me. Oh, how good it would be if the great renunciant went elsewhere from here for seven days!"

Then the Blessed One, knowing with his own mind the mind of Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, went elsewhere for seven days. During those seven days Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, received great gain and honor; he had food and drink in great abundance, and a great crowd of people came under his sway.

Then, when the seven-day sacrifice at the dwelling of Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, was over, he thought: "In these seven days I have received great gain and honor; I have had food and drink in great abundance, and a great crowd of people has come under my sway. Oh, how good it would be if the great renunciant came now! I would let him partake of alms from this as well."

Then the Blessed One, knowing with his own mind the mind of Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, went to where Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, was. Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, saw the Blessed One from far off, and on seeing him he said this to the Blessed One: "Great renunciant, have you come?"

"Kāśyapa, I have come."

"Great renunciant, why did you go elsewhere from here for these seven days?"

"Kāśyapa, when the occasion came for the seven-day sacrifice to be held at your dwelling, did you not think: 'If the people of Magadha learn that the great renunciant has such great miraculous powers and such great might, they will think of honoring him alone, and it will no longer be so for me. Oh, how good it would be if the great renunciant went elsewhere from here for seven days!'? Knowing your mind with my own mind, I went elsewhere from here for seven days."

"Then why have you come here?"

"Kāśyapa, when your seven-day sacrifice was over, did you not think: 'In these seven days I have received great gain and honor; I have had food and drink in great abundance, and a great crowd of people has come under my sway. Oh, how good it would be if the great renunciant came! I would let him enjoy this as well'?"

"Great renunciant, it is so."

"Knowing your mind with my own mind, I have come."

"Great renunciant, please enjoy it just as you please."

Then Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, thought: "This great renunciant truly has great miraculous powers and great might; it is most wondrous, and yet I too am an arhat."

Then Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, said this to the Blessed One: "Great renunciant, therefore please stay near me, and I shall provide you with a livelihood in accordance with the Dharma." The Blessed One consented to Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, by remaining silent.

Then Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, prepared food for the Blessed One with his own hands, went to where the Blessed One was, and when he arrived he said this to the Blessed One: "Great renunciant, it is mealtime; great renunciant, please consider that the time has now come."

"Kāśyapa, go on ahead then; I too shall come."

Then the Blessed One, knowing that Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, had not long gone, entered into such a meditative absorption that, as soon as he set his mind in equipoise, he filled his bowl with jambu fruits, full of color, fragrance and flavor, from the jambu tree after which Jambudvīpa is named, and, carrying it, went to the hermitage of Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic; and having arrived, he sat down on the seat that had been prepared.

Then, after that, Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, arrived, and Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, saw the Blessed One seated on the seat prepared in the hermitage. On seeing him he said this: "Great renunciant, have you come?"

"Kāśyapa, I have come."

"Great renunciant, what is in your bowl?"

"Kāśyapa, here, knowing that you had not long gone, I entered into such a meditative absorption that, as soon as I set my mind in equipoise, I filled my bowl with jambu fruits, full of color, fragrance and flavor, from the jambu tree after which Jambudvīpa is named, and came here. If you wish, enjoy them."

"Great renunciant, I ask only that you do as you please."

Then Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, thought: "This great renunciant truly has great miraculous powers and great might; it is most wondrous, and yet I too am an arhat."

As with the jambu tree after which Jambudvīpa is named, so it was likewise with the forest of harītakī myrobalans not very far from it, the forest of bilva trees, the forest of kapittha trees, and the forest of āmalakī myrobalans.

Going to the northern continent of Uttarakuru, he filled his bowl with śāli rice that had been neither plowed nor sown, and, carrying it, went to the hermitage of Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic; and having arrived, he sat down on the seat that had been prepared. Then, after that, Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, arrived, and Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, saw the Blessed One seated on the seat prepared in the hermitage. On seeing him he said this: "Great renunciant, have you come?"

"Kāśyapa, I have come."

"Great renunciant, what is in your bowl?"

"Kāśyapa, here, knowing that you had not long gone, I entered into such a meditative absorption that, as soon as I set my mind in equipoise, I went to the northern continent of Uttarakuru, filled my bowl with grains of śāli rice that had been neither plowed nor sown, and came here. If you wish, enjoy it."

"Great renunciant, I ask only that you do as you please."

Then Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, thought: "This great renunciant truly has great miraculous powers and great might; it is most wondrous, and yet I too am an arhat."

Then the Blessed One, having accepted alms from Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, sat down in another spot and took his meal. Then the Blessed One had need of water for washing. Then Śakra, lord of the gods, knowing that the Blessed One had need of water for washing, went to where the Blessed One was, and when he arrived he struck the ground with his hand in front of the Blessed One. Then a great spring arose and water flowed forth, and with it the Blessed One washed.

Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, while walking about in the hermitage, saw that a great spring had arisen and water was flowing forth. On seeing it he thought: "There was no water here before; where has this water come from now?"

Then Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, went to where the Blessed One was, and when he arrived he said this to the Blessed One: "Great renunciant, please be aware: as I was walking about here in the hermitage, I saw the water of a great spring. And on seeing it I thought: 'There was no water here before; where has this water flowed from now?'"

"Kāśyapa, here, when I had accepted alms from you and was sitting in this spot taking my meal, I had need of water for washing. Then Śakra, lord of the gods, knowing that I had need of water for washing, came to where I was, and when he arrived he struck the ground with his hand in front of me, and from that a great spring arose, and with it I washed. Kāśyapa, this pool is called the Pool Dug by Hand."

Then Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, thought: "This great renunciant truly has great miraculous powers and great might; it is most wondrous, and yet I too am an arhat."

Then the Blessed One, rising from seclusion in the late afternoon, went to where the Pool Dug by Hand was, and when he arrived he laid his robes and Dharma garments at one end of the bank of the Pool Dug by Hand, entered the Pool Dug by Hand and bathed. When he wished to come out of the Pool Dug by Hand, he stretched out his hand toward where an arjuna tree stood. Then a great branch of the arjuna tree bent down, and, taking hold of it, the Blessed One came out of the Pool Dug by Hand.

Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, while walking about in the hermitage, saw a great branch of the arjuna tree bent down on the bank of the Pool Dug by Hand. And on seeing it he thought: "This arjuna branch was not bent down before; who has now bent down this great arjuna branch?"

Then Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, went to where the Blessed One was, and when he arrived he said this to the Blessed One: "Great renunciant, please be aware: as I was walking about here in the hermitage, I saw a great branch of the arjuna tree bent down on the bank of the Pool Dug by Hand. And on seeing it I thought: 'This great arjuna branch was not bent down before; who has bent it down?'"

"Kāśyapa, here, rising from seclusion in the late afternoon, I went to where the bank of the Pool Dug by Hand is, and when I arrived I laid my robes and Dharma garments at one end of the bank of the Pool Dug by Hand, entered the Pool Dug by Hand and bathed my body. Wishing to come out of the Pool Dug by Hand, I stretched out my hand toward where these arjuna trees stand, and then this great arjuna branch bent down; I took hold of it and came out of the Pool Dug by Hand. Kāśyapa, this is called the Arjuna Grasped by Hand."

Then Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, thought: "This great renunciant truly has great miraculous powers and great might; it is most wondrous, and yet I too am an arhat."

At that time the Blessed One had obtained hempen rags from a rubbish heap. Then the Blessed One thought: "Where shall I wash my hempen rags from the rubbish heap?"

Then Śakra, lord of the gods, knowing with his own mind the Blessed One's thought, took a large, long slab of stone from a rocky mountain, went to where the Blessed One was, and when he arrived he said this to the Blessed One: "Blessed One, please wash your hempen rags from the rubbish heap on this." And on it the Blessed One washed the hempen rags from the rubbish heap.

Then the Blessed One thought: "Where shall I spread out my hempen rags from the rubbish heap?" Then Śakra, lord of the gods, knowing with his own mind the Blessed One's thought, carried a large, long slab of stone from a rock, went to where the Blessed One was, and when he arrived he said this to the Blessed One: "Blessed One, please spread out your hempen rags from the rubbish heap on this." And on it the Blessed One spread out the hempen rags from the rubbish heap.

Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, while walking about in the hermitage, saw the large, long slabs of stone. And on seeing them he thought: "These large, long slabs of stone were not here before; who has now carried these large, long slabs of stone here?"

Then Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, went to where the Blessed One was, and when he arrived he said this to the Blessed One: "Great renunciant, please be aware: as I was walking about here in the hermitage, I saw some large, long slabs of stone. And on seeing them I thought: 'These large, long slabs of stone were never here before; who has now brought these large, long slabs of stone here?'"

"Kāśyapa, here I obtained hempen rags from a rubbish heap. Then I thought: 'Where shall I wash the hempen rags from the rubbish heap?' Then Śakra, lord of the gods, knowing my thought with his own mind, took a large, long slab of stone from a rock, came to where I was, and when he arrived he said this to me: 'Blessed One, please wash your hempen rags from the rubbish heap on this.' And on it I washed the hempen rags from the rubbish heap. I thought: 'Where shall I spread out the hempen rags from the rubbish heap?' Then Śakra, lord of the gods, knowing my thought with his own mind, took a large, long slab of stone from a rock, came to where I was, and when he arrived he said this to me: 'Blessed One, please spread out your hempen rags from the rubbish heap on this.' And on it I spread out the hempen rags from the rubbish heap. Kāśyapa, these large, long slabs of stone are called the Ones Brought by the Yakṣa."

Then Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, thought: "This great renunciant truly has great miraculous powers and great might; it is most wondrous, and yet I too am an arhat."

Then the Blessed One stayed in the dense forest by the hermitage of Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic. At that time the Nairañjanā came down in a great flood. Then, where the river had covered the land deeper than a man's height, the Blessed One walked up and down on dry, dusty ground in the open, above the water.

Then Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, thought: "Might not so beautiful a great renunciant have been carried away by the great flood? Come, let me look for the great renunciant." Thinking this, he got into a dugout boat, and as he was looking for the Blessed One, Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, saw the Blessed One, where the river had covered the land deeper than a man's height, walking up and down on dry, dusty ground in the open. And on seeing him he said this to the Blessed One: "Great renunciant, are you alive?"

"Kāśyapa, I am alive."

"Great renunciant, will you get into the dugout boat?"

"Kāśyapa, I will get in."

Then the Blessed One thought: "Come, let me bring about such a miracle that, as soon as I set my mind in equipoise, I enter the dugout boat just as it is on the water." And then the Blessed One brought about such a miracle that, as soon as he set his mind in equipoise, he entered the dugout boat just as it was on the water.

Then Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, thought: "This great renunciant truly has great miraculous powers and great might; it is most wondrous, and yet I too am an arhat."

Then the Blessed One, knowing with his own mind the mind of Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, said this to Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic: "Kāśyapa, matted-hair ascetic, you are not an arhat. You have not even entered upon the way to realize directly the fruit of arhatship, and you do not even know what the path of the arhat is."

Then Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, realizing, "The great renunciant Gautama has known my mind with his own mind," said this to the Blessed One: "Great renunciant, may I go forth and receive full ordination, the state of a monk, in the well-spoken Dharma and Vinaya; I would lead the pure life in the great renunciant's presence."

"Kāśyapa, have you consulted your following?"

"Great renunciant, I have not."

"Kāśyapa, then for now consult your following. For a person of renown like you, well known and well connected, it is good to consult one's following."

Then Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, went to where his own hermitage was, and when he arrived he said to the brahmin youths: "Brahmin youths, know this: brahmin youths, if I lead the pure life in the great renunciant's presence, what will you wise ones do?"

"Master, whatever little we have is all owing to the master. So if the master leads the pure life in the great renunciant's presence, we too shall go forth after the master has gone forth."

"Brahmin youths, in that case throw these hides, bark garments, staffs, water pots and ladles into the river Nairañjanā right now."

"Master, as you command," said those brahmin youths, and they threw the hides, bark garments, staffs, water pots and ladles onto the bank of the river Nairañjanā. Then they went to where Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, was, and when they arrived they said this to Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic: "Master, we have done what we had to do."

Then Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, together with his following of five hundred, went to where the Blessed One was, and when he arrived he said this to the Blessed One: "Great renunciant, I have consulted my following. May I go forth and receive full ordination, the state of a monk, in the well-spoken Dharma and Vinaya; I would lead the pure life in the great renunciant's presence."

"Come, Kāśyapa, lead the pure life."

By those very words those venerable ones went forth, by them received full ordination, and by them attained the state of monks.

At that time the brothers of Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, Nadī Kāśyapa and Gayā Kāśyapa, each with a following of two hundred and fifty, were practicing austerities in a hermitage on the bank at the lower reaches of the river Nairañjanā. The brothers Nadī Kāśyapa and Gayā Kāśyapa saw those hides, bark garments, staffs, water pots and ladles being carried along by the river Nairañjanā. And on seeing them they thought: "Might not some misfortune have befallen our companions in the pure life, from the king, or from robbers, or from fire or water? Come, let us look for our companions in the pure life."

Then the brothers Nadī Kāśyapa and Gayā Kāśyapa, looking for Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, went to where the hermitage of Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, was. At that time the venerable Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, his hair shaved off and wearing the outer robe, was seated with his following of five hundred in the presence of the Blessed One in order to hear the Dharma. Nadī Kāśyapa and Gayā Kāśyapa saw Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, his hair shaved off and wearing the outer robe, seated in the presence of the Blessed One in order to hear the Dharma. And on seeing him they said this to the venerable Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic: "Kāśyapa, is this the best, and is that not the best?"

"Kāśyapas, this is the best; that is not."

Then the brothers Nadī Kāśyapa and Gayā Kāśyapa thought: "The Buddha, the Blessed One, is surely nothing less than supreme, and the Dharma he teaches is surely nothing less than supreme. For now Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, more than a hundred and twenty years since his birth, old, decrepit and advanced in age, honored, revered, esteemed and worshiped by the people of Magadha and venerated as an arhat, has even he twice affirmed his going forth in the great renunciant's presence. Come, let us too lead the pure life in the great renunciant's presence." Thinking this, they said this to the Blessed One: "Great renunciant, may we go forth and receive full ordination, the state of a monk, in the well-spoken Dharma and Vinaya; we would lead the pure life in the great renunciant's presence."

"Have you consulted your following?"

"Great renunciant, we have not."

"Kāśyapas, then for now consult your following. For persons of renown like you, well known and well connected, it is good to consult one's following."

Then the brothers Nadī Kāśyapa and Gayā Kāśyapa went to where their own hermitage was, and when they arrived they said this to the brahmin youths: "Brahmin youths, know this: if we lead the pure life in the great renunciant's presence, what will you wise ones do?"

"Masters, whatever little we have is all owing to the masters. So if the masters lead the pure life in the great renunciant's presence, we too shall go forth after the masters have gone forth."

"Brahmin youths, know that now is the time for it."

Then the brothers Nadī Kāśyapa and Gayā Kāśyapa, each with a following of two hundred and fifty, went to where the Blessed One was, and when they arrived they said this to the Blessed One: "Great renunciant, we have consulted our following. Great renunciant, may we go forth and receive full ordination, the state of a monk, in the well-spoken Dharma and Vinaya; we too would lead the pure life in the great renunciant's presence."

"Come, Kāśyapas, lead the pure life."

By those very words those venerable ones went forth, by them received full ordination, and by them attained the state of monks.

Then the Blessed One, having given the going forth and full ordination to those thousand matted-hair ascetics, stayed at Uruvilvā for as long as he wished, and then set out wandering toward Gayā. Wandering by stages, he reached Gayā, and at the shrine of Gayāśīrṣa he stayed together with a thousand monks, all of them former matted-hair ascetics who had gone forth. Then the Blessed One instructed those thousand monks by means of the three kinds of miracle: the miracle of magical power, the miracle of reading minds, and the miracle of instruction.

Of these, the Blessed One's miracle of magical power was this. The Blessed One, just as he sat in meditative equipoise, vanished from his seat and rose up into the sky above the eastern quarter, and there he displayed the four modes of conduct: he walked, he stood, he sat, and he lay down. He entered into the samādhi of fire, and when he had entered into the samādhi of fire, rays of light of many kinds streamed forth from the body of the Buddha, the Blessed One: blue, yellow, red, white, crimson, and the color of crystal. He displayed the twin miracle: from the lower part of his body he blazed with fire, while from the upper part of his body he poured down streams of cold water; from the upper part of his body he blazed with fire, while from the lower part of his body he poured down streams of cold water. Such was the samādhi he entered. As in the east, so too in the south, the west, and the north. Having displayed the four kinds of the miracle of magical power in the four directions, he withdrew his magical manifestations and sat down on the seat prepared in the midst of the Saṅgha of monks. This, then, was the Blessed One's miracle of magical power.

Of these, the Blessed One's miracle of reading minds was this: "Monks, your mind is like this. Your mentation is like this. Your consciousness is like this. Reflect on this; do not reflect on that. Attend to this; do not attend to that. Abandon this; do not abandon that. Realize this directly with the body, bring it to completion, and abide in it." This was the Blessed One's miracle of reading minds.

Of these, the Blessed One's miracle of instruction was this: "Monks, all this is ablaze. Monks, what is the all that is ablaze? Monks, the eye, forms, eye consciousness, and eye contact are ablaze, and whatever inner feeling arises in dependence on eye contact, whether pleasant, painful, or neither pleasant nor painful, that too is ablaze. Monks, the ear, the nose, the tongue, the body, the mind, mental phenomena, mind consciousness, and mind contact are ablaze, and whatever inner feeling arises in dependence on mind contact, whether pleasant, painful, or neither pleasant nor painful, that too is ablaze. With what are they ablaze? They are ablaze with the fire of desire, the fire of aversion, and the fire of delusion; they are ablaze with suffering: with birth, old age, sickness, death, sorrow, lamentation, pain, unhappiness, and distress. So I declare." This, then, was the Blessed One's miracle of instruction.

When this discourse on the Dharma had been spoken, the minds of those monks, all of whom had formerly been matted-hair ascetics, were liberated from the defilements without grasping. Then the Blessed One stayed at the shrine of Gayāśīrṣa together with the thousand monks, all of whom had formerly been matted-hair ascetics, every one of them an arhat: their defilements exhausted, what was to be done done, their task accomplished, their burden laid down, their own aim attained, the fetters binding them to existence utterly exhausted, and their minds perfectly liberated through right understanding.

The men who lived on King Bimbisāra's wages, while traveling through the country, heard this: "A youth of the Śākyas has been born on the slope of the Himalaya, on the bank of the river Bhāgīrathī, not very far from the hermitage of the sage Kapila. The brahmin readers of marks and the soothsayers have predicted of him: If he becomes a householder and lives in the home, he will become a wheel-turning king, a righteous king, a king of Dharma, victorious to the four ends of the earth, possessed of the seven precious things. His seven precious things will be these: the precious wheel, the precious elephant, the precious horse, the precious jewel, the precious woman, the precious householder, and seventh, the precious minister. He will also have a full thousand sons, heroic and valiant, of supreme bodily form, crushers of enemy armies. He will dwell having conquered this whole great earth, bounded by the ocean, leaving it free of adversaries and free of oppression, without punishment and without the force of weapons, subduing it well in accordance with the Dharma and with impartiality. If he shaves his hair and beard, puts on saffron robes, and out of genuine faith goes forth from home into homelessness, he will become a tathāgata, an arhat, a perfectly complete buddha, whose renown will resound throughout the world."

Having heard this, they went to where King Śreṇya Bimbisāra of Magadha was, and when they had arrived, they said to King Śreṇya Bimbisāra of Magadha: "Please know, Your Majesty, that while we were traveling here through Your Majesty's realm, we heard a prediction," and so on as above, from "a youth of the Śākyas on the slope of the Himalaya" up to "he will become a tathāgata, an arhat, a perfectly complete buddha, whose renown will resound throughout the world." "Your Majesty, be on your guard! He will become more preeminent even than Your Majesty, who is most preeminent."

"Good people, do not speak like this, saying," and so on as above, from "a youth of the Śākyas on the slope of the Himalaya" up to "his renown will resound," "Your Majesty, be on your guard! He will become more preeminent even than Your Majesty, who is most preeminent." "Why is that? Good people," and so on as above, from "a youth of the Śākyas" up to "has been born": "if he becomes a wheel-turning king, I shall be his servant."

"If he shaves his hair and beard, puts on saffron robes, and out of genuine faith goes forth from home into homelessness, and his renown resounds throughout the world as a tathāgata, an arhat, a perfectly complete buddha, I shall declare myself his disciple."

Then King Śreṇya Bimbisāra of Magadha, seated on the upper terrace of his palace, made five wishes, thinking: "Oh, may a Teacher of the world appear in my land, a tathāgata, an arhat, a perfectly complete buddha, perfect in knowledge and conduct, the Sugata, knower of the world, unsurpassed guide of persons to be tamed, teacher of gods and humans, a blessed one; may I approach him in order to see him; when I have approached him, may he teach me the Dharma; may I understand the Dharma he teaches; and when I have understood the Dharma, may he lay down the training, and may I take up that training and abide in it. How good that would be!"

Then the Blessed One said to the monks: "Monks, King Śreṇya Bimbisāra of Magadha, seated on the upper terrace of his palace, made five wishes, thinking: Oh, may a Teacher of the world appear in my land, a tathāgata, an arhat, a perfectly complete buddha," and so on as above.

A certain man who lived on the wages of King Śreṇya Bimbisāra of Magadha, while traveling through the country, heard this: "The youth of the Śākyas who was born on the slope of the Himalaya, on the bank of the river Bhāgīrathī, not very far from the hermitage of the sage Kapila, of whom the brahmin readers of marks and the soothsayers," and so on as above, up to "predicted that his renown would resound throughout the world: he has shaved his hair and beard, put on saffron robes, and out of genuine faith gone forth from home into homelessness, and having fully awakened to unsurpassed, perfectly complete awakening, he is staying at Gayā, at the shrine of Gayāśīrṣa, together with a thousand monks, all of whom were formerly matted-hair ascetics, every one of them an arhat: their defilements exhausted, what was to be done done, their task accomplished, their burden laid down, their own aim attained, the fetters binding them to existence utterly exhausted, and their minds perfectly liberated through right understanding."

Having heard this, he went to where King Śreṇya Bimbisāra of Magadha was, and when he had arrived, he said to King Śreṇya Bimbisāra of Magadha: "Please know, Your Majesty, that while we were traveling here through Your Majesty's realm, we heard that the youth of the Śākyas," and so on as above, up to "his renown will resound throughout the world: he has shaved his hair and beard, put on saffron robes, and out of genuine faith gone forth from home into homelessness, and having fully awakened to unsurpassed, perfectly complete awakening, he is staying at Gayā, at the shrine of Gayā, together with monks, all of whom were formerly matted-hair ascetics, every one of them an arhat, their defilements exhausted," and so on, up to "their minds perfectly liberated through right understanding." "Your Majesty, pay them honor! If you do so, Your Majesty, preeminent as you are, will become still more preeminent."

Then King Śreṇya Bimbisāra of Magadha commanded a man: "Come here, my good man! Go to where the Blessed One is, and when you have arrived, bow your head at the Blessed One's feet in my name and ask: Are you free from ailment? Are you free from affliction? Are you in good health? Do you move about with ease? Are you possessed of strength, well-being, and vigor? Are you living in comfort? And say this as well: Blessed One, I would serve the Blessed One together with the Saṅgha of monks for as long as I live, with clothing and robes, alms food, bedding, medicine as a requisite for the sick, and other necessities. May the Blessed One please come to Rājagṛha."

"As Your Majesty commands," said the man, having listened to King Śreṇya Bimbisāra of Magadha, and he went to where the Blessed One was. When he had arrived, he bowed his head at the Blessed One's feet and sat down to one side. Seated, the man said to the Blessed One: "Venerable One, King Śreṇya Bimbisāra of Magadha bows his head at the Blessed One's feet and asks after your health: Are you free from ailment? Are you free from affliction?" and so on as above, up to "Are you living in comfort?"

"Good man, may you and King Śreṇya Bimbisāra of Magadha be well."

"Venerable One, King Śreṇya Bimbisāra of Magadha sends this message: I would serve the Blessed One together with the Saṅgha of monks for as long as I live, with clothing, robes, alms food, bedding, medicine as a requisite for the sick, and other necessities. May the Blessed One please come to Rājagṛha."

The Blessed One consented to this by remaining silent. Then the man, understanding that the Blessed One had consented by remaining silent, bowed his head at the Blessed One's feet and departed from the Blessed One's presence.

Then the Blessed One, wandering through the land of Magadha together with the great Saṅgha of monks, all of whom were formerly matted-hair ascetics, every one of them an arhat, their defilements exhausted, and so on, up to their minds perfectly liberated through right understanding, went to the Supratiṣṭha shrine of the people of Magadha, where the Yaṣṭivana grove is. Then the Blessed One, having arrived at Supratiṣṭha, stayed at the Supratiṣṭha shrine of the people of Magadha, where the Yaṣṭivana grove is.

King Śreṇya Bimbisāra of Magadha heard that the Blessed One, together with the great Saṅgha of a thousand monks, all of whom were formerly matted-hair ascetics, and so on, up to "their minds perfectly liberated," wandering through the land of Magadha, had gone to the Supratiṣṭha shrine of the people of Magadha, where the Yaṣṭivana grove is, and was staying at the Supratiṣṭha shrine of the people of Magadha, where the Yaṣṭivana grove is. Having heard this, he mounted his fine chariot and, with great royal might and great royal splendor, together with some hundred thousand horses and chariots and many hundreds of attendants, set out from Rājagṛha and went into the presence of the Blessed One in order to see the Blessed One and to pay him homage. As he went, the wheels of his chariot sank into the ground. He thought, "Can it be that I have done some faulty deed?"

The gods said to him, "Great king, you have not done any faulty deed, but those who have shared in deeds with you are sitting in prison. Release them."

The king said, "If that is so, let them be released," and the king had the prisoners released.

Then, as the king set out again, his crown struck against the gate. Again he thought, "Without a doubt I have done some faulty deed, and that is why this further ill omen has befallen me, my crown striking against the gate."

Those gods said again, "Great king, you have not done any faulty deed, but of those who have shared in deeds with you, some have assembled, while others are in such and such other districts, carrying on their work. Summon them."

The king summoned all the people living in those lands. Then King Bimbisāra, together with twelve thousand chariots, eighteen thousand attendants and troops of horsemen, and many hundreds of thousands of brahmins and householders of Magadha, set out from Rājagṛha and went into the presence of the Blessed One in order to see the Blessed One and to pay him homage. He rode as far as the ground allowed for riding, then dismounted and entered the park on foot alone. From the point at which King Śreṇya Bimbisāra of Magadha first saw the Blessed One, he took off the five royal insignia: the turban, the parasol, the sword, the jeweled yak-tail whisk, and the colorful sandals.

Then King Śreṇya Bimbisāra of Magadha, having set aside the five royal insignia, went to where the Blessed One was. When he had arrived, he arranged his upper robe over one shoulder, bowed with palms joined toward the Blessed One, and announced his own name three times: "Venerable One, I am King Śreṇya Bimbisāra of Magadha. Venerable One, I am King Śreṇya Bimbisāra of Magadha. I am the king."

"So it is, king, so it is. Great king, you are King Śreṇya Bimbisāra of Magadha. Great king, you are King Śreṇya Bimbisāra of Magadha. You are the king. Great king, sit down on your seat as you see fit."

Then King Śreṇya Bimbisāra of Magadha bowed his head at the Blessed One's feet and sat down to one side. Some of the brahmins and householders of Magadha bowed their heads at the Blessed One's feet and sat down to one side. Some exchanged with the Blessed One various kinds of talk that bring delight and satisfaction and sat down to one side. Some bowed with palms joined toward the Blessed One and sat down. Some, seeing the Blessed One from far away, sat down to one side in silence.

At that time the venerable Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, together with his retinue, had also gathered there and was seated. Then the brahmins and householders of Magadha thought: "Does the great ascetic practice the holy life under Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, or does Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, practice the holy life under the great ascetic?"

Then the Blessed One, knowing with his mind the thoughts of the brahmins and householders of Magadha, put a question in verse to the venerable Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic:

"You who dwelt at Uruvilvā, what have you seen here,
that you have abandoned the fire and practice these observances?
How is it that you have given up the worship of fire?
Kāśyapa, tell me what this means."

Uruvilvā Kāśyapa replied:

"Here some speak only of food and drink, and likewise of tastes,
of sense pleasures, and of women.
I clearly saw these as stains of the aggregates;
therefore I take no delight in sacrifice, nor in burnt offerings."

"If your mind takes no delight
in sense pleasures, in food and drink, nor likewise in tastes,
then in what, in the world of gods and humans,
does your mind delight? Kāśyapa, tell me, since I ask."

"Where there are no aggregates, peaceful, where there is nothing at all,
free of clinging to craving for pleasures, without becoming otherwise:
I have seen that supreme state that is not led by another;
therefore I take no delight in sacrifice, nor in burnt offerings.

Not having seen the supreme, unfailing state,
I thought I would be freed by sacrifices, observances and fires;
my mind was deluded,
and, become blind, I saw only what follows on birth and death.

Now that I have seen and accepted the unconditioned state
well proclaimed by the supreme and foremost protector,
you, O sage Gautama, are the guide;
the great multitude, striving for the goal, enters the truth."

The Blessed One said:

"What you have understood, you have understood well;
you have not thought amiss.
Of the teachings that have been set forth,
you have taken up the one that is foremost.
Kāśyapa, stir your assembly to a sense of urgency."

Then the venerable Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, given leave by the Blessed One, just as he sat with his mind in equipoise, vanished from his seat.

He rose up into the sky above the eastern quarter and displayed the four modes of conduct. As before, he displayed the four kinds of the miracle of magical power in the four directions, and then withdrew his magical manifestations. He arranged his upper robe over one shoulder, set his right knee on the ground, bowed with palms joined toward the Blessed One, and said to the Blessed One: "The Blessed One is my Teacher; I am the Blessed One's disciple. The Blessed One is my Teacher; I am the Blessed One's disciple."

"So it is, Kāśyapa, so it is. Kāśyapa, I am your Teacher; you are my disciple. Kāśyapa, I am your Teacher; you are my disciple. Kāśyapa, sit down on your seat as you see fit."

Then the venerable Uruvilvā Kāśyapa bowed his head at the Blessed One's feet and sat down to one side. Then the brahmins and householders of Magadha thought: "The great ascetic does not practice the holy life under Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic; rather, it is Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, the matted-hair ascetic, who practices the holy life under the great ascetic."

Then the Blessed One said to King Śreṇya Bimbisāra of Magadha: "Great king, form has arising, and it has passing away; its arising and passing away should be understood. Great king, feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness have arising, and they have passing away; their arising and passing away should be understood. Great king, a son of good family, having understood that it is the nature of form to arise and pass away, fully understands form. Great king, a son of good family, having understood that it is the nature of feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness to arise and pass away, fully understands consciousness. Great king, when a son of good family fully understands form, he does not take form to be a self or to belong to a self; he does not grasp it, does not dwell on it, and does not cling to it. Great king, when a son of good family fully understands feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness, he does not take them to be a self or to belong to a self; he does not grasp them, does not dwell on them, and does not cling to them. Great king, when a son of good family does not take form to be a self or to belong to a self, does not grasp it, does not dwell on it, and does not cling to it, that is declared to be nirvāṇa, immeasurable and beyond count. Great king, when a son of good family does not take feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness to be a self or to belong to a self, does not grasp them, does not dwell on them, and does not cling to them, that is declared to be nirvāṇa, immeasurable and beyond count."

Then the brahmins and householders of Magadha thought: "If form is not the self, and feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness are not the self, then who will it be: the self, the sentient being, the life principle, the one who is born, the nourisher, the person, the one born of Manu, the son of Manu, the agent, the instigator, the knower, the cognizer, the one who rouses, the one who instigates, the speaker, the declarer, the feeler, the experiencer, which never came into being and will not come into being and yet exists even now, by which one experiences, in this place and that, the ripening of the virtuous and evil deeds one has done, and which casts off these aggregates and links up with other aggregates?"

Then the Blessed One, knowing with his mind the thoughts of the brahmins and householders of Magadha, said to the monks: "Monks, 'self, self' is something that foolish, unlearned ordinary people fall into by following a mere designation. Here, monks, there is no self and nothing belonging to a self. When there is arising, this suffering arises; when there is ceasing, this suffering ceases. When there is arising, formations arise; when there is ceasing, formations cease. In dependence on that cause and that condition, the stream of formations of sentient beings goes on. Monks, the Tathāgata, having realized the linking of formations from one life to the next, designates the death and birth of sentient beings. Monks, with the divine eye, purified and surpassing that of humans, I know the death of sentient beings," and so on as above, up to "they are born among the gods in the happy destinies, in the heavenly world."

"Monks, since I know thus and see thus, I do not say," as above, from "self" or "sentient being" up to "experiencer," "this is that which never came into being, will not come into being, and does not exist even now, by which one experiences, in this place and that, the ripening of the evil deeds one has done, and which casts off these aggregates and links up with other aggregates," except as a conventional designation for phenomena.

That conventional designation for phenomena is this: when this exists, that comes to be; from the arising of this, that arises. That is to say: conditioned by ignorance, formations; conditioned by formations, consciousness; conditioned by consciousness, name and form; conditioned by name and form, the six sense sources; conditioned by the six sense sources, contact; conditioned by contact, feeling; conditioned by feeling, craving; conditioned by craving, grasping; conditioned by grasping, becoming; conditioned by becoming, birth; and conditioned by birth arise old age and death, sorrow, lamentation, pain, unhappiness, and distress. Thus this entire great mass of suffering comes about.

That is to say: when this does not exist, that does not come to be; from the cessation of this, that ceases. That is to say: from the cessation of ignorance, formations cease; from the cessation of formations, consciousness ceases; from the cessation of consciousness, name and form cease; from the cessation of name and form, the six sense sources cease; from the cessation of the six sense sources, contact ceases; from the cessation of contact, feeling ceases; from the cessation of feeling, craving ceases; from the cessation of craving, grasping ceases; from the cessation of grasping, becoming ceases; from the cessation of becoming, birth ceases; and from the cessation of birth, old age and death, sorrow, lamentation, pain, unhappiness, and distress cease. In this way this entire great mass of suffering ceases.

"Monks, formations are suffering; nirvāṇa is peace. From the origination of the cause, suffering originates, and from the cessation of the cause, suffering ceases. This very cessation, in which, because the stream has been cut, nothing further arises, and in which there is no linking to a new life, is the end of suffering. Monks, what then is complete nirvāṇa? It is the cessation of suffering with nothing else remaining: that is utter pacification, that is becoming cool, that is the going down to rest. Monks, this is the state of peace: namely, the abandonment of all aggregates, the exhaustion of craving, freedom from desire, cessation, nirvāṇa."

Then the Blessed One spoke once more to King Śreṇya Bimbisāra of Magadha: "Great king, what do you think? Is form permanent or impermanent?"

"Venerable One, it is impermanent."

"Is what is impermanent suffering, or is it not suffering?"

"Venerable One, it is suffering."

"Would a learned noble disciple take what is impermanent, suffering, and subject to change as a self, thinking, This is I, I am this, this is my self?"

"No, Venerable One."

"Great king, what do you think? Are feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness permanent or impermanent?"

"Venerable One, they are impermanent."

"Is what is impermanent suffering, or is it not suffering?"

"Venerable One, it is suffering."

"Would a learned noble disciple take what is impermanent, suffering, and subject to change as a self, thinking, This is I, I am this, this is my self?"

"No, Venerable One."

"Therefore, great king, whatever form there may be, whether past, future, or present, inner or outer, inferior or excellent, far or near, all of it you should see as it really is with correct wisdom, thus: This is not I, I am not this, this is not my self. Therefore, great king, whatever feeling, perception, or formations there may be, and whatever consciousness there may be, whether past, future, or present, inner or outer, coarse or subtle, inferior or excellent, far or near, all of it you should see as it really is with correct wisdom, thus: This is not I, I am not this, this is not my self.

"Great king, a learned noble disciple who sees in this way becomes disenchanted with form, and becomes disenchanted with feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness. Being disenchanted, he becomes free from desire. Being free from desire, he is liberated. Being liberated, he sees the knowledge of liberation: My birth is exhausted, the holy life has been lived, what was to be done has been done, I know no further existence beyond this."

When this discourse on the Dharma had been spoken, there arose in King Śreṇya Bimbisāra of Magadha, in eighty thousand gods, and in many hundreds of thousands of brahmins and householders of Magadha, the Dharma eye regarding phenomena, free of dust and free of stain.

Then King Śreṇya Bimbisāra of Magadha, having seen the Dharma, attained the Dharma, understood the Dharma, and plumbed the depths of the Dharma; having crossed beyond hesitation and crossed beyond doubt; no longer depending on others and no longer led by others; having attained fearlessness with regard to the teachings taught by the Teacher, rose from his seat, arranged his upper robe over one shoulder, bowed with palms joined toward the Blessed One, and said to the Blessed One: "Venerable One, I have gone beyond; I have truly gone beyond. Venerable One, I go for refuge to the Blessed One, to the Dharma, and to the Saṅgha of monks; please accept me as a layman. From this day onward, for as long as I live, staking even my life, I go for refuge with full faith. Blessed One, I would serve the Blessed One together with the Saṅgha of monks for as long as I live, with clothing and robes, alms food, bedding, and medicine and requisites for the sick. May the Blessed One please come to Rājagṛha."

The Blessed One consented to King Śreṇya Bimbisāra of Magadha, of Rājagṛha, by remaining silent. Then King Śreṇya Bimbisāra of Magadha, understanding that the Blessed One had consented by remaining silent, bowed his head at the Blessed One's feet and departed from the Blessed One's presence.

Then the Blessed One, wandering on from Magadha, came to Rājagṛha, and at Rājagṛha he stayed at the Kalandakanivāpa.

At that time in Rājagṛha there were non-Buddhists (tīrthikas) known as the followers of Sañjayin, and not long after they had come into being, their teacher passed away. Among them were two, called Upatiṣya and Kolita, who gathered and led the assembly. While they were leading it, the two made this pledge: "Whichever of us first finds the deathless shall tell the other."

Then, in the morning, the venerable Aśvajit put on his lower robe, took up his alms bowl and his Dharma robe, and entered Rājagṛha for alms, graceful in his going and his returning, in his glancing ahead and his looking about, in drawing in and stretching out his limbs, and in the way he carried his outer robe, his Dharma robe and his alms bowl.

At that time the wandering mendicant (parivrājaka) Upatiṣya had come into the town on some business in Rājagṛha, and from a long way off he saw the venerable Aśvajit going about Rājagṛha for alms, graceful in his going and his returning, in his glancing ahead and his looking about, in drawing in and stretching out his limbs, and in the way he carried his outer robe, his Dharma robe and his alms bowl. Seeing him, he thought: "Of all the renunciants who have come to Rājagṛha and stay here, never before have I seen any with such deportment as this renunciant. Let me go up to him and ask, 'Monk, who is your teacher? For whose sake did you go forth? In whose Dharma do you delight?'" With this in mind, he waited in a narrow lane, pacing back and forth most of the time.

Then the venerable Aśvajit came to where the wandering mendicant Upatiṣya was. And the wandering mendicant Upatiṣya said this to the venerable Aśvajit: "Monk, who is your teacher? For whose sake did you go forth? In whose Dharma do you delight?"

"Venerable one, there is a son of the Śākyas, the ascetic Gautama, who shaved off his hair and beard from the Śākya clan, put on saffron robes, and out of genuine faith went forth from home into homelessness. Having fully awakened to unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening, that Blessed One is my teacher. It is for his sake that I went forth, and it is in his Dharma that I delight."

"Monk, then teach me the Dharma."

"Venerable one, I am young and newly gone forth, so it is not easy for me to set out in full the teaching of that Blessed One, the Tathāgata, the arhat, the perfectly and completely awakened Buddha. Nevertheless, I shall teach its meaning in this way."

"Monk, it is the meaning I need. What use to me are a great many words? Teach me the meaning, whether much or little."

Then the venerable Aśvajit spoke this verse:

Of those phenomena that arise from causes,
their cause, and also what their cessation is,
the Tathāgata himself has declared;
so speaks the great ascetic.

When this discourse on the Dharma had been spoken, the stainless, immaculate eye of Dharma regarding phenomena arose in the wandering mendicant Upatiṣya. Then the wandering mendicant Upatiṣya, having seen the Dharma, attained the Dharma, understood the Dharma, and fathomed the depths of the Dharma; having crossed beyond doubt and crossed beyond uncertainty; no longer relying on others, no longer led by others, and having gained confidence in the teachings the Teacher had taught, straightened his upper body, stretched out his right hand, bowed with palms joined toward where the venerable Aśvajit was, and spoke this verse:

This is the Dharma of the protectors.
Through countless tens of millions of eons
unseen before and unheard,
this state, deathless and free of sorrow,
has here been realized.

"Venerable one, where is that Blessed One staying now?"

"Venerable one, right here in Rājagṛha, at the Kalandakanivāpa in the Bamboo Grove."

Then the wandering mendicant Upatiṣya praised and rejoiced in what the venerable Aśvajit had said, bowed his head at the venerable Aśvajit's feet, circled the venerable Aśvajit three times, and departed, going to where the wandering mendicant Kolita was.

The wandering mendicant Kolita saw the wandering mendicant Upatiṣya from a long way off, and seeing him, he said this to the wandering mendicant Upatiṣya: "Venerable one, your senses are utterly clear, the color of your face is pure, and the color of your skin is radiant white. Have you found the deathless?"

"Venerable one, I have found it."

"Venerable one, then teach me the Dharma."

Then the wandering mendicant Upatiṣya spoke this verse:

Of those phenomena that arise from causes,
their cause, and what their cessation is,
the Tathāgata himself has declared;
so speaks the great ascetic.

"Venerable one, say it once more."

"Venerable one, I shall say it." "Of those phenomena that arise from causes," and so on, as before.

When this discourse on the Dharma had been spoken, the stainless, immaculate eye of Dharma regarding phenomena arose in the wandering mendicant Kolita. Then the wandering mendicant Kolita, having seen the Dharma, attained the Dharma, understood the Dharma, and fathomed the depths of the Dharma; having crossed beyond doubt and crossed beyond uncertainty; no longer relying on others, no longer led by others, and having gained confidence in the teachings the Teacher had taught, straightened his upper body, stretched out his right hand, bowed with palms joined toward where the wandering mendicant Upatiṣya was, and spoke this verse:

This is the Dharma of the protectors.
Through countless tens of millions of eons
unseen before and unheard,
this state, deathless and free of sorrow,
has here been realized.

"Venerable one, where is that Blessed One staying now?"

"Venerable one, right here in Rājagṛha, at the Kalandakanivāpa in the Bamboo Grove."

"Venerable one, come! Let us practice the holy life in the presence of that Blessed One."

"Venerable one, first we should consider our following. For people like us, who are known and recognized among the people, it is right to consider our following."

Then the two wandering mendicants, Upatiṣya and Kolita, said to the young brahmins, "Young brahmins, know this: the two of us are going to practice the holy life in the presence of the Blessed One. What will you do?"

"Our two preceptors, whatever little understanding we have, all of it we have gained through relying on you two preceptors. If the two preceptors are to practice the holy life in the presence of the Blessed One, then we too shall go forth after the two preceptors have gone forth."

"Young brahmins, then know that the time has come for it."

Then the two wandering mendicants, Upatiṣya and Kolita, together with their following of two hundred and fifty, went to where the Blessed One was. At that time the Blessed One was seated teaching the Dharma in the midst of an assembly of many hundreds of monks. From a long way off the Blessed One saw the two wandering mendicants, Upatiṣya and Kolita, and seeing them, he said to the monks, "Monks, do you see those two, Upatiṣya and Kolita, who gather their company of companions, who lead that company, and who come at the head of that company?"

"Venerable One, we see them."

"These two will become my pair of śrāvakas, the foremost pair, the excellent pair. One will become foremost among those who possess miraculous powers, and the other will become foremost among those who possess wisdom."

Then a monk spoke this verse:

The Teacher, whose sphere of thought is boundless,
lord among men, who has reached the end of knowledge,
the Blessed One, who has made an end of existence,
saw the companions Upatiṣya and Kolita
coming thus, before they had reached the Bamboo Grove,
and in the midst of the excellent assembly
he declared of those two, "These two
will become the foremost pair of my śrāvakas."
He foretold that one would be foremost of those with miraculous powers,
and the other foremost of those with wisdom.

Then the two wandering mendicants, Upatiṣya and Kolita, went to where the Blessed One was, and having arrived, they bowed their heads at the Blessed One's feet and said to the Blessed One, "Venerable One, if you grant us going forth in the well-spoken Dharma and Vinaya, and full ordination into the state of a monk, we shall practice the holy life in the presence of the Blessed One."

The Blessed One gave the two of them the going forth with the words "Come, monk," declaring, "Come, monks, practice the holy life." And immediately upon the Blessed One's words, their heads were shaven and they were clothed in the outer robe, holding alms bowls in their hands, with hair and beards like seven days' growth, and they stood with the deportment of monks who had been fully ordained for a hundred years.

It is further said:

When the Tathāgata declared, "Come,"
at once their bodies were shaven and clad in the outer robe,
and their senses came to rest in perfect peace;
by the Buddha's intention their bodily forms were clothed.

Then a great many monks put on their lower robes in the morning, took up their alms bowls and Dharma robes, and entered Rājagṛha for alms. Then those people of Rājagṛha who had gone forth from the non-Buddhist (tīrthika) community of Sañjayin and had received full ordination not long before, being displeased and dissatisfied, saw those various monks and, mocking them, spoke this verse:

The Buddha has come to Rājagṛha,
the foremost city of the Magadhans;
having led away all of Sañjayin's people,
whom will he lead away from now on?

At this the monks became ashamed and unhappy, and lost their confidence.

Then the great many monks, having gone about Rājagṛha for alms and having taken their meal, since they do not receive alms in the afternoon, put away their alms bowls and Dharma robes, washed their feet, and, pained and unhappy, went to where the Blessed One was. Having arrived, they bowed their heads at the Blessed One's feet and sat down to one side. Seated to one side, the great many monks said this to the Blessed One:

"Venerable One, here a great many of us monks put on our lower robes in the morning, took up our alms bowls and Dharma robes, and entered Rājagṛha for alms. Then those people of Rājagṛha who had gone forth from the non-Buddhist community of Sañjayin and had received full ordination not long before, being displeased and dissatisfied, saw those various monks and, mocking them, spoke this verse:

The Buddha has come to Rājagṛha,
the foremost city of the Magadhans;
having led away all of Sañjayin's people,
whom will he lead away from now on?

Saying this, they made us ashamed and unhappy, and we lost our confidence."

The Blessed One said, "Monks, to those people of Rājagṛha who speak in this way, say this:

Here the great heroes, the Sugatas,
lead by means of the true Dharma.
Since they lead by means of the Dharma,
what wise person would not accept it?"

When this is said, whatever conceit or boldness those people of Rājagṛha who speak in this way have will vanish entirely.

Then a great many monks put on their lower robes in the morning, took up their alms bowls and Dharma robes, and entered Rājagṛha for alms. Then those people of Rājagṛha who had gone forth from the non-Buddhist community of Sañjayin and had received full ordination not long before, being displeased and dissatisfied, saw those various monks and, mocking them, spoke this verse:

The Buddha has come to Rājagṛha,
the foremost city of the Magadhans;
having led away all of Sañjayin's people,
whom will he lead away from now on?

Then a monk spoke this verse:

Here the great heroes, the Sugatas,
lead by means of the true Dharma.
Since they lead by means of the Dharma,
what wise person would not accept it?

When he had said this, whatever conceit or boldness those people of Rājagṛha who spoke in this way had vanished entirely.

Then the Buddha, the Blessed One, was staying in Śrāvastī, in Prince Jeta's Grove, the park of Anāthapiṇḍada. King Prasenajit of Kosala sent a letter to King Śuddhodana: "Your Majesty, please rejoice! Your son has attained the deathless and is satisfying beings with the deathless." When King Śuddhodana heard this message, he sent a messenger, but that messenger went forth right there; and whatever messengers he sent, every one of them went forth right there in the well-spoken Dharma and Vinaya.

King Śuddhodana sat with his cheek resting on his hand, brooding, thinking, "Whatever messengers I send, every one of them goes forth right there in the well-spoken Dharma and Vinaya." Udāyin saw him and said, "Your Majesty, why do you sit with your face resting on your hand, so unhappy?"

He replied, "Udāyin, when Prince Sarvārthasiddha was enjoying his pleasures, whenever I sent men to carry letters, they would come back to me and say, 'Prince Sarvārthasiddha is staying here and there.' But now, when I send men to carry letters, not one of them comes back bearing a letter. How could I not brood?"

Udāyin said, "Your Majesty, I shall go, deliver your message, and come back."

King Śuddhodana said, "Udāyin, whoever goes there sees the excellence of his teaching and stays right there. So if you go too, there is every reason that you will stay right there as well."

Udāyin said, "Your Majesty, I will come back."

Then King Śuddhodana himself wrote a letter:

From the time you entered the womb
I cared for you and raised you well.
While the fire of the afflictions blazes fiercely,
if others take refuge in the tree of the Victor,
its branches and shoots, your disciples,
will spread and flourish,
and others will rejoice in happiness,
while I am the one whose lot is suffering.

Moreover, when you were like a tender sapling, I thought, "This is my son," and nurtured you from childhood.
Now that the people of other lands have come to enjoy the fruit of my only son,
you give no fruit of your good qualities to me: you have no compassion for me.

When you were first born,
you made this vow:
"Having accomplished my own aim,
I shall greatly benefit my kin."
Now that you have found that peace,
take me and the host of our kin
into your loving care,
and please resolve to come.

Udāyin took the letter carrying this message and set out, and in due course he reached Śrāvastī. Having rested from the weariness of the road, he went to where the Blessed One was, and having arrived, he offered the letter to the Blessed One, saying, "This is a letter of request from King Śuddhodana." The Blessed One took it himself and read it. Having read it, he set the letter aside, and Udāyin said, "Blessed One, what is it? Will you not go to Kapilavastu?"

The Blessed One said, "Udāyin, I will go."

Udāyin had formerly been especially intimate with the Blessed One, and out of that former intimacy he said, "Blessed One, if you will not go, I shall lead you there by force."

Then, on this occasion, the Blessed One spoke in verse:

The Buddha who is not attached to the queen of sense objects,
whom no craving whatever can lead away,
whose range is boundless:
he has no footing, so by what footing would you lead him?

The Buddha who is not attached to the queen of sense objects,
whom no craving whatever can lead away,
whose prowess is boundless:
he has no footing, so by what footing would you lead him?

He whose conquest, once won, is never undone,
and in the world there is nothing left unconquered,
the Buddha whose range is boundless:
he has no footing, so by what footing would you lead him?

Unable to give an answer, Udāyin said, "Blessed One, let me go and inform King Śuddhodana."

"Udāyin, a messenger of the Buddha is not like this."

Udāyin asked, "Blessed One, what is he like?"

"He is one who has gone forth, so you too go forth."

"Blessed One, I promised King Śuddhodana that I would come back."

The Blessed One said, "Udāyin, go forth, and then do as you promised."

For in the past, when the Blessed One was a bodhisattva, he had never once turned back any word in accord with the Dharma spoken by his father, his mother, his preceptor, his teacher, his guru, or those in the place of a guru. So Udāyin said, "Blessed One, if that is so, I shall go forth."

Then the Blessed One said to the venerable Śāriputra, "Śāriputra, give this Udāyin the going forth, for his lasting welfare, benefit and happiness."
