"Venerable One, as you command," said the venerable Śāriputra, heeding the Blessed One. He gave Udāyin the going forth and full ordination, explained to him in detail the rules of conduct, and said, "Udāyin, this is your duty: from today onward you must not sleep in the same house as a woman."

"Venerable one, as you command," said Udāyin. He bowed his head at the venerable Śāriputra's feet and left, and went to where the Blessed One was. Having arrived, he bowed his head at the Blessed One's feet and said this to the Blessed One: "Blessed One, I am going to Kapilavastu."

The Blessed One said, "Udāyin, go. Do not enter the royal palace straightaway; stay at the gate and say, 'A Śākya monk has come.' If they say, 'Please come in,' then you may enter. Once inside, if they say, 'Are there other Śākya monks too?' you should say, 'There are.' If they ask, 'Does Prince Sarvārthasiddha also look like this, and dress like this?' you should say, 'He looks like this, and dresses like this.' You must not stay in the royal palace. If they ask, 'Does Prince Sarvārthasiddha also not stay in a royal palace?' you should say, 'He does not.' If they ask, 'Where does he stay?' you should say, 'In the wilderness, or in a monastery.' If they ask, 'What is a monastery like?' you should draw the layout of Prince Jeta's Grove and show it to them. If they ask, 'Will Prince Sarvārthasiddha come?' you should say, 'He will come.'"

"If they ask, 'When will he come?' you should say, 'When seven days have passed.'"

Udāyin said, "Blessed One, I am going."

The Blessed One said, "Go.

Lords of the earth accomplish things by their word;
gods accomplish them by thinking of them;
and likewise, for those who meditate,
all is accomplished by a mere arising of the mind."

The Blessed One blessed the venerable Udāyin so that in that very instant he went to the city of Kapilavastu. The venerable Udāyin, in whom intense faith in the Blessed One had arisen, stood at the gate of the royal palace and said, "Sirs, please tell the king that a Śākya monk has come."

They said, "What, are there other Śākya monks as well?"

"Sirs, there are."

They said to the king, "A Śākya monk has come."

The king said, "Let him in."

When he had come in, King Śuddhodana saw him and said, "Udāyin, have you gone forth?"

"Your Majesty, I have gone forth."

"Did you not say that you would come back? What is this?"

Udāyin said, "Your Majesty, the Blessed One declared, 'A messenger of the Buddha is not like this,' and so I went forth; but because the Dharma and Vinaya are well spoken, I also went forth out of my own faith."

The king embraced him with all his limbs and had him sit on a seat already prepared. For his sake he had water strained and fruit prepared, made it clean, and had him eat. The venerable Udāyin, supremely peaceful and abiding in discipline, partook of it according to the procedure he had been taught. The king observed him, and, filled with wonder, said, "Udāyin, is Prince Sarvārthasiddha also as peaceful as this?"

Udāyin said, "Your Majesty, my discipline and peace are only a tiny fraction. To give a simile, it is like Mount Meru compared to a mustard seed, like the ocean compared to the water in a hoofprint, like the sun compared to a firefly, like the sky compared to the hollow in a lotus stalk."

King Śuddhodana said, "Udāyin, does Prince Sarvārthasiddha also look like this, and dress like this?"

"Your Majesty, he looks like this, and dresses like this as well."

King Śuddhodana fell to the ground in a faint. When they had sprinkled him with plenty of water, he came to again and said, "Udāyin, will Prince Sarvārthasiddha come?"

Udāyin said, "Your Majesty, he will come."

"When?"

"When seven days have passed."

King Śuddhodana commanded his ministers, "Sirs, Prince Sarvārthasiddha is going to come. Clean the royal residence and the palace."

Udāyin said, "The Blessed One does not stay in palaces."

The king said, "Where does he stay?"

Udāyin said, "In the wilderness, or in a monastery."

King Śuddhodana said, "Udāyin, what is a monastery like?"

The venerable Udāyin drew the layout of Prince Jeta's Grove as a picture and showed it to him. Then the king said to his ministers, "Sirs, in the Nyagrodha Park, have sixteen great monastery buildings and sixty small dwellings built after the design of Prince Jeta's Grove."

"Your Majesty, as you command," said the ministers, heeding King Śuddhodana, and in the Nyagrodha Park they had sixteen great monastery buildings and sixty small dwellings built after the design of Prince Jeta's Grove.

Then the Blessed One said this to the venerable Mahāmaudgalyāyana: "Maudgalyāyana, go and instruct the monks: 'The Blessed One is going to Kapilavastu, where father and son will meet. Whichever of you would like to see it, take up your robes.'"

"Venerable One, as you command," said the venerable Mahāmaudgalyāyana, heeding the Blessed One, and he said to the monks, "Venerable ones, the Blessed One has declared: 'The Blessed One is going to Kapilavastu, where father and son will meet. Whichever of you would like to see it, take up your robes.'"

Then the Blessed One, disciplined and with a disciplined following, peaceful and with a peaceful following, liberated and with a liberated following, consoled and with a consoled following, thoroughly disciplined and with a thoroughly disciplined following, an arhat with a following of arhats, free of desire and with a following free of desire, beautiful and with a beautiful following; like a bull surrounded by a herd of cattle, like a great elephant surrounded by a herd of young elephants, like a lion surrounded by a pack of fanged beasts, like a king of geese surrounded by a flock of geese, like a garuḍa surrounded by a flock of birds, like a brahmin surrounded by a group of students, like a skilled physician surrounded by a crowd of patients, like a warrior surrounded by his troops, like a guide surrounded by a band of travelers, like a caravan leader surrounded by a company of merchants, like a regional king surrounded by a council of ministers, like a wheel-turning king surrounded by his thousand sons, like the moon surrounded by the host of constellations, like the sun surrounded by its thousand rays, like Dhṛtarāṣṭra surrounded by a host of gandharvas, like Virūḍhaka surrounded by a host of kumbhāṇḍas, like Virūpākṣa surrounded by a host of nāgas, like Vaiśravaṇa surrounded by a host of yakṣas, like Vemacitrin surrounded by a host of asuras, like Śakra surrounded by the host of the Thirty-Three, like Brahmā surrounded by the host of the Brahmakāyika gods; like the ocean, unshaken; like a cloud, laden with rain; like a noble elephant, free of arrogance; his senses well tamed, so that his deportment was never disturbed; his body thoroughly adorned with the thirty-two marks of a great being and beautified by the eighty excellent minor marks; his body adorned with a halo a fathom wide, its radiance surpassing a thousand rays of the sun; altogether beautiful, like a moving mountain of jewels; possessed of the ten powers, the four fearlessnesses, the three unmixed applications of mindfulness, and great compassion, came together with a great Saṅgha of monks to the Rohitaka River.

King Śuddhodana heard that Prince Sarvārthasiddha had come to the Rohitaka River.

When he heard this, King Śuddhodana had the city of Kapilavastu cleared of stones, pebbles and gravel and sprinkled with sandalwood water. He had bowls of exquisitely fragrant incense set out and many silk streamers hung up, and the whole road from the Rohitaka River to the Nyagrodha Park was made exceedingly beautiful. And across a broad, spacious area he had colorful seats arranged.

Meanwhile, at the Rohitaka River, some monks were washing their hands, some were discarding their tooth sticks, and some were bathing. The Śākyas living in Kapilavastu heard that Prince Sarvārthasiddha had come. When they heard it, some, urged on by their former roots of virtue, and some, filled with curiosity, all came out from the Nyagrodha Park, thinking, "Let us see whether the father will bow at the feet of the son, or the son at the feet of the father."

Then the Blessed One thought, "If I go to Kapilavastu on foot, the proud Śākyas will lose faith, saying, 'Alas! Prince Sarvārthasiddha went to the forest of austerities, and all he achieved was an empty belly, look! Such a one ought to come through the sky above, attended by many hundreds of thousands; yet after practicing hundreds of thousands of austerities for so long and attaining the deathless, look, he comes on foot!'"

Then the Blessed One composed his mind in meditation, rose up into the sky in the east, and displayed the four kinds of deportment: walking, standing, sitting and lying down. He also entered the samādhi of fire, and having entered the samādhi of fire, he sent forth from the body of the Buddha, the Blessed One, rays of light of many kinds: blue, yellow, red, white, crimson, and the color of crystal. He also displayed the twin miracle: flames blazed from the lower part of his body while streams of cool water poured from the upper part of his body, and flames blazed from the upper part of his body while streams of cool water poured from the lower part of his body. Such was the samādhi he entered. As in the east, so too in the south, the west and the north.

Having displayed the four kinds of miraculous display in the four directions, he withdrew his manifestation of miraculous power and remained at the height of seven palm trees, while the monks were at the height of six palm trees. When the Blessed One was at the height of six, the monks were at the height of five. When the Blessed One was at the height of five, the monks were at the height of four. When the Blessed One was at the height of four, the monks were at the height of three. When the Blessed One was at the height of three, the monks were at the height of two. When the Blessed One was at the height of two, the monks were at the height of one palm tree.

When the Blessed One remained at the height of seven men, the monks were at the height of six men. When the Blessed One was at the height of six, the monks were at the height of five. When the Blessed One was at the height of five, the monks were at the height of four. When the Blessed One was at the height of four, the monks were at the height of three. When the Blessed One was at the height of three, the monks were at the height of two. When the Blessed One was at the height of two, the monks were at little more than a man's height. When the Blessed One was at little more than a man's height, the monks were on the ground.

When King Śuddhodana saw such a miracle and so many monks, he was bewildered and did not know which of them was the Blessed One. So he said to the venerable Udāyin, "Udāyin, there are a great many renunciants here wearing saffron robes. Which of them is the prince?"

The venerable Udāyin pointed out the Blessed One and said:

"This is the Blessed One, the Ender of existence, the lord of the mind, the Guide.
This is the sun that dispels the afflictions, who teaches every aim.
This is he who has attained what he intended, possessed of the ten powers, gone beyond all.
He who sought the stainless, the lord of Dharma, is your son. Lord of men, behold him!"

Then King Śuddhodana bowed his body, prostrated at his feet, and spoke in verse:

O you of vast wisdom, with this
I have bowed three times at the feet of the All-Seeing One:
when at your birth the earth shook,
and when the jambu tree's shade did not pass from your body.

Then the Śākya men and women, seeing King Śuddhodana touch his head to the Blessed One's feet, lost faith, saying, "What sort of conduct is this, a father bowing at the feet of his son?"

To make the Śākya men and women understand, King Śuddhodana said this: "Sirs, it is not only now that I have bowed at the feet of Prince Sarvārthasiddha. When Prince Sarvārthasiddha was born, there was a great earthquake, and this whole world was lit up with a vast radiance. Even the dark intervals between the worlds, black and pitch dark, never reached by the light of the sun and moon, so mighty in miraculous power and so great in strength, were at that time lit up by a great radiance. By that light the beings born there saw one another and recognized one another, saying, 'Friends, look, other beings are born here too! Friends, look, other beings are born here too!' At that time too I bowed my head at the feet of the Bodhisattva.

When Prince Sarvārthasiddha went to watch the work at the edge of the fields, he sat in the shade of a jambu tree. Secluded from desires, secluded from evil and unwholesome phenomena, with conception and with analysis, he attained and dwelt in the first meditative concentration, which has the joy and happiness born of seclusion and is congruent with the undefiled. Then the shadows of the other trees inclined toward the east and sloped toward the east, but although they fell toward the east, the shadow of the jambu tree did not move away from the body of the Bodhisattva. At that time too my mind was captivated by that wondrous and marvelous phenomenon, and I bowed once more at the feet of the Blessed One. With this, I have bowed three times at the feet of the Blessed One. Therefore he said:

O you of vast intelligence, with this
I have bowed three times at the feet of the All-Seeing One:
when you were born this earth shook,
and the shadow of the jambu did not move away from your body.

Then the Blessed One withdrew the miracle he had manifested and, outshining an assembly of many hundreds, sat down on the seat that had been prepared in the midst of the Saṅgha of monks.

Then King Śuddhodana, his voice faltering and his eyes filled with tears, questioned the Blessed One in the melody of verse:

From dwellings like celestial palaces,
from living in your father's house,
how is it that you now dwell alone
in wildernesses full of dangers?

The Blessed One said:

Lord of men, wherever they may be,
freed from the bonds of household life,
the sages make their dwelling
in the ten abodes of the noble ones.

The king said:

You used to sleep upon a couch
of ivory with feet of gold,
spread with the softest coverings:
how do you not suffer on a simple mat?

The Blessed One said:

O king, on the bed of liberation,
strewn with the flowers of joy and the limbs of awakening,
I sleep without attachment,
free of every torment.

The king said:

Hero, in your own palace
you went by elephant, chariot and horse:
how do you now go on foot
over ground full of thorns?

The Blessed One said:

My chariot is the bases of miraculous power,
driven on by right effort;
though I roam through every land,
the thorns of the afflictions do me no harm.

King Śuddhodana said:

Hero, once you wore garments
of Kāśi cloth and were splendid:
how does your body now bear
a coarse saffron robe?

The Blessed One said:

O king, my garment is a sense of shame;
what I wear is the patched outer robe;
wearing it, the peaceful sages
become beautiful in the forest.

King Śuddhodana said:

Once you ate rice of many flavors
from bowls of gold;
now, O lord, how does this alms food,
this common fare, go down your throat?

The Blessed One said:

Having abandoned all craving for food,
I partake of the supreme taste of my Dharma,
which brings renunciation and meditative equipoise;
yet I take alms out of compassion for the world.

King Śuddhodana said:

You used to drink grape wine and sugared water,
which bring much delight to the throat:
how do you drink water
that is murky and foul-smelling?

The Blessed One said:

Lord of men, I drink the water of supreme taste;
when one has drunk it,
all other drinks
appear to me like poison.

King Śuddhodana said:

Once you dwelt in cool chambers, in mansions like celestial palaces,
and on the upper storeys, each according to its season;
now that you dwell alone in the forest,
O noble-minded one, how do you live without fear?

The Blessed One said:

Gautama, since I have abandoned the root of fear,
the stains together with their habitual tendencies,
I dwell free of sorrow and fear;
here I have not the slightest fear.

King Śuddhodana said:

Once, in your most delightful bathhouse,
you were always cooled by the finest, most exquisite baths;
now, Lord of Sages, as you dwell alone in the forest,
who will bathe you?

The Blessed One said:

Gautama, the stainless pool of the Dharma
has the ford of moral discipline, praised by the holy;
people of virtue who touch it and bathe there
cross over to the far shore with bodies unwet.

King Śuddhodana said:

Hero, in your own house
you were bathed from vases of gold and silver:
how do you now bathe in water
that is murky and foul-smelling?

The Blessed One said:

Gautama, the river of faith is unclouded;
it has the ford of merit, praised by the holy;
people endowed with knowledge who bathe there
cross over to the far shore with bodies unwet.

King Śuddhodana said:

Hero, once in Kapilavastu
your body was bathed with sandalwood,
and you were splendid, clothed in the finest Kāśi cloth;
parted from all that, you are splendid no longer.

The Blessed One said:

Moral discipline is my ornament;
moral discipline itself is my unguent.
Gold, jewels and earrings, born of delusion,
what use are they to me?

King Śuddhodana said:

What makes you disdain these things?
From what does fear arise?
And from what does weariness arise?
Tell me the things I have asked.

The Blessed One said:

What makes me disdain them,
what it is that brings fear,
and from what weariness has arisen,
listen, and I shall explain it to you.
Old age, sickness and death:
if these three did not exist,
I too would take delight
in objects that are most pleasing.

King Śuddhodana said:

Joy of the Śākya clan,
hero, well born, excellent!
You are in no way stained
by the eight worldly concerns.

Then, overjoyed, his faith grown strong,
he circumambulated the Saṅgha of sages,
bowed his head at the feet of the Sage,
and sat gazing at the Sugata.

Then King Śuddhodana thought, "That my son has gained such a wealth of good qualities means that I have truly gained what is worth gaining."

Then the Blessed One, in the Nyagrodha Park, knowing the intentions, the latent tendencies, the constituents and the natures of that assembly, taught the Dharma in accord with them. Hearing it, seventy-seven thousand Śākyas, Śuklodana and the others, directly realized the fruit of stream-entry, all except King Śuddhodana.

Devadatta, who was arrogant with pride upon pride, of evil desires, and who had not seen the truth, said in a fit of anger, "If I knew how to bring people under my control with an illusion like this, I too would bring the whole world under my control." The Śākyas rebuked him with words of reproach: "Devadatta, the Blessed One has great miraculous power. Do not speak like that!" Thus restrained, he sat without saying anything.

In the pleasure grove called Encircled by Brahmā, the Blessed One taught the Dharma in the same way, and on hearing it sixty-six thousand Śākyas, Droṇodana and the others, all except King Śuddhodana, directly realized the fruit of stream-entry. Even then Devadatta said the very same thing.

In the Rohitaka pleasure grove the Blessed One taught the Dharma in the same way, and on hearing it seventy-five thousand Śākyas, Amṛtodana and the others, all except King Śuddhodana, directly realized the fruit of stream-entry. Even then Devadatta said the very same thing.

Of the rest of the assembly, some set their minds on the awakening of a śrāvaka, some on the awakening of a pratyekabuddha, and some on unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening. Some directly realized the fruit of stream-entry, some the fruit of once-returning, and some the fruit of non-returning. Some went forth, abandoned all the afflictions, and became arhats. Some went for refuge to the Buddha, some to the Dharma, and some to the Saṅgha. Indeed, most of that assembly were established so as to incline toward the Buddha, slope toward the Dharma, and flow toward the Saṅgha.

Then King Śuddhodana thought, "Formerly the Blessed One was honored, revered and praised by the world with its gods and asuras, but now he is honored by humans and not by the gods."

Then a young Śākya addressed him in verse:

Great sage, supreme among speakers, with the rain
of the true Dharma you satisfy these beings.
This world that has fallen onto a terrible path,
lead it, please, and set it on the path of liberation.

Then the Blessed One reflected, "King Śuddhodana is thinking, 'Only my son has such great miraculous power and such great might,' and he has become excessively elated. In short, I must make him give up his pride. For two reasons one does not come to see the truths: excessive dejection and excessive elation. He has both a dejected mind and excessive elation, so I shall make him give up both his dejection and his excessive elation."

Then the Blessed One said to the venerable Mahāmaudgalyāyana, "Maudgalyāyana, go to King Śuddhodana."

"Venerable One, I shall do as you say," replied the venerable Mahāmaudgalyāyana, heeding the Blessed One, and he went to where King Śuddhodana was. King Śuddhodana saw the venerable Mahāmaudgalyāyana coming from far off, and when he saw him he said to the venerable Mahāmaudgalyāyana, "Come, Venerable Mahāmaudgalyāyana! Mahāmaudgalyāyana, you are welcome. Venerable Mahāmaudgalyāyana, please sit on the seat that has been prepared." The venerable Mahāmaudgalyāyana sat on the seat that had been prepared.

Then the venerable Mahāmaudgalyāyana settled his mind in such a way that he vanished from his seat, rose into the sky above in the east, and there adopted the four modes of conduct, namely walking, standing, sitting and lying down. He also entered the absorption of fire, and when he had entered the absorption of fire, his body emitted light rays of many kinds, namely blue, yellow, red, white, crimson and the color of crystal. He also displayed the twin miracle: he made fire blaze from the lower part of his body and a stream of cold water pour down from the upper part of his body; he made fire blaze from the upper part of his body and a stream of cold water pour down from the lower part of his body. Such was the meditative absorption that he entered. As in the east, so too in the south, the west and the north. Having displayed these four kinds of miraculous display in the four directions, he withdrew his miraculous manifestation and sat down on the seat that had been prepared.

Then King Śuddhodana said to the venerable Mahāmaudgalyāyana, "Venerable Mahāmaudgalyāyana, are there other śrāvakas of the Blessed One who have such great miraculous power and such great might?"

"There are, great king," he said, and spoke in verse:

Those of great might, possessing the three knowledges,
those who know the minds of others,
and arhats whose defilements are exhausted:
the Sage has many such śrāvakas.

Then King Śuddhodana thought, "It is not only my son who has such great miraculous power and such great might; there are other renunciants too who have such great miraculous power and such great might." And with that he was freed of his excessive elation.

On that occasion the Blessed One brought forth a worldly thought: "Ah, if Śakra, Brahmā and the other gods would come, I would teach the Dharma to a pure assembly of gods." By the nature of things, when the buddhas, the blessed ones, bring forth a worldly thought, even the minds of tiny creatures such as ants and beetles come to know the Blessed One's intention, so what need is there to mention Śakra, Brahmā and the other gods? But when they bring forth a thought that transcends the world, not even the minds of the great śrāvakas know the Blessed One's intention, so what need is there to mention Śakra, Brahmā and the other gods? How could tiny creatures such as ants and beetles know it?

Then Śakra, lord of the gods, wondered, "Why has the Blessed One brought forth a worldly thought?" and he realized, "He wishes to teach the Dharma to a pure assembly of gods." So he commanded the god Viśvakarman, "Viśvakarman, the Blessed One wishes to teach the Dharma to a pure assembly of gods. Conjure up in the Nyagrodha Park a multistoried pavilion of the four kinds of precious substance, furnished with all kinds of seats."

"Kauśika, your word is excellent indeed," said the god Viśvakarman, heeding Śakra, lord of the gods, and in the Nyagrodha Park he conjured up a multistoried pavilion of the four kinds of precious substance, furnished with all kinds of seats.

At its four gates were stationed the Four Great Kings: Dhṛtarāṣṭra, Virūḍhaka, Virūpākṣa and Kubera. At the eastern gate was Dhṛtarāṣṭra, adorned with armlets, earrings, bracelets, garlands and all kinds of ornaments. On the south was Virūḍhaka, adorned with every divine ornament. On the west was Virūpākṣa, adorned with the seven kinds of precious substance. On the north was Kubera, adorned with every ornament.

Then Śakra, lord of the gods, had the Nyagrodha Park lavishly adorned, went to where the Blessed One was, and on arriving reported the matter to the Blessed One in full. Then the Blessed One, surrounded by Śakra, Brahmā and many hundreds of thousands of gods, entered the multistoried pavilion and sat on a lion throne adorned with all kinds of jewels, in the midst of a vast assembly of gods.

Then the venerable Mahāmaudgalyāyana, bringing King Śuddhodana with him, went to where the Blessed One was. When they arrived, the venerable Mahāmaudgalyāyana went inside, but the Great King Dhṛtarāṣṭra stopped King Śuddhodana, saying, "Great king, do not go in. Stay here."

"Why?"

"The Blessed One is teaching the Dharma to a pure assembly of gods, and no one who is human may enter there."

"Fair one, who are you?"

"Great king, I am Dhṛtarāṣṭra."

Then King Śuddhodana went to the southern gate, and when the Great King Virūḍhaka saw him, he said, "Great king, do not enter. Stay here."

King Śuddhodana looked at him and said, "Fair one, who are you?"

Virūḍhaka said, "Great king, I am Virūḍhaka."

"Why?"

"The Blessed One is teaching the Dharma to a pure assembly of gods, and no one who is human may enter there."

Then King Śuddhodana went to the western gate, and when the Great King Virūpākṣa saw him, he said, "Great king, do not enter. Stay here."

King Śuddhodana looked at him and said, "Fair one, who are you?"

Virūpākṣa said, "Great king, I am Virūpākṣa."

"Why?"

"The Blessed One is teaching the Dharma to a pure assembly of gods, and no one who is human may enter there."

Then King Śuddhodana went to the northern gate, and when the Great King Vaiśravaṇa saw him, he said, "Great king, do not enter. Stay here."

King Śuddhodana said, "Fair one, who are you?"

Vaiśravaṇa said, "Great king, I am Vaiśravaṇa."

"Why?"

"The Blessed One is teaching the Dharma to a pure assembly of gods, and no one who is human may enter there."

Then in King Śuddhodana there arose the longing, "Oh, how I wish I could watch the Blessed One teaching the Dharma to the pure assembly of gods!" And with that King Śuddhodana was freed from his state of dejection.

Then the Blessed One reflected, "If King Śuddhodana does not see me, he will reach a state in which he will vomit warm blood and die. I must devise a means for this." So the Blessed One transformed the multistoried pavilion of the four kinds of precious substance so that it was made of crystal, and then King Śuddhodana saw the body of the Buddha without obstruction. When he saw it, joy and delight arose in him; he bowed his head at the feet of the Blessed One and sat down to one side.

Then the Blessed One, knowing King Śuddhodana's intention, latent tendencies, constituents and nature, taught him the Dharma in such a way that he would come to realize each of the four noble truths. Hearing it, King Śuddhodana shattered with the vajra of wisdom the lofty twenty-peaked mountain of the view of the perishable collection, and directly realized the fruit of stream-entry. Having seen the truths, he three times uttered this solemn utterance:

"Venerable One, what was not done for me by my father, nor by my mother, nor by the king, nor by the gods, nor by my departed ancestors, nor by ascetics and brahmins, nor by my loved ones, my own people and the host of my kin, that the Blessed One has done for me. He has dried up the ocean of blood and tears, rescued me from the mountain of bones, closed the doors to the lower realms, opened the doors to the higher realms and to liberation, and established me among gods and humans."

And he said further:

The kindness a worthy son should do
to repay his father,
you have done for me:
you have turned my mind toward liberation.
You drew me up out of the lower destinies
and set me where I need not strain
for the higher realms and liberation;
well have you brought me joy.
Loving protector, like a merchant
who has found his profit,
you have let me gain the higher realms
and the riches of existence.

Then King Śuddhodana rose from his seat, draped his upper robe over one shoulder, bowed toward the Blessed One with palms joined, and said to the Blessed One, "Blessed One, I am preparing a midday meal at my residence tomorrow; please consent to come, together with the Saṅgha of monks." The Blessed One consented to King Śuddhodana by remaining silent.

Then King Śuddhodana, understanding that the Blessed One had consented by remaining silent, bowed his head at the feet of the Blessed One, departed from the presence of the Blessed One, and went to where Śuklodana was. When he arrived, he said to Śuklodana, "Prince, the kingdom has come to you. Take the kingdom."

"Why is that?"

"Because I have seen the truth in the presence of the Blessed One."

"On what day?"

"Just today."

"I too saw the truth, together with seventy-seven thousand Śākyas, on the day the Blessed One taught the Dharma in the Nyagrodha Park."

In the same way he said to Droṇodana, "Take the kingdom," and he replied, "I too saw the truth, together with seventy-six thousand Śākyas, at the time the Blessed One taught the Dharma in the pleasure grove called Encircled by Brahmā."

In the same way he said to Amṛtodana, "Take the kingdom," and he replied, "I too saw the truth, together with seventy-five thousand Śākyas, at the time the Blessed One himself taught the Dharma in the Rohitaka pleasure grove."

"If that is so, whom shall we place on the throne?"

They said, "Bhadrika, king of the Śākyas, shall take it." So they assembled and consecrated Bhadrika, king of the Śākyas, to sovereign rule over the kingdom.

Then King Śuddhodana spent that very night preparing pure and delicious food, both hard and soft. Rising early the next morning, he had the seats set out and a jeweled water vessel placed, and he sent a messenger to invite the Blessed One and announce the time: "Venerable One, it is time for the midday meal. Please know that the time has come."

In the morning the Blessed One put on his lower robe, took up his bowl and Dharma robe, and, surrounded by the company of monks and preceded by the Saṅgha of monks, went to the dining hall of King Śuddhodana. When he arrived, he sat down on the seat prepared in the midst of the Saṅgha of monks.

Then King Śuddhodana, seeing that the Saṅgha of monks headed by the Buddha was comfortably seated, served them with his own hands with pure and delicious food, both hard and soft, and satisfied them; in many ways he served them with his own hands with pure and delicious food, both hard and soft, and satisfied them. When he saw that the Blessed One had finished his meal, had washed his hands and had put away his bowl, he took up a golden spouted pitcher and offered the Nyagrodha Park to the Blessed One. The Blessed One, with a voice of five qualities, dedicated the offering:

By whatever merit there is in this gift,
may the hopes the Śākyas have held
in their minds be fulfilled,
and may they attain the permanent state.

Then the Blessed One stayed at Kapilavastu, in the Nyagrodha Park. Accordingly, the meditators too set it down in the sūtras: "The Blessed One was staying at Kapilavastu, in the Nyagrodha Park."

While the Blessed One stayed at Kapilavastu, on one day he took his midday meal in the king's palace and on another day in the women's quarters.

King Śuddhodana saw that the monks who had gone forth from among the matted-hair ascetics, though serene in their conduct and inspiring confidence, were unlovely in body, their bodies wasted by various kinds of fierce ascetic vows. When he saw this, he thought, "These matted-hair ascetics are serene in conduct and inspire confidence, but they are not lovely in body. With what kind of retinue would the Blessed One look splendid?" And he concluded, "With a retinue of Śākyas."

So he gathered all the Śākyas and said, "Friends, if Prince Sarvārthasiddha had not gone forth, what would he have become?"

"He would have become a universal monarch."

"And what would you have done?"

"We would have served him."

"Now Prince Sarvārthasiddha has become the unsurpassed king of Dharma; why do you not serve him?"

"Your Majesty, are we to go forth?"

"Go forth!"

"All of us?"

"One from each household."

"As you command."

King Śuddhodana had it proclaimed with bells throughout the city of Kapilavastu: "The king has commanded: Of the Śākyas who live in my realm, one from each household must go forth!"

Droṇodana had two sons, Aniruddha and Mahānāman. Mahānāman devoted himself above all to work in the fields, while Aniruddha stayed on the upper terrace of the palace, amusing himself, delighting and enjoying himself with music played without men.

His mother said to Mahānāman, "Son, the king has had it proclaimed with bells, commanding that of the Śākyas one from each household must go forth. You go forth."

He said, "I will not go forth. Have your darling son, who lives in comfort, go forth."

"Son, do not try to compete with that being of great merit and fortune."

"Mother, the one you favor may well have great merit and fortune, but today send him nothing at all, and it will become clear whether he has great merit and fortune or not."

"Son, I will do just that, and we shall see for certain."

She filled an empty basket with dishes, wrapped its mouth in white cloth, and sealed it with a seal of lac. She sent it in the hands of a serving girl, instructing her, "If he asks what is in it, say, 'There is nothing.'"

The girl took it and set off. Now the knowledge and vision of Śakra, lord of the gods, reaches down below, and he thought, "Why should the one who offered alms to the pratyekabuddha All-Knowing go short of food today? I shall prepare a meal for him." So he filled those dishes with lentil soup, vegetables and food of every flavor.

In due course the serving girl brought it to Aniruddha. He said, "Girl, what is in this?"

She said, "Young master, there is nothing."

Aniruddha thought, "If my mother did not love me, why would she send this empty? Surely she has sent a food called 'nothing.' Let me have a look." When at last he opened it, the whole pleasure grove was filled with the delicious smell of food. Tasting it, Aniruddha was utterly amazed. Since he was respectful toward his mother, he sent her the first portion of the food with the message, "Mother, every day you should send me this kind of food called 'nothing.'"

She too saw it and was utterly amazed. She showed it to Mahānāman: "Son, do you see?"

"Mother, I see."

"Did I not tell you before not to try to compete with that being of great merit and fortune, who has a human's best of fortune?"

He said, "Mother, whether he has great merit and fortune or not, I will not go forth." Thus Mahānāman would not under any circumstances agree to go forth.

Then she said to Aniruddha, "Son, the king has had it proclaimed with bells, commanding that of the Śākyas one from each household must go forth. Will you go forth, or will you remain a householder?"

He said, "Mother, what are the benefits of going forth, and what are its drawbacks? What are the benefits of remaining a householder, and what are its drawbacks?"

"Son, if one who has gone forth practices well, he will attain nirvāṇa. If he does not practice well, he will attain the state of gods and humans. If one remains a householder and keeps to the good, he will attain the state of gods and humans. If he does not, he will be reborn among hell beings, animals and hungry ghosts."

"Mother, the drawback of going forth is the very benefit of living in a household. Therefore I do not wish to live in a household. Please give me your permission, and I will go forth."

She said, "Good, son. Do so."

Aniruddha's close friend was Bhadrika, king of the Śākyas, so he went to see him. At that moment Bhadrika, king of the Śākyas, was playing the vīṇā, and as he played, a string broke and the sound changed. Aniruddha was skilled at playing the vīṇā, and noting how the sound had changed, he stayed at the door and said to the doorkeeper, "Hey, chamberlain, go and tell King Bhadrika: 'Aniruddha wishes to see Your Majesty's face and is waiting at the door.'"

The doorkeeper went and said to Bhadrika, king of the Śākyas, "Your Majesty, Aniruddha wishes to see Your Majesty's face and is waiting at the gate."

He said, "Friend, who would keep Aniruddha out? Friend, let Aniruddha in."

When he came in, the king embraced him, had him sit down, and said, "How long is it since you arrived?"

He said, "Since the moment when, as you were playing the vīṇā, the string broke and the sound changed."

The king was utterly amazed and said, "You are skilled at playing the vīṇā." Then Bhadrika, king of the Śākyas, said, "Prince, what have you come for?"

Aniruddha said, "Your Majesty, King Śuddhodana has had it proclaimed with bells, commanding that of the Śākyas one from each household must go forth. Please take note of this: I am going forth."

The king said, "If that is so, I too shall go forth. There are things to discuss, so stay here today." So he lodged right there.

The bed-maker had spread the bed with cloth that smelled of fever and strewn it with loose flowers. When Aniruddha lay down on it, he felt the touch of a flower stalk underneath and said, "Is there a pebble under here?" When they began to search, they found a flower stalk, and they were utterly amazed. Aniruddha slept badly that night until dawn.

The king said, "Aniruddha, did you sleep well?"

He said, "Your Majesty, I did not sleep well."

The king said, "Why?"

Aniruddha said, "Your Majesty, a cloth smelling of fever was spread out, and among the loose flowers strewn over it a flower stalk hurt my body."

The king summoned the bed-maker and said, "Why did you spread a cloth smelling of fever?"

The bed-maker said, "Your Majesty, the treasurer handed it to me."

The king summoned the treasurer and said, "Why did you issue a cloth smelling of fever?"

He said, "Your Majesty, the weaver handed it to me."

The king summoned the weaver and said, "Why did you supply a cloth smelling of fever?"

The weaver said, "Your Majesty, when only a little of the cloth was left to weave, I finished it while stricken with fever."

The king said, "Prince, how did you know?"

He said, "I felt a feverish touch, so it was the smell of fever."

The king was utterly amazed and said, "Friend Aniruddha is the Śākya endowed with the best of fortune."

Then Bhadrika, king of the Śākyas, said, "Prince, if I go forth, Devadatta will become king of the Śākyas, and he will do harm to the Śākyas. So let us all gather and stir up Devadatta's enthusiasm for going forth." So Bhadrika, king of the Śākyas, summoned him, and when they had all gathered, they said, "Devadatta, if all of us go forth, what will you do?"

Devadatta thought, "If I say, 'I will not go forth,' then Bhadrika, king of the Śākyas, will not go forth either. I must devise some means for this and deceive him."

"Moreover, on the day that the Blessed One displayed a miracle of magic in the Nyagrodha Park, the thought arose in me, 'Just as this man brings a great multitude of people under his sway by his magic, so too shall I bring them under my sway.'" With this in mind, he said, "If you all go forth, why would I stay behind?"

Bhadrika, king of the Śākyas, thought, "He is speaking falsely. I will make him bind himself by a pledge, so that many people will each come to know of it." Then the king wrote these words on a tablet and had them proclaimed with a bell throughout the city of Kapilavastu: "Listen, you wise ones who dwell in the city of Kapilavastu! Five hundred Śākyas, Bhadrika, Revata, Aniruddha, Devadatta and others, are going to go forth. Take note of this, and rejoice greatly!"

When Devadatta heard this, great suffering and unhappiness arose in him, and he thought, "Had I known that Bhadrika, king of the Śākyas, would certainly go forth, I would never have agreed. Now, if I do not go forth, it will be said, 'He is a liar,' and I will not gain the kingship either. Is everything then altogether lost at this point? Why not go forth? Even while having gone forth, I will exercise the kingship."

Then King Śuddhodana thought, "I shall look upon this last splendor of the Śākyas." He had the city cleared of gravel and potsherds and sprinkled with sandalwood water, had parasols and victory banners raised and many silk streamers hung, had censers of exquisitely fragrant incense set out, and had it made delightful with scatterings of every kind of flower. Then, surrounded by many hundreds of thousands of Śākyas, he sat upon a lion throne at the city gate. The Śākya women and the wonderstruck Śākya youths took their places at the latticed windows, the windows, and the terraces. Crowds of men who had come from many lands, wishing to see the splendor of the Śākyas, stood along the highways, in the marketplaces, at the crossroads and at the junctions of three roads. He summoned brahmin soothsayers and diviners to discern which of the Śākyas would bring satisfaction and which would bring grief.

The Śākyas, adorned with necklaces, bracelets, armlets and other ornaments, looked upon their fathers and mothers, each mounted his own chariot, and they began to ride out. When the soothsayers saw Bhadrika, king of the Śākyas, come out at their head, they said, "This one, for his part, will bring satisfaction." Likewise, when Revata, Aniruddha and the others came out, the soothsayers made predictions about them too. Then Devadatta came out, and as he came out, the soothsayers saw a hawk snatch away the jewel from his crown. They said, "Judging by this sign, he will without doubt quarrel with the Teacher and plunge into hell." When Kokālika, Khaṇḍadravya, Kaṭamoraka, Tiṣya and Samudradatta came out, a donkey brayed, and the soothsayers made a prediction about them as well: "These too will speak abusively to the great śrāvakas and plunge into hell." As Upananda, riding on an elephant, was looking this way and that, his pearl necklace fell, and he climbed down from the elephant and picked it up. Of him too they predicted, "This one, being exceedingly greedy, will be reborn among the beings of hell."

Then those five hundred Śākyas, Bhadrika, Revata, Aniruddha, Devadatta and the rest, vying with one another in finery as though going to a festival in a pleasure garden, went to where the Blessed One was. The Blessed One reflected, "If I give the Śākyas the going forth with the words 'Come, monk,' some will bring satisfaction and some will not, and those who will not would think otherwise. Those who go forth by 'Come, monk' are all beings in their final existence. Therefore I shall have the monks give all of them the going forth and full ordination by a formal act of motion and three proclamations." With this in mind, he declared to the monks, "Monks, give the Śākyas of Kapilavastu the going forth and full ordination by a formal act of motion and three proclamations." "Venerable One, as you command," said those monks, heeding the Blessed One.

King Śuddhodana sent the Śākyas' barber, who was called Upāli, to cut their hair as they went forth. When he was cutting the hair of Bhadrika, king of the Śākyas, he cut it with his eyes darkened, his tears falling, his voice choking, and his breath heaving with sighs. Bhadrika, king of the Śākyas, said, "Upāli, why are you like this?" In a pitiful, faint voice, slowly, word by word, he said, "My lord, just think: I, who have served the foremost men of Jambudvīpa, shall now have to serve common people! Abandoned by you, I would rather die than live."

Bhadrika, king of the Śākyas, said, "Upāli, you have already shown heartfelt devotion in pleasing your master. Enough; do not grieve. I will see to it that you will not have to serve common people." Having said this, he spread a cloth out to one side and said to the Śākya youths, "Listen, wise young men! This barber Upāli has shown us honor, and we will not dwell in the household again, so place your ornaments on the cloth to provide for his livelihood." And upon the cloth they heaped up a great pile of their splendid ornaments, adorned with every kind of jewel: chains, necklaces, collars, bracelets, armlets, earrings and the rest.

Upāli cut their hair one after another, and when they had gone to bathe, he thought, "Well now, if these young men, endowed with noble birth and wealth, in the prime of youth, possessed of wives and rich in treasuries and storehouses, abandon all this and go forth, then it is not fitting for me to cling to these mere ornaments; it would only bring me suffering."

"If only I had not been born into a low caste, I too would go forth in the well-spoken Dharma and Vinaya, and would strive in seeking and in effort in order to cross over the floods and pass completely beyond the yokes."

Now it is the nature of things that, just as the buddhas, the blessed ones, survey the world with the buddha eye three times by day and three times by night, so too do the great śrāvakas. Thus the venerable Śāriputra saw that the barber Upāli had become deeply disheartened. Having seen this, he went to where the barber Upāli was, and when he arrived, he said this to the barber Upāli: "Upāli, why are you sitting so very unhappily, with your cheek resting in your hand, sunk in brooding?"

Upāli said, "Venerable Śāriputra, it is because now these young men, endowed with noble birth and wealth, in the prime of youth, possessed of wives and rich in treasuries and storehouses, abandon all this and go forth; so it is not fitting for me to cling to these mere ornaments, and it would only bring me suffering. If only I had not been born into a low caste, I too would go forth in the well-spoken Dharma and Vinaya, and would strive in seeking and in effort in order to cross over the floods and pass completely beyond the yokes. How could I not be sunk in brooding?"

The venerable Śāriputra said, "Good friend, in this teaching of the Lord of Sages, caste is not what counts as essential, lineage is not what counts as essential, ancestry is not what counts as essential, and learning is not what counts as essential. Rather, in this teaching of the Lord of Sages, it is practice that counts as essential.

The tathāgatas do not consider
Caste, or ancestry, or lineage;
It is deeds that were done
Long ago that they consider.

If you wish to go forth in the well-spoken Dharma and Vinaya and to attain full ordination and the state of a monk, come, let us go before the Blessed One, and the Blessed One will give you the going forth." He agreed.

Then the venerable Śāriputra took the barber Upāli with him and went to where the Blessed One was. When they arrived, he bowed his head at the feet of the Blessed One and said this to the Blessed One: "Venerable One, this Upāli wishes to go forth in the well-spoken Dharma and Vinaya and to attain full ordination and the state of a monk. Out of your loving kindness, please take him in, and may the Blessed One give him the going forth and full ordination."

The Blessed One said to him, "Come, monk! Practice the pure life!" The very moment the Blessed One spoke these words, his head was shaven and he was wearing the monk's robe; his hands held an alms bowl and a water pot with a spout; his hair and beard were like those grown for just seven days; and he stood with the deportment of a monk fully ordained for a hundred years. It is said:

When the Tathāgata declared, "Come!"
His head was shaven, his body clad in the monk's robe,
At once his senses came to rest in perfect peace,
And by the Buddha's intention his bodily form was clothed.

The Śākyas who had gone forth paid homage in order of seniority. When Bhadrika, king of the Śākyas, was bowing down, he recognized that these were Upāli's feet. Then he raised his face and said, "Blessed One, this is Upāli. Must I bow at his feet too?"

The Blessed One said, "Son, you have gone forth in order to abandon pride. He is your senior, so enough of the arrogance of pride: bow down!"

When he touched his head to Upāli's feet, the great earth quaked in six ways. This great earth shook, shook strongly, and shook strongly all over; it rocked, rocked strongly, and rocked strongly all over. When the east rose, the west sank; when the west rose, the east sank; when the south rose, the north sank; when the north rose, the south sank; when the center rose, the edges sank; and when the edges rose, the center sank. In this way all of them, one after another, touched their heads to his feet.

When Devadatta did not bow down, the Blessed One said, "Son, you have gone forth in order to abandon pride, so bow down!"

He replied, "If I do not touch my head to his feet, what harm is it to you?" He would not bow down, and there, for the first time, Devadatta defied the command of the Blessed One.

The Buddha, the Blessed One, was staying in the land of Kapilavastu, in the Nyagrodha Park. At that time a great many Śākyas living in Kapilavastu assembled and gathered in the council hall, and this sort of conversation arose among them: "Friends, from whom were the Śākyas born? What was their beginning? What was their origin? What was the ancient lineage of the Śākyas? If someone were to come to us and ask, 'Friends, from whom were the Śākyas born? What was their beginning? What was their origin? What was the ancient lineage of the Śākyas?', and we did not know from whom the Śākyas were born, what their beginning was, what their origin was, or what the ancient lineage of the Śākyas was, what answer could we give? Come, let us go to where the Blessed One is, and when we arrive, let us ask the Blessed One about this very matter, and keep it in mind just as the Blessed One explains it to us."

Then a great many Śākyas living in Kapilavastu went to where the Blessed One was. When they arrived, they bowed their heads at the feet of the Blessed One and sat down to one side. Seated to one side, the Śākyas of Kapilavastu said this:

"Venerable One, here a great many of us Śākyas of Kapilavastu assembled and gathered in the council hall, and this sort of conversation arose among us: 'From whom were the Śākyas born? What was their beginning? What was their origin? What was the ancient lineage of the Śākyas? If someone were to come to us and ask, "Friends, from whom were the Śākyas born? What was their beginning? What was their origin? What was the ancient lineage of the Śākyas?", and we did not know from whom the Śākyas were born, what their beginning was, what their origin was, or what the ancient lineage of the Śākyas was, what answer could we give? Come, let us go to where the Blessed One is, and when we arrive, let us ask the Blessed One about this very matter, and keep it in mind just as the Blessed One explains it to us.' For that reason we ask the Blessed One about this very matter. Blessed One, from whom were the Śākyas born? What was their beginning? What was their origin? What was the ancient lineage of the Śākyas?"

Then the Blessed One thought, "If I give a Dharma talk on the ancient lineage of the Śākyas, there is the possibility that non-Buddhists (tīrthikas) and other wandering ascetics will say, 'The monk Gautama declares whatever he likes and so praises himself.' Which of my śrāvakas is able to give a Dharma talk on the ancient lineage of the Śākyas?"

At that time the venerable Mahāmaudgalyāyana was present, seated in that very assembly. Then the Blessed One said to the venerable Mahāmaudgalyāyana, "Maudgalyāyana, my back is aching, so for now, you take this up. Be inspired to give the monks a Dharma talk on the ancient lineage of the Śākyas." The venerable Mahāmaudgalyāyana consented to the Blessed One by remaining silent.

Then the Blessed One, knowing that the venerable Mahāmaudgalyāyana had consented by remaining silent, folded his monk's robe, placed it as a pillow, and lay down on his right side, with one foot placed upon the other, attending only to the perception of light and to the perception endowed with mindfulness and vigilance, and so he slept.

Then the venerable Mahāmaudgalyāyana thought, "Now then, I shall enter into a samādhi such that, when my mind is settled in it, I will see from whom the Śākyas were born, what their beginning was, what their origin was, and what the ancient lineage of the Śākyas was." Having thought this, the venerable Mahāmaudgalyāyana entered into a samādhi such that, when his mind was settled in it, he would see the ancient lineage of the Śākyas. And the venerable Mahāmaudgalyāyana saw the ancient lineage of the Śākyas: from whom the Śākyas were born, what their beginning was, what their origin was, and what the ancient lineage of the Śākyas was. Having seen it, he arose from that samādhi and sat down on the seat prepared in the midst of the monastic Saṅgha. Seated there, the venerable Mahāmaudgalyāyana said to the Śākyas of Kapilavastu:

"Gautamas, when this world is destroyed, and after the world has perished, most beings are reborn among the gods of Clear Light. There they have mind-made forms, perfect, with unimpaired faculties and nothing lacking, possessed of all their limbs, major and minor. They abide with a fine complexion, are self-luminous, and move through the sky. They feed on joy, and on the food of joy they live long and remain for a long time.

"At that time this great earth becomes nothing but water, a single ocean. On this great earth that has become a single ocean of nothing but water, the wind causes a skin to form, to harden, and to spread over it. Just as, when boiled milk cools, the wind causes a skin to form on it, to harden, and to spread over it, so the wind causes a skin to form, to harden, and to spread over this great earth that has become a single ocean of nothing but water. That essence of the earth is perfect in color, perfect in fragrance and perfect in taste. Its color is like this: like fresh butter. Its taste is like this: like pressed honey from bees. Such was that time.

"Gautamas, when this world forms again, and the world has come into being, then certain beings, through the exhaustion of their lifespan, the exhaustion of their karma and the exhaustion of their merit, die and pass from among the gods of Clear Light, and come here, sharing the same lot as humans. Here they have mind-made forms, perfect, with unimpaired faculties and nothing lacking, possessed of all their limbs, major and minor. They abide with a fine complexion, are self-luminous, and move through the sky. They feed on joy, and on the food of joy they live long and remain for a long time.

"At that time neither sun nor moon appears in the world; there are no constellations; there are no instants, moments or hours; there is neither night nor day; and months, fortnights, seasons and years do not appear in the world either. Nor is there any distinction of male and female; rather, they are counted simply as beings, as 'beings.'

"Then a certain being of greedy nature tastes the essence of the earth with the tip of a finger. The more it tastes, the more it craves; and the more it craves, the more it eats it, mouthful by mouthful. Other beings, too, see that being tasting the essence of the earth with the tip of a finger, craving it the more it tastes, and eating it mouthful by mouthful the more it craves. Seeing this, they too begin to taste the essence of the earth with the tips of their fingers. The more they taste, the more they crave; and the more they crave, the more they eat it, mouthful by mouthful."

"When those beings ate the essence of the earth mouthful by mouthful, coarseness and heaviness entered their bodies. The radiance of their fine complexions faded, and so darkness arose in the world. Gautamas, it is the nature of things that when darkness arises in the world, the sun and moon appear in the world. Constellations, instants, moments, hours, nights and days, months and fortnights, seasons and years appear in the world.

"They ate it, and by taking it as food they lived long and remained for a long time. Among them, whoever ate very little of the food kept a good complexion, and whoever ate a great deal of the food took on a poor complexion. Because of the two measures of food, two kinds of complexion appeared; and because there were two kinds of complexion, being looked down on being, saying, 'Hey, being, I have a good complexion; you have a poor complexion.' Because those who were proud of their complexions took up those very evil, nonvirtuous things, the essence of the earth vanished.

"Gautamas, when the essence of the earth vanished, those beings assembled and gathered and said, 'Alas, the taste! Alas, the taste!' They lamented, and grieved, and suffered. Just as people nowadays, after eating a little tasty food, recall those very letters, words and syllables of old and say, 'Alas, the taste! Alas, the taste!', so those beings, when the essence of the earth vanished, assembled and gathered and said, 'Alas, the taste! Alas, the taste!' They lamented, grieved and suffered, yet they did not know its meaning, that is, 'This is the meaning of what is said; this is the meaning of what is said.'

"Gautamas, when the essence of the earth had vanished for those beings, the fat of the earth appeared, perfect in color and perfect in taste. Its color was like this: like the karṇikāra flower. Its taste was like this: like pressed honey from bees. They ate it, and by taking it as food they lived long and remained for a long time. Among them, whoever ate very little of the food kept a good complexion, and whoever ate a great deal of the food took on a poor complexion. Because of the two measures of food, two kinds of complexion appeared; and because there were two kinds of complexion, being looked down on being, saying, 'Hey, being, I have a good complexion, and you have a poor complexion.' Because those who were proud of their complexions took up those very evil, nonvirtuous things, the fat of the earth vanished.

"Gautamas, when the fat of the earth vanished, those beings assembled and gathered and said, 'Alas, the taste! Alas, the taste!' They lamented, and grieved in their suffering. Just as people nowadays, when touched by nothing but suffering and unhappiness, recall those very letters, words and syllables of old and say, 'Ah, alas! Ah, alas!', so those beings, when the fat of the earth vanished, assembled and gathered and said, 'Alas, the taste! Alas, the taste!' They lamented, grieved and suffered, yet they did not know 'This is the meaning of what is said; this is the meaning of what is said.'

"Gautamas, when the fat had vanished for those beings, a grove of shoots appeared, perfect in color, perfect in fragrance and perfect in taste. Its color was like this: like the flower of the kadamba tree. Its taste was like this: like pressed honey from bees. They ate it, and by taking it as food they lived long and remained for a long time. Among them, whoever ate very little of the food kept a good complexion, and whoever ate a great deal of the food took on a poor complexion. Because of the two measures of food, two kinds of complexion appeared; and because there were two kinds of complexion, being looked down on being, saying, 'Hey, being, I have a good complexion, and you have a poor complexion.' Because those who were proud of their complexions took up those very evil, nonvirtuous things, the grove of shoots vanished.

"Gautamas, when the grove of shoots vanished, those beings assembled and gathered and said, 'Get away from before me! Get away from before me!' They lamented, grieved and suffered. Just as people nowadays, when they wish to punish someone with a harsh punishment, repeat those very letters, words and syllables of old and say, 'Get away from before me! Get away from before me!', so those beings, when the grove of shoots vanished, assembled and gathered and said, 'Get away from before me! Get away from before me!' They lamented, grieved and suffered, yet they did not know its meaning, that is, 'This is the meaning of what is said; this is the meaning of what is said.'

"Gautamas, after the grove of shoots had vanished for those beings, unplowed and unsown sāli rice grew, its grains four finger-breadths long, without husk, without chaff, without bran, clean and pure. If it was reaped in the evening, it grew and ripened again by morning; if it was reaped in the morning, it grew and ripened again by evening. Since it grew back however much it was reaped, no sign of reaping could be seen. They ate it, and by taking it as food they lived long and remained for a long time.

"From the time those beings ate the unplowed and unsown sāli rice mouthful by mouthful, distinct sexual organs appeared in them: some had the male organ, and some had the female organ. Then those with the female organ and those with the male organ gazed at one another, eye to eye. The more they gazed at one another eye to eye, the more they became infatuated; and the more they became infatuated, the more they clung to one another."

"And the more they clung to one another, the more they engaged in misconduct. Other beings saw beings engaging in misconduct with beings. Having seen this, they pelted them with earth, clods, gravel and potsherds, saying, 'Wretched, base beings are doing what should not be done! Wretched, base beings are doing what should not be done! Hey, being, how could you now defile a being?' Just as people nowadays, when a bride is taken, shower her with powders, perfumes, garlands, cloth and parched rice, saying, 'May the bride be happy!', so, when beings saw beings engaging in misconduct, they pelted them with earth and pelted them with clods, gravel and potsherds too, saying, 'Wretched, base beings are doing what should not be done! Wretched, base beings are doing what should not be done! Hey, being, how could you now defile a being?' Such was that time.

"Gautamas, thus what was formerly considered contrary to Dharma and what was formerly considered contrary to discipline is now considered Dharma and considered discipline; what was formerly considered blameworthy is now considered praiseworthy. They shunned those beings for a single day, and then shunned them for two days, for three, and for seven. Then those beings, who, in that wicked and ignoble practice, followed an exceedingly wicked way of conduct, set about building houses, saying, 'Here we may do what should not be done; here we may do what should not be done.' And so the word 'house' came to be known. Gautamas, this was the first undertaking from which the works of house-building arose in the world. This came about in accordance with what is right, not with what is wrong; for in the Dharma of the Lord of Victors this is what is foremost.

"Those who wished to eat in the evening went for sāli rice in the evening, and those who wished to eat in the morning went for it in the morning. Then a certain being of lazy nature took the morning's sāli rice in the evening. Then another being said to that being, 'Hey, being, come, let us go for sāli rice.' That being replied to the other, 'Hey, being, look to your own sāli rice. I took the morning's sāli rice in the evening.' Then that being thought, 'Ah, that is good! Ah, that is very good! Well then, I shall take sāli rice for two days, for three days, and for up to seven days.' And he took sāli rice for two days, for three days, and for up to seven days.

"Then a certain being said to that being, 'Hey, being, come, let us go for sāli rice.' That being replied, 'Hey, being, look to your own sāli rice. I have already taken sāli rice for two days, for three, and for up to seven days.' Then that being thought, 'Ah, that is good! Ah, that is very good! Well then, I shall take sāli rice for a fortnight and for a month.' And he took sāli rice for a fortnight and for a month.

"From the time those beings ate the unplowed and unsown sāli rice by storing up the grain for their use, the grains of that sāli rice became covered with husk, chaff and bran. What was reaped did not grow back, so the reaping could be seen, and the sāli rice came to be enclosed in husks or in stalk sheaths.

"Then those beings assembled and gathered and said, 'Friends, formerly we had mind-made forms, perfect, with unimpaired faculties and nothing lacking, possessed of all our limbs, major and minor. We abode with a fine complexion, were self-luminous, and moved through the sky. We fed on joy, and on the food of joy we lived long and remained for a long time. Then the essence of the earth appeared for us, perfect in color, perfect in fragrance and perfect in taste. We ate the essence of the earth mouthful by mouthful, and when we ate the essence of the earth mouthful by mouthful, roughness and heaviness entered our bodies. The radiance of our fine complexions faded, and so darkness arose in the world. We ate it, and by taking it as food we lived long and remained for a long time. Among us, whoever ate very little of the food kept a fine complexion, and whoever ate a great deal of the food took on a poor complexion. Because of the two measures of food, two kinds of complexion appeared; and because there were two kinds of complexion, being looked down on being, saying, "Hey, being, I have a good complexion, and you have a poor complexion." Because we who were proud of our complexions took up those very evil, nonvirtuous things, the essence of the earth vanished.

"'When the essence of the earth vanished, the fat appeared, perfect in color, perfect in fragrance and perfect in taste. We ate it, and by taking it as food we lived long and remained for a long time. Among us, whoever ate very little of the food kept a good complexion, and whoever ate a great deal of the food took on a poor complexion. Because of the two measures of food, two kinds of complexion appeared; and because there were two kinds of complexion, being looked down on being, saying, "Hey, being, I have a good complexion, and you have a poor complexion." Because we who were proud of our complexions took up those very evil, nonvirtuous things, the fat of the earth vanished.

"'When the fat of the earth vanished, the grove of shoots appeared, perfect in color, perfect in fragrance and perfect in taste. We ate it, and by taking it as food we lived long and remained for a long time. Among us, whoever ate very little of the food kept a good complexion, and whoever ate a great deal of the food took on a poor complexion, and two kinds of complexion appeared.

"'Because there were two kinds of complexion, being treated being with contempt, saying, "Hey, being, I have a good complexion, and you have a poor complexion." Because we who were proud of our complexions took up those very evil, nonvirtuous things, the grove of shoots vanished.

"'When the grove of shoots vanished, there grew sāli rice, unplowed and unsown, with grains four finger-widths long, without husk, without chaff, without bran, pure and clean. What was reaped in the evening grew again and ripened by morning; what was reaped in the morning grew again and ripened by evening. Because it grew back again however often it was reaped, no sign of reaping could be seen. We ate it, and by taking it as food we lived long and remained for a long time.

"'From the time we began to store up the unplowed and unsown sāli rice grain and to consume it, the grain of that sāli became covered with husk, chaff and bran. However often it was reaped, it did not grow back, so the reaping could be seen, and the sāli came to stand in clumps, in sheaths on its stalks. So we said, "Come, let us gather and assemble, measure out the fields, mark their limits, and set up boundary markers, saying, 'This is yours, and this is mine.'" They gathered and assembled, divided the fields, and set up boundary markers at their limits.

"'Gautamas, this was the first beginning in the world of the work of setting up boundary markers. That too came about through Dharma, not through what is contrary to Dharma; therefore, in the Dharma of the Lord of Victors, this is the supreme Dharma.

"'Then one being, though he had sāli of his own, took another's sāli that had not been given. Other beings saw that this being, though he had sāli of his own, had taken another's sāli that had not been given. When they saw it, they said to that being, "Hey, being, why do you take another's sāli that has not been given when you have sāli of your own?" They dragged him this way and that, and at length led him into the midst of the assembled people, and reviled him, saying, "Friends, three times now this being, though he has sāli of his own, has taken another's sāli that was not given."

"'Then those beings said to that being, "Hey, being, why do you take another's sāli that has not been given when you have sāli of your own? Hey, being, go, and from now on do not do that."

"'Then that being said to those beings, "Friends, over sāli this being dragged me this way and that, at length even brought me out into the midst of the assembly, and reviled me." Then those beings said to that being, "Hey, being, why did you drag a being this way and that over sāli, at length even bring him out into the midst of the assembly, and revile him? Hey, being, go, and from now on do not do that."

"'Then those beings thought, "Friends, we see people dragged this way and that over sāli, and at length reviled in the midst of the assembly. Come, let us gather and assemble, and appoint as lord of our fields whichever being among us is most handsome in form, most pleasing to behold, most beautiful, and most renowned for great merit. He will punish those of us who are to be punished and will benefit those who are to be benefited, and from whatever grows in our fields we will give him a share of the harvest in accordance with Dharma." So they gathered and assembled, and appointed as lord of the fields whichever of them was most handsome in form, most pleasing to behold, most beautiful, and most renowned for great merit, saying, "Hey, being, come here. Punish those among us who are to be punished, and benefit those who are to be benefited. From whatever grows in our fields we will give you a share of the harvest in accordance with Dharma."

"'He punished those among them who were to be punished and benefited those who were to be benefited, and they offered him a share of the harvest from whatever grew in their fields, in accordance with Dharma. Because he was honored by a great multitude of people, honored by a great multitude of people, he became known as Mahāsammata, the one honored by many. Because, having become lord of those fields, he protected them from harm, he became known as a kṣatriya, a kṣatriya. Because he delighted the people with Dharma, and lived practicing moral discipline and practicing wisdom, he became known as a king, a king.

"'Gautamas, in the time of King Mahāsammata, people were called "beings, beings." Gautamas, the son of King Mahāsammata was called Roca. Gautamas, in the time of King Roca, people were called "come here, come here." The son of Roca was called Kalyāṇa. Gautamas, in the time of King Kalyāṇa, people were called "the marked ones, the marked ones." Gautamas, the son of Kalyāṇa was called Varakalyāṇa. Gautamas, in the time of King Varakalyāṇa, people were called "cloud-throats, cloud-throats." The son of Varakalyāṇa was called Upoṣadha. Gautamas, in the time of King Upoṣadha, people were called "palm-trunks, palm-trunks."

"'On the crown of King Upoṣadha's head there grew a swelling, smooth and exceedingly soft, like a tuft of tree cotton or a tuft of cotton wool, which caused him not the slightest pain. When it ripened and burst open, there appeared a boy, handsome, pleasing to behold, beautiful, fully adorned with the thirty-two major marks of a great being. Because he was born from the crown of the head, he was called Mūrdhaja, Born from the Crown.

"'As soon as the prince was born, he was carried among King Upoṣadha's sixty thousand queens, and milk flowed from the breasts of them all. Each one said, "Suck from me, suck from me," and so he became known as Māndhātṛ, Suck-from-Me.

"'Gautamas, at the time when King Māndhātṛ was reigning, people would think, weigh things up, and examine them closely. Having thought, weighed and closely examined, they took up the various crafts and the various occupations, and for that reason they became known as "mind-born (mānavas), mind-born."

"'Gautamas, these six kings had an immeasurable span of life, a lifespan beyond reckoning.

"'Gautamas, on King Māndhātṛ's right thigh there grew a swelling, smooth and exceedingly soft, like a tuft of tree cotton or a tuft of cotton wool, which caused him not the slightest pain. When it ripened and burst open, there appeared a boy, handsome, pleasing to behold, beautiful, fully adorned with the thirty-two major marks of a great being. Because he was born from the right thigh, he was named Cāru, Cāru. Because that prince had great miraculous power and great might, he too came to wield sovereign lordship over the four continents.

"'Gautamas, on King Cāru's left thigh there grew a swelling, smooth and exceedingly soft, like a tuft of tree cotton or a tuft of cotton wool, which caused him not the slightest pain. When it ripened and burst open, there appeared a boy, handsome, pleasing to behold, beautiful, fully adorned with the thirty-two major marks of a great being. Because he was born from the left thigh, he was named Upacāru, Upacāru. Because that prince had great miraculous power and great might, he too came to wield sovereign lordship over three continents.

"'Gautamas, on King Upacāru's right foot there grew a swelling, smooth and exceedingly soft, like a tuft of tree cotton or a tuft of cotton wool, which caused him not the slightest pain. When it ripened and burst open, there appeared a boy, handsome, pleasing to behold, beautiful, fully adorned with the thirty-two major marks of a great being. Because he was born from the right foot, he was named Cārumat, Cārumat. Because that prince had great miraculous power and great might, he too came to wield sovereign lordship over two continents.

"'Gautamas, on King Cārumat's left foot there grew a swelling, smooth and exceedingly soft, like a tuft of tree cotton or a tuft of cotton wool, which caused him not the slightest pain. When it ripened and burst open, there appeared a boy, handsome, pleasing to behold, beautiful, fully adorned with the thirty-two major marks of a great being. Because he was born from the left foot, he was named Upacārumat, Upacārumat. Because that prince had great miraculous power and great might, he too came to wield sovereign lordship over one continent.

"'Gautamas, thus the son of King Mahāsammata was Roca. The son of Roca was Kalyāṇa. The son of Kalyāṇa was Varakalyāṇa. The son of Varakalyāṇa was Upoṣadha. The son of Upoṣadha was Māndhātṛ. Māndhātṛ's son was Cāru. Cāru's son was Upacāru. Upacāru's sons were Cārumat, Upacārumat, Bhadra, Subhadra, Relinquisher, Relinquisher-Holder, Aṅga, Aṅga-Chariot, Evil-Shunner, Bhāgīrathī, Sāgara, Son of Sāgara, Mahāsāgara, Śakuni, Mahāśakuni, Kuśa, Upakuśa, Mahākuśa, Sudarśana, Mahāsudarśana, Well-Attained, Great Well-Attained, Praṇāda, Mahāpraṇāda, Light-Maker, Hero, Possessed of Meru, Having Meru, Ray, Rayed, Having Rays, and All-Around Ray.

"'Gautamas, in the line of the sons, grandsons, great-grandsons and great-great-grandsons of All-Around Ray, a full hundred kings arose in the land of Potala. The last of them was a king called Arindama; because he tamed his enemies, he was called Arindama, Arindama, the Tamer of Foes.

"'Gautamas, in the line of the sons, grandsons, great-grandsons and great-great-grandsons of King Arindama, fifty-four thousand kings arose in the land of Ayodhyā. The last of them was a king called Victor over the Unvanquished; because he was victorious over those who had never been vanquished, he became known as Victor over the Unvanquished, Victor over the Unvanquished.

"'Gautamas, in the line of the sons, grandsons, great-grandsons and great-great-grandsons of King Victor over the Unvanquished, thirty-six thousand kings arose in the land of Vārāṇasī. The last of them was a king called Hard to Bear.

"'Gautamas, in the line of the sons, grandsons, great-grandsons and great-great-grandsons of King Hard to Bear, eighty-four thousand kings arose in the city of Kāmpilya. The last of them was a king called Brahmadatta.

"'Gautamas, in the line of the sons, grandsons, great-grandsons and great-great-grandsons of King Brahmadatta, thirty-two thousand kings arose in the city of Hastināpura. The last of them was a king called Elephant-Given.

"'Gautamas, in the line of the sons, grandsons, great-grandsons and great-great-grandsons of King Elephant-Given, five thousand kings arose in the land of Takṣaśilā. The last of them was a king called Woolen-Robed.

"'Gautamas, in the line of the sons, grandsons, great-grandsons and great-great-grandsons of King Woolen-Robed, thirty-two thousand kings arose in the city of Lying-on-the-Breast. The last of them was a king called Tamer of Great Elephants.

"'Gautamas, in the line of the sons, grandsons, great-grandsons and great-great-grandsons of King Tamer of Great Elephants, thirty-two thousand kings arose in the city of Victor over the Unvanquished. The last of them was a king called Jayadatta.

"'Gautamas, in the line of the sons, grandsons, great-grandsons and great-great-grandsons of King Jayadatta, thirty-two thousand kings arose in the city of Kanyakubja. The last of them was a king called Jayasena.

"'Gautamas, in the line of the sons, grandsons, great-grandsons and great-great-grandsons of King Jayasena, eighteen thousand kings arose in the city of Campā. The last of them was a king called Nāgadeva.

"'Gautamas, in the line of the sons, grandsons, great-grandsons and great-great-grandsons of King Nāgadeva, twenty-five thousand kings arose in the city of Palm Garland. The last of them was a king called Naradeva.

"'Gautamas, in the line of the sons, grandsons, great-grandsons and great-great-grandsons of King Naradeva, twelve thousand kings arose in the city of Tamala. The last of them was a king called Sāgaradeva.

"'Gautamas, in the line of the sons, grandsons, great-grandsons and great-great-grandsons of King Sāgaradeva, eighteen thousand kings arose in the city of Dantapura. The last of them was a king called Sumati.

"'Gautamas, in the line of the sons, grandsons, great-grandsons and great-great-grandsons of King Sumati, twenty-five thousand kings arose in the city of Rājagṛha. The last of them was a king called Dispeller of Darkness.

"'Gautamas, in the line of the sons, grandsons, great-grandsons and great-great-grandsons of King Dispeller of Darkness, a full hundred kings arose, again in the city of Vārāṇasī. The last of them was a king called Maheśvarasena.
