"'Gautamas, in the line of the sons, grandsons, great-grandsons and great-great-grandsons of King Maheśvarasena, eighty-four thousand kings arose in the city of Kuśāvatī. The last of them was a king called Sāgarasena.

"'Gautamas, in the line of the sons, grandsons, great-grandsons and great-great-grandsons of King Sāgarasena, a thousand kings arose, again in the city of Potala. The last of them was a king called Practitioner of Austerities.

"'Gautamas, in the line of the sons, grandsons, great-grandsons and great-great-grandsons of King Practitioner of Austerities, eighty-four thousand kings arose, again in the city of Kuśāvatī. The last of them was a king called Face of the Earth.

"'Gautamas, in the line of the sons, grandsons, great-grandsons and great-great-grandsons of King Face of the Earth, a hundred thousand kings arose, again in the city of Vārāṇasī. The last of them was a king called Lord of the Earth.

"'Gautamas, in the line of the sons, grandsons, great-grandsons and great-great-grandsons of King Lord of the Earth, a hundred thousand kings arose, again in the city of Ayodhyā. The last of them was one called Held by Kings.

"'Gautamas, in the line of the sons, grandsons, great-grandsons and great-great-grandsons of Held by Kings, eighty-four thousand kings arose in the city of Mithilā. The last of them was a king called Mahādeva.

"'Gautamas, in the line of the sons, grandsons, great-grandsons and great-great-grandsons of King Mahādeva, eighty-four thousand kings, again in the city of Mithilā, practiced the pure conduct of the sages (ṛṣis). The last of them was a king called Nemi.

"'After Nemi came Middling, Dṛḍhanemi, Digger, Near Digger, Possessed of Digging, All-Around Digger, Splendid Appearance, Śrutasena, Dharmasena, Realization, Great Realization, Army of Realization, Aśoka, Vigataśoka, Steadfast Army, Jarāsandha, Fool-Slayer, Dawn, Diśāṃpati, Reṇu, Śaṅkara, Ānanda, Ādarśamukha, Generator, All-Around Generator, Bull among Men, Provider of Food and Drink, Provider of Abundant Food and Drink, Ajita, Aparājita, Abiding, Well-Abiding, Mahābala, Rider of Great Strength, Good Intellect, Steady Rider, Daśadhanu, Śatadhanu, Navatidhanu, Victorious Bow, Citradhanu, Dṛḍhadhanu, Daśaratha, Śataratha, Navatiratha, Citraratha, Victorious Chariot, and Dṛḍharatha.

"'Gautamas, in the line of the sons, grandsons, great-grandsons and great-great-grandsons of King Dṛḍharatha, seventy-seven thousand kings arose in the city of All-Illuminating. The last of them was a king called Kṛkin.

"'At that time there appeared in the world the Teacher, the Tathāgata, the arhat, the perfectly and fully awakened Buddha, perfect in knowledge and conduct, the Sugata, knower of the world, unsurpassed guide of persons to be tamed, teacher of gods and humans, the Buddha, the Blessed One called Kāśyapa. In his presence the Blessed One, as a bodhisattva, made the aspiration to attain awakening in the future and practiced pure conduct, and was born in the company of the Tuṣita gods.

"'Gautamas, the son of King Kṛkin was Sujāta. Gautamas, in the line of the sons, grandsons, great-grandsons and great-great-grandsons of King Sujāta, a full hundred kings arose, again in the city of Potala. The last of them was a king called Karṇa.

"'Gautamas, King Karṇa had two sons, Gautama and Bharadvāja. Of the two, Gautama delighted in renunciation, and Bharadvāja delighted in kingship. Gautama saw his father ruling the kingdom both in accordance with Dharma and contrary to Dharma, and he thought, "When my father has passed away I will become king, and I too will rule both in accordance with Dharma and contrary to Dharma, and so I will be bound for the hells as a being. If one asks what the time calls for here, I should go forth from home into homelessness." With this thought he went to where King Karṇa was, and when he arrived he touched his head to his feet and made this request: "Father, please know this: out of faith I am going forth from home into homelessness."

"'His father said, "Son, the reason I perform sacrifices, make fire offerings and practice austerities is precisely this: when I have passed away, the kingdom will come into the palm of your hand and you will reign as king. So why would you go forth?"

"'He replied, "Father, I cannot bear to rule the kingdom both in accordance with Dharma and contrary to Dharma. So please know this: I am going forth."

"'Then the king, realizing that he could not be dissuaded, gave his permission.

"'At that time a sage (ṛṣi) called Kṛṣṇavarṇa was living in a hermitage. Prince Gautama, having been given leave by the king, was glad and delighted, and vast joy and happiness arose in him. He went to where the sage Kṛṣṇavarṇa was, and when he arrived he bowed at his feet with gentle demeanor and said, "Great sage, I wish to go forth. Please let me go forth." And Kṛṣṇavarṇa let him go forth. The sage Kṛṣṇavarṇa lived on fruits and roots and drank water, and so did Gautama, and he became known as the sage Gautama, the sage Gautama.

"'Some time later King Karṇa died, and Prince Bharadvāja came to wield sovereign lordship over his father's kingdom.

"'Some time later the sage Gautama said to his preceptor, "Preceptor, I cannot sustain myself with food in this place in the wilderness, so I shall live on the outskirts of a town."

"'His preceptor said, "Son, very well. Whether a sage lives in a town or in the wilderness, in short what matters is guarding the senses. Go, build a hut of leaves near Potala, and stay there."

"'Saying, "Preceptor, I will do as you say," the sage Gautama built a hut of leaves near Potala and stayed there.

"'At that time there lived in the city of Potala a courtesan called Bhadrā and a rogue called Mṛṇāla. He sent her clothes and ornaments so that they might enjoy themselves together. She had put on the clothes and ornaments and was setting out, when another man arrived carrying five hundred kārṣāpaṇas and said, "Bhadrā, come here and enjoy yourself with me." She thought, "If I go, I will not get the five hundred kārṣāpaṇas, and to turn away someone who has come to my house and go elsewhere would not be good." So she said to her maidservant, "Go to Mṛṇāla and tell him from your lady: 'For the moment I have no opportunity; I will come later.'" The girl went to him and told him.

"'That other man had a great deal of business to see to, so after enjoying himself with her he left in the very first watch of the night. She thought, "There is still a long time until dawn, so I can still keep his good will." So she again instructed the girl: "Go to Mṛṇāla and say from your lady: 'I have made myself ready; to which pleasure grove shall I come?'" The girl went to him and said this, and he said, "A moment ago your lady had no opportunity, and a moment later she has an opportunity?"

"'The girl was not on good terms with her mistress, and she said, "Son of my lord, it is not that she had no opportunity. Wearing your clothes and ornaments, she was enjoying herself with another man."

"'The attachment that had bound him fell away, and in its place the bond of malice arose. Seized by rage, he said, "Girl, go and tell Bhadrā that Mṛṇāla says, 'Come to such-and-such a pleasure grove.'" She went and told Bhadrā, and Bhadrā then went to the pleasure grove. The rogue Mṛṇāla said, "Is it right for you to enjoy yourself with another man wearing my clothes and ornaments?"

"'She said, "Son of my lord, I did wrong in that. But women are always full of faults, so please forgive me."

"'Then, seized by rage, he drew his sword from its sheath and killed her. The maidservant raised a great outcry: "My lady has been killed! My lady has been killed!" A great crowd of people heard it and came running. Now it so happened that the sage Gautama was staying in that very place. The rogue Mṛṇāla, terrified, threw the bloodstained sword down in front of the sage Gautama and slipped in among that very crowd of people.

"'When that great crowd of people saw the bloodstained sword, they said, "This renunciant has killed Bhadrā!" Then they surrounded the sage Gautama and, seized with rage, said, "Hey, renunciant, while bearing the banner of a sage, do you do a deed like this?"

"'He said, "What have I done?"

"'They said, "You enjoyed yourself with Bhadrā and then killed her."

"'He said, "Because my evil has been stilled, I do not do such deeds." But although he spoke calmly, that crowd of people bound his arms tightly behind his back and handed him over to the king, saying, "Your Majesty, this renunciant enjoyed himself with Bhadrā and then killed her."

"'Since kings do not examine things, he said, "If that is what happened, I give up this renunciant. Impale him on a stake."

"'They hung a garland of karavīra flowers around that renunciant's neck, and surrounded by men wearing blue garments and bearing weapons in their hands, he was proclaimed by public announcement along the highways, in the marketplaces, at the crossroads and at the junctions of three roads. He was led out through the southern gate of the city and impaled on a stake while still alive.

"'His preceptor Kṛṣṇavarṇa used to visit his hermitage from time to time. Some while later he went there and did not find him, and he began to search for him here and there. At length he saw him impaled on the stake, and in a faltering voice, his eyes filled with tears, he said slowly, in words of compassion, "Alas, son, what is this?"

"'He too, in a faltering voice, despairing, tormented by the harmful feelings that had arisen in his vital points, said, "Preceptor, what else could it be? It is karma."

"'His preceptor said, "Son, have you not done some wound or some harm?"

"'"Preceptor, my body has been wounded, but not my mind."

"'"Son, how can that be known?"

"'"Preceptor, I shall make a declaration of truth. Listen. By the truth and by the words of truth that my body has been wounded but not my mind, by that truth and those words of truth, may the dark color of my preceptor's skin turn to the color of gold!"

"'Because that great being had cultivated superior resolve, as soon as he had spoken these words the dark skin of the sage Kṛṣṇavarṇa disappeared and turned the color of gold. The news spread everywhere: "The sage Kṛṣṇavarṇa has turned the color of gold!" And so he came to be named Golden-Hued. It was most wonderful.

"'Then the sage Gautama said, "Preceptor, when I die and pass from here, where will I go? Where will I be born? What will my next life be?"

"'His preceptor said, "Son, the brahmins say that for one without a son there is no good destiny. Have you produced any son at all?"

"'"Preceptor, when my father urged me toward kingship, I went forth while still a youth, and I have not even known the touch of a woman. How then could I have had a son born to me?"

"'"Son, if that is so, call to mind the sensual pleasures you enjoyed in the past."

"'"Preceptor, I am racked by fierce feelings. Now my vital points are severed and my joints are coming apart, and I can think of nothing but dying. How could I call to mind the sensual pleasures I enjoyed in the past?"

"'His preceptor had attained the higher knowledges, so by his miraculous power he conjured up a great storm of wind and rain, and the raindrops struck Gautama's body. Then, as the touch of the rain and the cool wind subdued his suffering, he began to call to mind the sensual pleasures he had enjoyed in the past. When he called to mind the desire of sexual union, two drops of semen fell, together with blood.

"'There are four domains that are inconceivable: thinking about the self, thinking about the world, thinking about the karmic maturation of the actions of beings, and thinking about the domain of the buddhas, the buddha domain.

"'Those two drops of semen turned into two eggs, and at the time of sunrise they were ripened by the rays of the sun and burst open. Two boys came forth, and the two of them went into a thicket of sugarcane not at all far from there. Then the sun's rays grew very hot, and the sage Gautama, tormented by the rays of the sun, died.

"'Then the sage Golden-Hued came and saw that he was dead. When he looked around the stake, he found two broken eggshells lying there. He followed the tracks this way and that into the sugarcane thicket, and there at length he saw two boys. When he set himself to thinking whose sons these two were, he realized that they were the sons of the sage Gautama. Then a special affection arose in him, and he took the two of them to the hermitage and raised them.

"'He gave the two of them names. Because they were born when, at sunrise, they had been ripened by the rays of the sun, they were of the solar lineage, and so they were called "Solar Lineage, Solar Lineage." Because they were the sons of the sage Gautama, their second name was "Gautama, Gautama." Because they were born from his own limbs, their third name was "Āṅgirasa, Āṅgirasa." Because they were found in a sugarcane thicket, their fourth name was "Ikṣvāku, Ikṣvāku."

"'Some time later King Bharadvāja died without a son. The ministers assembled and began to deliberate: "Friends, whom shall we empower as king?"

"'Others said, "His elder brother Gautama went forth among the sages. Since this kingdom has come down in his family line, let us empower him."

"'Having deliberated, they went to the sage Golden-Hued, and when they arrived they touched their heads to his feet and said, "Great sage, where is Gautama?"

"'He said, "You yourselves killed him."

"'They said, "Great sage, we do not even remember having seen him. How could we have killed him?"

"'"I will remind you. Make no mistake." When he had reminded them, they said, "Great sage, if that is so, let that evildoer be; his very name is not to be spoken aloud."

"'"What evil deeds did he do?"

"'"This and this."

"'"That faultless man, who did no harm, did no evil deed, and you killed him."

"'"How is that evident?" He told them in detail how it had happened, and they, growing distressed, said, "Great sage, if that is so, then he did no wrong; it is we who committed an evil deed."

"'As they were saying this, the two boys also came into the sage's presence. The ministers said, "Great sage, whose are these two boys?"

"'He said, "They are that very man's sons."

"'They said, "How were these two born? What are their names?"

"'He told them in detail, together with all that had happened. When the ministers heard it, they were filled with the greatest wonder. They petitioned the sage, and of the two they consecrated the elder prince with the consecration of kingship. Having been consecrated, he too died without a son. Then they consecrated the other, the younger, and his name became King Ikṣvāku.

"'Gautamas, in the line of King Ikṣvāku's sons, grandsons, great-grandsons and great-great-grandsons, a hundred King Ikṣvākus arose in the city of Potala, and the last of them was the one called King Ikṣvāku Virūḍhaka.

"'Gautamas, King Ikṣvāku Virūḍhaka had four sons: Ulkāmukha, Karakarṇin, Hastinika and Nūpura.

"'At another time his chief queen died, and he sat with his hand to his cheek, brooding. The ministers said, "Your Majesty, why do you sit with your face in your hand, displeased?"

"'He said, "My chief queen has died. Why should I not sit brooding?"

"'"Your Majesty, if that is so, the kings of the world have daughters. Why not seek a chief queen for Your Majesty?"

"'The king said, "These princes long for the kingship, and while there are these rivals, who would give a daughter?"

"'"Let Your Majesty consider it, and we shall search."

"'In time the men discovered that another king had a daughter who was beautiful, handsome and lovely, fit to be a chief queen. They went to that king, and when they had made themselves known to him through one intermediary after another, the king commanded, "Bring them in."

"'Then they went before the king, touched his two feet, made conversation at first, and sat down. Finding an occasion in the conversation and seeing the king's face, they said, "May Your Majesty be fortunate! May you be fortunate!"

"'The king said, "Gentlemen, what is your request?"

"'"The chief queen of King Ikṣvāku Virūḍhaka has died, and we ask for your daughter for him."

"'The king said, "Since he is the finest of matches, it may be agreed. But let a pledge be made: if my daughter has a son, I will give her provided that he is to be placed in sovereignty over the kingdom."

"'"Your Majesty, it shall be as you command. We shall go and report it to our lord." So they went and told King Ikṣvāku Virūḍhaka what had happened.

"'The king said, "Gentlemen, to pass over the elder princes, who delight in the kingship, and set a younger one on the throne is not fitting."

"'"Your Majesty, nothing is certain in this matter. For now, take the queen, and with her sport, delight and enjoy yourself. Who knows whether she will bear a son, or a daughter, or have no child at all?"

"'The king said, "Gentlemen, if that is so, fetch her today."

"'They went and received her, and in time she was brought with the great splendor and wealth of royalty. The king became enamored of her, and he sported with her, delighted in her and made love with her. As he sported, delighted and made love, at another time the queen conceived. When eight or nine months had passed, she gave birth to a boy who was beautiful, handsome and lovely. After his birth they held a great birth celebration, and in naming him they asked, "What name shall be given to this boy?"

"'The ministers said, "Your Majesty, since even before he was born he has thus been one who delights in the kingship, let the boy's name be Delight in Kingship."

"'The prince Delight in Kingship was entrusted to eight nurses, and so on, as before, up to: he grew quickly, like a lotus in a pond.

"'When he was not placed in the kingdom as heir apparent, his maternal uncle, the king, heard of it and sent him a messenger bearing a letter filled with harsh words: "If you do as you formerly promised and pledged, that is well. If you do not, then wait, with whatever might and prowess you have for overcoming foes. I shall come there and lay waste your realm to dust."

"'Because that king was very mighty, when he heard this he was afraid. He gathered his ministers and said, "Gentlemen, that king has sent me this message. How should I deal with it?"

"'They said, "Your Majesty, consecrate Delight in Kingship as heir apparent."

"'He said, "Gentlemen, to pass over the elder princes, who delight in the kingship, and consecrate a younger one with the kingship is not fitting."

"'They said, "Your Majesty, that king has vast forces and mounts. If he comes here, there is the danger that he will certainly lay waste the country. So consecrate Delight in Kingship to wield the sovereignty of heir apparent, and please banish those princes."

"'The king said, "Gentlemen, how can I banish princes who are faultless and have done no harm?"

"'"Your Majesty, we are ministers, intent on what benefits Your Majesty. We would banish even those without fault and without offense; never mind those with faults who have committed offenses."

"'The king remained in equanimity and sat without saying anything.

"'The ministers gathered and began to deliberate among themselves: "Gentlemen, we must devise a means by which the king will become displeased with the princes." So they had the pleasure garden cleaned, set out very fragrant incense in places painted with all kinds of designs, hung up many silk streamers, and had it made delightful with all kinds of flowers.

"'Then those headstrong princes, seeing from far off the ministers cleaning that pleasure garden, went toward the pleasure garden. When the ministers had finished cleaning the pleasure garden and came out to the gate, the princes asked, "Whose pleasure garden is this?"

"'They said, "It is His Majesty's."

"'As the princes started to turn back, the ministers said, "Princes, go inside. Why turn back?"

"'They said, "If it is His Majesty's pleasure garden, how could it be proper to go in here?"

"'The ministers said, "What harm is there, whether for His Majesty or for the princes, in amusing yourselves here?" So they went inside and began to play.

"'The ministers said to the king, "Your Majesty, the pleasure garden has been well cleaned, and the time has come for Your Majesty to view it."

"'The king set out, and in the pleasure garden he heard the sound of an uproar. Hearing it, he said to the ministers, "Gentlemen, since there is the sound of an uproar in the pleasure garden, who is in there?"

"'The ministers said, "Your Majesty, it is the princes."

"'"If that is so, I renounce the princes."

"'The ministers touched his two feet and said, "Your Majesty, please be forbearing, and banish the princes."

"'The king said, "So be it." When it had been decided to banish the princes, they touched the king's two feet and said, "Your Majesty, if that is so, it would be right for you to command that, if we go together with our people and retinue, we are in no way to be prevented."

"'The king gave the command. When they set out, a great host of people from the palace retinue who were fond of them set out together with them, and for seven days the gates of the city of Potala were opened and the crowds of people streamed out. The ministers said to the king, "Your Majesty, if the city gates are left open, before long Potala will become empty."

"'"If that is so, close the gates."

"'Then those princes, taking their own sisters, made their way by stages to a place on the bank of the river Bhāgīrathī, near the Himalaya mountains, not very far from the hermitage of the sage Kapila. There they made huts of leaves and settled. They lived by killing game, and three times a day they would go to the hermitage of the sage Kapila. Gripped by the arrogance of youth and the prime of life, they were sorely tormented by desire, and they became thin, sickly, pale and gaunt.

"'At length, at another time, the sage Kapila saw them in this state and asked, "Why have you become pale and gaunt?"

"'They said, "Great sage, it is because we are sorely tormented by sensual desire."

"'He said, "Leave aside each your own sister, and live with your half-sisters born of other mothers."

"'"Great sage, is it permissible for us to do such a thing?"

"'"Gentlemen, it is permissible for those of royal caste who have thus lost their kingdom."

"'Then, thinking, "The words of the sage are authoritative," and being tormented by sensual desire, they were filled with joy and delight, and they sported, delighted and made love with their half-sisters born of other mothers. As they sported, delighted and made love with them, sons and daughters were born, and these grew up. Because noise is the thorn of meditative concentration, the sage Kapila could not attain one-pointedness of mind, and he said, "Gentlemen, I have seen enough. I shall go elsewhere."

"'"Great sage, why?"

"'"Because noise is the thorn of meditative concentration, I cannot attain one-pointedness of mind."

"'"Great sage, stay right here. We will go elsewhere. But assign us some piece of ground."

"'"Gentlemen, very well."

"'Because that great-souled sage was one whose wishes were fulfilled just as he desired, he took a golden water jar and traced the outline of a city with a stream of water, and they built the city. Because the sage Kapila gave up the site entirely for their dwelling, it came to be called Kapila's Site, Kapilavastu.

"'They flourished there and became a great multitude, but because it was cramped, they could not grow any further. The gods, knowing their minds with their own minds, showed them another place, and they went there and built another city, which came to be called Shown by the Gods.

"'Then they assembled and gathered together and began to deliberate among themselves: "Gentlemen, the reason we were banished is that we did not take wives from those of equal birth. Therefore none of us should take a wife who is not of equal birth, and we should be content with one alone." From then on they took a wife only from a single family of equal birth, and they did not take co-wives.

"'Then, at another time, King Virūḍhaka remembered his beloved sons and said to the ministers, "Ho, citizens, where are those princes now?"

"'They said, "When Your Majesty banished them for some offense, they took their own sisters and went away from here. Near the Himalaya, on the bank of the river Bhāgīrathī, not very far from the hermitage of the sage Kapila, they settled. Leaving aside each their own sister, they sport, delight and make love with their half-sisters born of other mothers. As they sport, delight and make love, sons and daughters have been born."

"'"Citizens, did the princes dare to do such a thing?"

"'"Your Majesty, they were capable of it."

"'Then King Ikṣvāku Virūḍhaka straightened his upper body, stretched out his right hand, and uttered this inspired utterance: "Ah, the princes are capable! Ah, the princes are truly capable!" Because a being of great power had spoken these words, "Ah, the princes are capable! Ah, the princes are truly capable!", they came to be called Śākyas, the Śākyas.

"'At another time King Ikṣvāku Virūḍhaka died, and they consecrated Delight in Kingship in the kingship. He too died without a son, and Ulkāmukha was made to wield the sovereignty of the kingdom. He too died without a son, and Karakarṇin became king. He too died without a son, and Hastinika became king. He too died without a son, and Nūpura became king. His son was Abode Founder. Gautamas, the son of Abode Founder was Earth House.

"'Gautamas, in the line of King Earth House's sons, grandsons, great-grandsons and great-great-grandsons, fifty-five thousand kings arose in the city of Kapilavastu. The last of them were Daśaratha, Śataratha, Navatiratha, Citraratha, Victorious Chariot, Dṛḍharatha, Daśadhanu, Śatadhanu, Navatidhanu, Victorious Bow, Citradhanu and Dṛḍhadhanu.

"'Gautamas, Dṛḍhadhanu had two sons: Siṃhahanu and Siṃhanāda. Gautamas, of all the archers there were in Jambudvīpa, Siṃhahanu was renowned as the foremost.

"'Gautamas, Siṃhahanu had four sons: Śuddhodana, Śuklodana, Droṇodana and Amṛtodana; and daughters: Śuddhā, Śuklā, Droṇā and Amṛtā.

"'Śuddhodana had two sons: the Blessed One and the venerable Nanda. Śuklodana had two sons: the venerable Tiṣya and the Śākya king Bhadrika. Droṇodana had two sons: Mahānāma and the venerable Aniruddha. Amṛtodana had two sons: the venerable Ānanda and Devadatta.

"'The son of Śuddhā was Suprabuddha. The son of Śuklā was Garlanded. The son of Droṇā was Taker of Good. The son of Amṛtā was Increase of Virtue.

"'The son of the Blessed One is Rāhula. Gautamas, the line of Mahāsammata culminates in the venerable Rāhula. The stream of existence is cut off, saṃsāra is exhausted, and now there is no further becoming.'"

Then the Blessed One, knowing that the venerable Mahāmaudgalyāyana's discourse was complete, rose and sat up. Having sat up, he said to the venerable Mahāmaudgalyāyana, "Excellent, Maudgalyāyana, excellent! Maudgalyāyana, it was excellent of you to give this Dharma discourse before the monks concerning the ancient lineage of the Śākyas. Maudgalyāyana, again and again you should give this Dharma discourse concerning the ancient lineage of the Śākyas, for it will bring them long-lasting benefit, help and happiness."

Then the Blessed One said to the monks, "Monks, take up this Dharma discourse concerning the ancient lineage of the Śākyas. Retain it. Recite it. Why is that? Monks, the Dharma discourse concerning the ancient lineage of the Śākyas is meaningful, in accord with the Dharma, and so on, up to: connected with the holy life. Monks, sons of good family who have gone forth out of faith should retain and recite the Dharma discourse concerning the ancient lineage of the Śākyas."

Then the Śākyas of Kapilavastu delighted in what the Blessed One had said and rejoiced in it. They bowed their heads at the Blessed One's feet and departed from the Blessed One's presence.

At that time, in the city of Kapilavastu, a king called Siṃhahanu held the kingship. The kingdom was prosperous, flourishing, peaceful and blessed with good harvests, and filled with many folk and people. It was free of fighting, strife, quarrels, disputes and robbers, and illnesses had been fully quelled. It was rich in rice, rows of sugarcane, cattle and buffalo, and he ruled the kingdom as one cherishes an only son.

In the city of Shown by the Gods, a king called Suprabuddha held the kingship. The kingdom was prosperous, flourishing, peaceful and blessed with good harvests, and filled with many folk and people. It was free of fighting, strife, quarrels, disputes and robbers, and illnesses had been fully quelled. It was rich in rice, rows of sugarcane, cattle and buffalo, and he ruled the kingdom as one cherishes an only son.

King Suprabuddha had a chief queen, beautiful, handsome and lovely, the fairest lady of the land, called Lumbinī.

In Shown by the Gods there lived a householder who was rich, of great wealth and great means, with vast and extensive possessions, possessing the wealth of Vaiśravaṇa, rivaling Vaiśravaṇa in wealth. His park was rich in flowers, rich in fruits and rich in water, and with all kinds of birds calling, that park was delightful. From time to time the king would go there with his retinue of queens and enjoy pleasant amusements there.

When Lumbinī saw it, she conceived a desire for it, and she said, "Your Majesty, please grant me this park."

The king said, "Since this park belongs to the householder, how can I give it to you? If you need a park, I will have another made for you that is finer than this one."

Then King Suprabuddha had a park made for Lumbinī that was far superior to that one, and its name came to be Lumbinī Grove.

For a long time King Siṃhahanu had held this thought: "Ah, how wonderful it would be if a wheel-turning monarch were born in my line!" And for a long time King Suprabuddha too had held this thought: "Ah, how wonderful it would be if I became related by marriage to Siṃhahanu!"

In time, as he sported, delighted and made love with his queen, at another time the queen conceived. When eight or nine months had passed, she gave birth to a daughter who was beautiful, handsome and lovely, complete in all her limbs and features. At the beauty of the newborn child, King Suprabuddha, together with his retinue of queens, and the crowds of people living in Shown by the Gods were filled with the greatest wonder. They fell into doubt and thought, "Is this a girl, or is it an illusion conjured up by various karmic forces?"

They held a great birth celebration for three weeks, twenty-one days, and in naming her they asked, "What name shall be given to this girl?"

The ministers said, "Among the crowds of people living in Shown by the Gods, talking on the highways, in the marketplaces, at the junctions of three roads and at the crossroads, some say, 'She is simply a girl, who through the ripening of her former karma has become beautiful and perfect in complexion.' Others say, 'She is not a girl. What then? She is an illusion conjured up by various karmic forces.' Therefore let the girl be given the name Māyā, Illusion." And so she was named.

The girl Māyā was entrusted to eight nurses, and so on, as before, up to: she grew up. The soothsayers who read the marks prophesied of her, "She will give birth to one endowed with many excellent marks, who will become a wheel-turning monarch by force."

Again, as he sported, delighted and made love, a daughter was born who was beautiful, handsome and lovely, of fine complexion, endowed with the most perfect development. When she was born, that entire city was lit up with a vast radiance, and just as with Māyā, her beauty could not be fully gauged in every respect. Again they held a great birth celebration, and in naming her they asked, "What name shall be given to this girl?"

The ministers said, "Since she is even more beautiful than Māyā, let her be given the name Mahāmāyā, Great Illusion." And so she was named.

She too was raised and nurtured until she grew up, and the soothsayers who read the marks prophesied of her, "She will give birth to one endowed with the thirty-two marks of a great being, and he will become a wheel-turning king."

King Suprabuddha sent a messenger to King Siṃhahanu: "Two daughters have been born to me, Māyā and Mahāmāyā. Of one it has been prophesied that she will give birth to a son perfect in all the marks, who will become a wheel-turning king by force. Of the other it has been prophesied that she will give birth to a son adorned with the thirty-two marks of a great being, who will become a wheel-turning king. Whichever of these two you wish, take her as a wife for Prince Śuddhodana."

Siṃhahanu sent back word: "The prince would indeed want both. But one should not give two such wives to one man. For now I want the one of whom it was prophesied that she will give birth to a son adorned with the thirty-two marks of a great being, who will become a wheel-turning king. As for the other, I shall have to consult the assembly." So Suprabuddha sent her off with a retinue of five hundred.

At that time there were mountain people called the Pāṇḍavas, enemies of the Śākyas. The Śākyas gathered and went to King Siṃhahanu and said, "Your Majesty, the Pāṇḍava mountain people are doing us harm. Please help us."

He said, "Gentlemen, I am old and cannot do battle with them."

"Your Majesty, please send Prince Śuddhodana."

"Let a pledge be made: I will send him provided that, whatever boon the prince asks, that boon will be granted."

They said, "Your Majesty, as you command; we shall grant it."

The king assigned the fourfold army to Prince Śuddhodana, and he defeated those mountain people, routed them utterly and wiped them out. Then the Śākyas, delighted, went to King Siṃhahanu and said, "Your Majesty, since the prince has defeated the mountain people, routed them utterly and wiped them out, we are overjoyed. Let the prince say what boon he wishes, and we shall grant it."

"Gentlemen, the Śākyas have laid it down as law that no one may take a co-wife, even from those of equal birth."

"Your Majesty, does this mean breaking the law?"

He said, "You should rather keep it all the more strictly, not break it. Yet the one boon the prince asks is to take a co-wife from those of equal birth."

"Your Majesty, it is permissible, so let it be done."

Then Siṃhahanu sent a messenger to Suprabuddha: "I have consulted the assembly, so send the other daughter as well." He sent her too with a retinue of five hundred, and Prince Śuddhodana took them both.

In time, at another time, King Siṃhahanu died, and in Kapilavastu King Śuddhodana took up the kingship. The kingdom was prosperous, flourishing, peaceful and blessed with good harvests, and filled with many folk and people. It was free of fighting, strife, quarrels, disputes and robbers, and illnesses had been fully quelled. It was rich in rice, rows of sugarcane, cattle and buffalo, and he ruled the kingdom as one cherishes an only son.

At another time he went with Queen Mahāmāyā to the upper terrace of the palace, where no men were present, and there, with music, he sported, delighted and made love.

This is the nature of things: when the bodhisattva dwells in the abode of Tuṣita, he surveys the world with five surveys: he surveys the family, he surveys the country, he surveys the time, he surveys the lineage, and he surveys the woman. For the details, connect this with the earlier sūtra.

The Sūtra of the Great Departure is complete.

It was translated, edited and finalized by the Indian preceptor Ācārya Dharmaśrībhadra and the chief editor-translator, the monk Rinchen Zangpo.

This concludes the translation. The following are translator's notes.

In the fourth verse, འབྲས་རྩོལ ("fruit-effort") is read as འབད་རྩོལ, "labor, effort." In the closing prose, ཡིན་ནད་ནི is read as ཡིན་ན། ད་ནི, "and now." The lord of elephants story follows the tradition in which King Prabhāsa first resolved on awakening on seeing an untamable elephant in rut.  Among the six proclaimers, the names rendered Illusion-Crosser (སྒྱུས་རྒལ), Ear-Striker (རྣས་རྡེག) and Endowed with Goodness (བཟང་ལྡན) could not be identified with confidence and are translated by meaning; likewise, among the six attainers of absorption, the brahmin youth All-Victorious (ཀུན་རྒྱལ) and the sage Free of Afflictions (ཉོན་མོངས་མེད་པ). Puṣkarasārin (པད་མའི་སྙིང་པོ) and Lohita (དམར་པོ) are probable identifications with the brahmins known in Pāli as Pokkharasāti and Lohicca.  The tree called མྱ་ངན་འཚང is taken as the aśoka (usually མྱ་ངན་མེད); other versions of the story name a plakṣa or śāla tree. The wanderer མིག་དམར is translated by meaning as Red-Eyes, since no Sanskrit form can be identified with confidence.  "It is the nature of things" renders the recurring formula ཆོས་ཉིད་ཀྱིས (dharmatā), meaning this is what invariably happens at the birth of every bodhisattva. Kauśika is Śakra's clan name, used here in address. The Tibetan ཤ་མ, rendered "membranes," most likely refers to the afterbirth. In the verse on the bath, the nāgas' offering is grammatically to the lineage (or to him, within it); the meaning is that the nāgas honored the bodhisattva's family. རྒྱ་སྤོས is rendered generically as "incense"; the exact substance (possibly tagara) is uncertain.  The kings and princes named here follow the account of the Mūlasarvāstivāda Vinaya: Aranemi Brahmadatta (father of Prasenajit), Mahāpadma (father of Bimbisāra, whose mother was Bimbī), Śatānīka (father of Udayana), and Anantanemi (father of Pradyota). The Tibetan for Brahmadatta's epithet, literally "flying by spokes," is taken as a rendering of Aranemi, "spoke-rimmed"; this identification is probable rather than certain. The name explanations are puns in the Indic originals that do not carry fully into English. The closing sentence is an abbreviation formula indicating that the soothsayers' declaration is to be understood in full as given earlier.  "The lord of riches" renders ནོར་ལྡན, literally "the Wealthy One," taken as an epithet of the god of wealth; the exact figure intended is uncertain. "The sacred verses" renders སྔགས་ཀྱི་ཚིག, here the brahmanical treatises on bodily marks rather than Buddhist mantras. The refrain literally reads "this is the great man's mark of a great man of the prince," repeated after each mark. Some marks are grouped together under a single refrain, so the count of refrains does not equal thirty-two.  The verse line "with few troubles, and wise" is compressed in the Tibetan; it may describe the king's sons rather than the king himself. The verse phrase rendered "born of karma" is literally "arisen from karma, with karma," an awkward doubling in the Tibetan.  The sage's name, ཉོན་མོངས་མེད (literally "Free of Afflictions"), is taken as this text's rendering of Asita, since the episode, including his nephew Naradatta, is the well-known story of Asita's visit to the infant Buddha. The mountain ཀུན་འཛིན is translated literally as "Mount All-Bearing"; it is not identified with confidence, though parallel accounts place Asita on the Vindhya or in the Himalaya. "The deathless" renders བདུད་རྩི (amṛta), meaning nirvāṇa.  The writing master's name, Tibetan "Worm Armor," and the archery master or masters "Good Taker and With the Gods," could not be confidently identified with Sanskrit names in this version of the story, so they are translated literally; the Tibetan may denote either one master or two. The "kettledrums" render an uncertain instrument name. "Fast" renders the poṣadha observance.  The tree called Essence of Virtue is translated literally, since its Sanskrit name could not be confidently identified. ལེགས་པར་རབ་སད is taken as Suprabuddha, the Śākya elder known from the Buddha's family. The phrase "to be told at length" renders an abbreviation formula: the Tibetan text cuts the archery episode short.  The Elephant Pit renders the Tibetan "elephant hollow," known in Sanskrit sources as Hastigarta. The "working estates" render ལས་ཀྱི་མཐའ (Skt. karmānta), the royal farmlands.  ས་འཚོ་མ and རི་དགས་སྐྱེས are taken as Gopikā and Mṛgajā, the bodhisattva's wives known from the Vinaya account. Their fathers' names, the Śākya "Good Time" and the Śākya "Bell Sound," could not be confidently identified with Sanskrit forms and are translated literally. The second group of four verse lines appears to be the narrator's voice rather than Mṛgajā's. Mṛgajā's verse plays on the word nirvṛta, which means both "blissful, at peace" and "having attained nirvāṇa."  In the first verse, the phrase rendered "not set his mind on leaving" (bdag ngor mi gzhug) is obscure; the sense is clearly the king's aim of keeping the prince absorbed in pleasures so that he would not renounce, but the literal wording is uncertain. "What is beneficial" (don) and "what is wholesome" (dge legs) render two items in the list of goods that are close in meaning; the Sanskrit parallels list such terms as artha and kuśala.  In the prophecy verse, ཆད་པ་མེད་པས is rendered "without punishment," the classic description of a wheel-turning monarch who rules without rod or sword, rather than the glossary's doctrinal sense of annihilation. In Mahāprajāpatī's dream, ཕྱག་དར ("sweepings, refuse") and the clause about seeing herself outside weeping and laughing for an instant are rendered literally; the omens are terse and their exact imagery is somewhat uncertain. In Śuklodana's verse, བྲེ་བོ is taken as the measure (droṇa) of liquor drunk; it may also be a wordplay on Droṇodana's name.  In the first of the five dreams, the Tibetan reads only "he made the great one his bed"; "the great earth" is supplied from context and from the parallel accounts of this dream. The grass "called Upright" (འགྲེང་བ) is translated literally because its Sanskrit name cannot be identified with confidence. The clause about the white, black-headed birds ("could be seen up as far as his kneecaps") is terse and its exact sense is uncertain. In the bodhisattva's reply, རྒྱས་བསྐོར is taken as "encircled by a net" (reading rgya, net), the lord of beasts being the lion.  In two verses the Tibetan reads རྒན་ད་འཆི; ད is taken as a slip for ན ("sickness"), giving the standard triad old age, sickness and death. Likewise ད་པར་འགྱུར ("people will ...") is read as སད་པར་འགྱུར, "will wake up." The four sons of the gods are rendered by meaning where the name cannot be confidently identified: "Sprout" (མྱུ་གུ), "Radiant" (འོད་ཟེར་ཅན) and "Leaf-Clad" (ལོ་མ་གྱོན་པ); Maṅkura is a transcription. The phrase མ་སྟེས་ཏེ after Mahānāman's verse to the king is obscure and is rendered by sense ("so he said, but").  The city reached after twelve leagues, དཔེ་མེད, is rendered by meaning as "Peerless," since its Sanskrit name cannot be fixed with confidence. ལུས་ངན is the standard Tibetan rendering of Kubera. In Chandaka's verse the forest is said to be "full of herdsmen" (ཕྱུགས་རྫི), which reads oddly beside lions and tigers but is translated as written.  The sage's name ངན་སྤོང ("one who shuns evil") could not be identified with confidence as the rendering of a known Sanskrit name, so it is translated by meaning as "Shunner of Evil". The phrase སྒྲ་ཅན་གྱི་བྲམ་ཟེ, rendered "a renowned brahmin", is literally "a brahmin possessing sound"; it may instead be a place or clan name. The sentence "From 'In the city of Peerless...' up to 'ten sons were born to him'" is the text's own abbreviation formula, referring to a stock narrative not written out here. The bodhisattva's instruction to Chandaka not to lead Kaṇṭhaka into the palace is given as the Tibetan has it.  In the verse line ending སོ་ག་ཚ་དུས་ཤིང་གི་མི་བཞིན་ནོ, the word མི ("man") makes little sense; the reading མེ ("fire") gives the well-known simile of a fire never sated by fuel, and has been translated as "a fire fed with wood in the scorching heat of summer". In the final verse, དགའ་བདག་ཏུ is taken in the sense "to where it delights", in line with the parallel verse of the Pabbajjā Sutta. བུ་རམ་ཤིང་པ is rendered Ikṣvāku and ཉི་མའི་རུས "the solar clan" (Ādityagotra). The aims of the sages, "to be Śakra, Brahmā, or Māra", are elliptical in the Tibetan ("Śakra himself", and so on).  "The market town of the community of Uruvilvā Kāśyapa" renders ལྟེང་རྒྱས་འོད་སྲུང་གི་སྡེའི་གྲོང་རྡལ literally; it corresponds to the town of Senāni at Uruvilvā in other accounts. In the teachers' invitations, the phrase འདྲ་བར་ཐོས་ཀྱིས is obscure; ཐོས ("heard") has been taken loosely and the sense "we are alike" given. As the Tibetan stands, Udraka himself, rather than his father Rāma, claims the attainment.  རྣམ་པར་བམ་པའི་བསམ་གཏན, literally "the meditations of swelling", corresponds to the breathless meditations (āsphānaka) of the Sanskrit biographies. ཐུགས་ཡོངས་སུ་གཏུགས་ཏེ་མི་གནས is taken in the sense of the familiar formula "the painful feeling did not overpower his mind and remain".  ཆང་ཆུང, here rendered "a mere handful", corresponds to the Pāli formula "a little, by the handful". In the list of single grains, the text reads འབྲས་བུ་གཅིག ("a single fruit"); the parallel list in the following passage has འབྲས་འབྲུ་གཅིག ("a single grain of rice"), which has been followed. The identifications of the pulses (སྲན་གོ་ལ chickpea, རྒྱ་སྲན pea, མོན་སྲན་གྲེའུ lentil, མོན་སྲན་སྡེའུ mung bean) are approximate.  The phrase rendered "pining for him all the more" (ལྷག་པར་དང་ཀ་ན) is obscure; the sense of longing is inferred from context. The line about Yaśodharā's womb reflects the tradition, found in Mūlasarvāstivāda sources, that her pregnancy with Rāhula was arrested during the bodhisattva's six years of austerity; the wording (རུམ་མི་བདེ་བ་དེ་ཉིད་དུ་ཞུ་བ) is terse and the rendering is interpretive. In the second simile the Tibetan reads literally "is (ཡིན) fully brought to an end in their minds and remains," which contradicts the argument (these practitioners still cannot awaken); it is translated according to the evident sense, as still present and not brought to an end, probably a slip for a negated form. The text as it stands gives the same image (wet wood taken out of the water) for both the first and second similes; this is kept as written.  The town སྡེ་ཅན (literally "having an army") is identified with Senānī, the village near Uruvilvā known from parallels (Pāli Senānigama); the identification is likely but not certain. The wandering ascetic ཉེ་འགྲོ is given as Upaga, the standard equivalent of this Tibetan rendering. The name of the medicine (སྨན་མཐུ་བསྐྱེད་པ) is translated by meaning as "Strength-Giving." The phrase ཆོས་གོས་མན་བས་ནས is unclear; "dried his robes" is a contextual guess. The attendants who leave are the five companions of the bodhisattva's austerities, not named in this passage.  The nāga king དུས་ཅན is given as Kālika, the name he bears in Sanskrit accounts of this episode. The grass seller བཀྲ་ཤིས is Svastika, as in the Sanskrit tradition. The phrase rendered "dried your robes" (ཆོས་གོས་མན་བས་པ) is obscure, as in the previous passage, and the rendering is a contextual guess.  The verb in "the Śākyas have been subjugated" (ཁ་བཏག) is uncertain; it may mean they have been overpowered or plundered. The phrase rendered "Your hope ... is vain" (དེ་སྲིད་རེ་སྐན) is idiomatic and the rendering follows the evident sense. In the final sentence "as a bird does upon the ground" reads the evident དཔེར་ན་ས་ལ for the written དཔེར་ནས་ལ, a copyist's slip.  Of Māra's three daughters, སྲེད་མ is the standard rendering of Tṛṣṇā ("Craving"). The other two, དགའ་བྱེད་མ and དགའ་སྤྱོད་མ, do not match the usual Tibetan forms of the familiar names (Rati, Arati, Rāgā) with confidence, so they are translated literally as "Delight-Maker" and "Delight-Enjoyer". In the final section, འདུན་པ is the common noun "desire," not the name Chandaka.  The names explained here depend on their Tibetan meanings: Rāhula is rendered as "Seized by Rāhu," matching the eclipse at his birth, and Ānanda as "Joy," matching the city's rejoicing. The last line of Māra's speech in verse may also be read as "He will surely remove it from you." I have translated སྤྱོད་པ་པ and ཀུན་ཏུ་རྒྱུ as two classes of wandering mendicants, carakas and parivrājakas.  The opening sentence continues from the previous piece: Yaśodharā, distressed by Śuddhodana's doubt, sets her son on a stone. "ཕང་ཚད" is unclear; I have read it as "the size of the bodhisattva's lap," but it might mean the stone's weight or another measure. The food the merchants offer, འདའ་གུ, is uncertain; "barley meal" follows the barley preparation and honey of the parallel accounts of Trapuṣa and Bhallika.  The last verse of the rejoicing is compressed. I read "rnam par mthong" as the clear seeing (insight) that ends suffering, which the generous person "touches" or attains. The second mention of the barley meal is spelled འདེ་གུ, a variant of འདའ་གུ in the previous line, and is taken as the same food.  The illness is called a "chill" (bser ma) in Śakra's speech and a "wind disorder" (rlung nad) in the narration; the text treats them as the same complaint, and both terms are kept.  In the refrain of the opening verses, ཚངས་པ renders Sanskrit brāhmaṇa in its sense of the true brahmin, the accomplished practitioner, as in the parallel Udāna verses; it is not the god Brahmā here. In the lotus simile, the Tibetan says the flowers "rise from the water" and then lists those level with it and those submerged; it is read as the usual threefold division. The word རྗེད in "highest respect" is taken in the sense of honoring or respecting, in line with the parallels in which the teacher honored the Bodhisattva; the form is unusual. The speech beginning "Venerable One, among the beings born in the world" has no stated speaker, but the humble verbs and the request mark it as Brahmā Sahāṃpati's entreaty.  "Yesterday evening" renders མ་དང་སུམ་སྲོད་ལ, an unusual expression read in the light of the following sentence ("died in the evening") and the parallels. The phrase about the seat the five monks planned to offer, སྟན་ནར་མཁོ་ནར་བཏིང་བ, is obscure; it is taken as a seat simply set out, without the courtesy of rising. "The yoga of abandoning suffering" translates the Tibetan literally; it refers to the Bodhisattva's period of ascetic striving, when the five attended him.  In the five monks' reply, ནད་ལྟར is read as ན་ད་ལྟར ("if ... then now"), which the sentence plainly requires. In the eightfold path, ཡང་དག་པའི་དག is read as ཡང་དག་པའི་ངག, "right speech," an evident scribal slip. The unattributed question about the Tathāgata's complexion is the Blessed One's reply to the five monks.  འབབ་ཆུ་གནོད་པ་ཅན, literally "the Harmful River," is taken as the Varaṇā, the river at Vārāṇasī named in the parallel accounts of Yaśas. རིགས་ཀྱི་གཙོ་བོ, "head of the family," is understood as the guild master (śreṣṭhin) who is Yaśas's father in the parallels. The phrase describing the sleeping women, བྱུང་རྒྱལ་དུ་བླ་བ, is obscure; "slack and drooling" follows the sense of the parallel descriptions.  "I have gone beyond; I have gone utterly beyond" renders བདག་ནི་འཕགས་སོ། །མངོན་པར་འཕགས་སོ, the stock exclamation of a new convert (Skt. abhikrānto 'haṃ bhadanta abhikrāntaḥ); here འཕགས is the verb "to go beyond, excel," not the noun "noble one." In the verse, ཆད་པ means punishment or the rod (Skt. daṇḍa, "having laid down the rod toward all beings"), not annihilation, and འབྱུང་པོ means beings in general (Skt. bhūta), not a class of spirits. "Beyond training" and "in training" render མི་སློབ་པ and སློབ་པ (aśaikṣa, śaikṣa): the arhat versus the stream-enterer and other trainees. The father's line reads ཇི་ལྟར ... མངོན་པར་རྟོག་པ, taken as the usual རྟོགས "realized."  ཆུང་མ is rendered "former wife," since Yaśas has by now gone forth as a monk. In the guild master's request, "to where the guild master lives" renders རིགས་ཀྱི་གཙོ་བོ་གང་ན་བ་དེར: the householder is speaking of himself in the third person and inviting the Buddha to his house. ཀྱེ་མའོ ... མཆོག་མ་ཡིན་པ་མ་ཡིན is a double negative ("not not supreme"), rendered "can surely be no ordinary one." The four friends are named with the usual Sanskrit forms: གང་པོ Pūrṇa, དྲི་མེད Vimala, བ་ལང་བདག Gavāṃpati, ལག་བཟངས Subāhu. They are listed as the "second" to "fifth" sons, counting Yaśas as the first to go forth.  "The unsurpassed culmination of the pure life" renders ཚངས་པར་སྤྱོད་པའི་མཐའ་བླ་ན་མེད་པ; མཐའ here is the end or goal (Skt. paryavasāna), not "extreme." "Do not go two of you by one way" renders ཁྱེད་གཉིས་ཤིག་ནི་གཅིག་ཏུ་མ་འགྲོ་ཞིག, the familiar instruction that the monks should not travel in pairs on the same road. In the second account of the fifty youths, the text shortens the stock passage with "as before ... up to" (སྔ་མ་བཞིན་དུ་ཇི་སྲིད), and the translation keeps that shortening.  "The company of sixty Bhadravargīyas" renders དྲུག་ཅུ་སྡེ་བཟང་པོའི་ཚོགས, the "fortunate group" of young men (Skt. bhadravargīya); here སྡེ་བཟང་པོ is a group name, not the merchant Bhallika. "The discipline of abandoning suffering" translates སྡུག་བསྔལ་སྤོང་བའི་རྣལ་འབྱོར literally; it refers to the period of austerities before the awakening, when the village girls fed the Bodhisattva. "Blazing with splendor like a golden pillar" renders གསེར་གྱི་མཆོད་སྡོང, literally a golden sacrificial post (Skt. yūpa).  "Has treated me with disdain" is a tentative rendering of the obscure ཕྱས་སྤྱད in ང་ལྟེང་རྒྱས་འོད་སྲུང་རལ་པ་ཅན་འཁོར་དང་བཅས་པས་ཕྱས་སྤྱད་ཀྱིས, read as ཕྱ "to scorn." "Became a blaze of fire" reads མིར་གྱུར་པ ("became a man"), which makes no sense here, as མེར་གྱུར་པ. In Kāśyapa's lament, ཉོན་མོངས is the everyday "poor wretch," not the technical "affliction." "Matted-hair ascetic" renders རལ་པ་ཅན (Skt. jaṭila).  In the second episode Kāśyapa's thought refers to the "great monk" (dge slong chen po) where everywhere else the text has "great renunciant" (dge sbyong chen po); this is read as a copyist's slip and rendered "great renunciant." Likewise "ma dang sum" is read as "mdang sum" ("last night"), as in the Buddha's reply, and "'od srung cing" as "'od srung ci nga" ("Kāśyapa, do I ...?"), which the following denial requires.  In the Brahmā episode the text abbreviates the description by citing its first and last words ("those very four masses of fire burning" up to "those five great masses of fire ... were burning"), meaning his radiance surpassed that of the Four Great Kings and Śakra together; the rendering keeps the abbreviation as it stands.  The phrase "phye ma red (du / nang du)" before "rising from seclusion" is obscure; it is taken here as a time expression, "in the late afternoon," matching the parallel narratives in which the Buddha rises from seclusion toward evening. In the second meal episode the text again has "great monk" for "great renunciant," read as a copyist's slip. The sentence on the four forests is an abbreviation indicating that the same episode is repeated with fruits from each.  The final phrase "mi grags pa dang ldan pa shes nyen can" is taken as "a person of renown, well known and well connected," reading "nyen" for "gnyen" (kin, connections); the general sense, that a teacher with many followers should consult them first, is clear.  The text once reads "che dang ldan pa," evidently a slip for "tshe dang ldan pa," "venerable," as in the parallel sentence. The phrase "lan gnyis kyi bar du" ("twice") in the brothers' reflection is rendered as it stands; it may refer to Uruvilvā Kāśyapa's two requests for ordination.  ཀུན་རྗོད་པའི་ཆོ་འཕྲུལ is the miracle of reading minds (ādeśanā-prātihārya), and the passage that follows it is a version of the Fire Sermon (Ādittapariyāya). In the description of the wheel-turning king's rule, ཆད་པ means punishment (Skt. daṇḍa, "the rod"), not annihilation; the phrase parallels the stock Sanskrit formula "without punishment and without the sword" (adaṇḍena aśastreṇa). ཁུམ་ཤིག ("shrink back, take care") is rendered as a warning, "be on your guard." ཞོ་ཤས་འཚོ་བའི་མི is taken as the king's paid men or servants. The repeated cross-references ("and so on as above") abbreviate the prophecy given in full earlier and are rendered as such.  The grove ལྟང་བྲང་གི་ཚལ is identified as the Yaṣṭivana (Pāli Laṭṭhivana) by the Sanskrit and Pāli parallels, which place the Buddha at the Supratiṣṭha shrine in that grove near Rājagṛha; the Tibetan name itself is unusual, so this identification rests on the parallel. In the man's report the text once has "the shrine of Gayā" rather than "the shrine of Gayāśīrṣa"; it is translated as written. The courtesy questions (free from ailment, free from affliction, living in comfort) follow the standard Vinaya greeting formula.  In the king's reply "if that is so, let them be released," གདོན is the verb "take out, release" (future of འདོན), not the noun for a seizing spirit. ལས་མཚུངས་པར་སྤྱོད་པ is rendered "those who have shared in deeds with you," that is, people bound to the king by common karma. In Kāśyapa's verses ཕུང་པོ, here rendered "aggregates," corresponds in the parallels to upadhi, the substrata of existence. ཞུགས in "sacrifices, observances and fires" is taken as the honorific word for fire. The line "seen and accepted the unconditioned state" reads the somewhat unclear ཏེ་བཟོད་མཐོང་ན literally. The final instruction, སྐྱོ་བ་བསྐྱེད, corresponds to Sanskrit saṃvejaya, "rouse to a sense of urgency."  The list of terms for a self (self, sentient being, life principle, one who is born, nourisher, person, one born of Manu, son of Manu, and so on) renders the standard Sanskrit series ātman, sattva, jīva, jantu, poṣa, pudgala, manuja, mānava, kāraka, kārāpaka, and so on. The brahmins' thought describes the self as something "which never came into being and will not come into being and yet exists even now", that is, an uncreated, permanent self. The Buddha's reply denies that such a thing exists now. In the reply the text mentions only "evil deeds", where the brahmins' version has "virtuous and evil deeds"; both are translated as written. "Linking up" (ཉིང་མཚམས་སྦྱོར་བ) is rebirth-linking (pratisaṃdhi).  In "the abandonment of all aggregates", ཕུང་པོ corresponds in the parallels to upadhi, the substrata of existence. The king's exclamation "I have gone beyond; I have truly gone beyond" renders འཕགས་སོ། །མངོན་པར་འཕགས་སོ, matching the Sanskrit formula abhikrānto 'haṃ bhadanta abhikrāntaḥ. The list of qualities for form omits "coarse or subtle", which the list for the other aggregates includes; both are translated as written. ཡུ་བུ་ཅག is taken as the first-person plural "we", so "whichever of us". The names Sañjayin, Upatiṣya (later Śāriputra), and Kolita (later Maudgalyāyana) follow the standard identifications of ཡང་དག་རྒྱལ་བ་ཅན, ཉེ་རྒྱལ and པང་ནས་སྐྱེས.  The phrase rendered "known and recognized among the people" (mi grags pa dang ldan pa ... shes par gyur pa) is read with mi as "people", i.e. "renowned among people", matching the Sanskrit parallels (jñātā abhijñātāḥ); read as a negative ("unknown") it would contradict the reasoning. The opening word of that exchange (rje) is taken as "first".  The size of the following reads literally "two hundred fifty, fifty"; it is taken as "two hundred and fifty", the traditional number of Upatiṣya's and Kolita's disciples, the extra "fifty" being apparently a repetition. In the Blessed One's instruction the result of reciting the verse ("will vanish") is given in a past form in the Tibetan, which is read as the anticipated outcome.  In the Blessed One's instructions to Udāyin, rgyal po'i khab is read in its literal sense, "the king's residence, the royal palace" in Kapilavastu, not the city Rājagṛha, since Udāyin is going to Kapilavastu and is told to wait at its gate. In the Buddha's verses (parallel to Dhammapada 179 to 180), the "queen of sense objects" renders yul gyi rgyal mo literally; the Pāli parallel speaks instead of craving as an ensnaring net, and the Tibetan image is uncertain.  As in the preceding piece, rgyal po'i khab is read here as "the royal palace" or "royal residence" in Kapilavastu, not the city Rājagṛha. The phrase rendered "fell to the ground in a faint" reads brgya la nas sa la 'gyel ba; brgya la is taken as an idiom of sudden collapse, which the sprinkling with water and reviving confirm.  The proud Śākyas' imagined complaint is compressed in the Tibetan; ltog po zhig grub pa is taken as "achieved only an empty belly", i.e. nothing but hunger from his austerities, and the following clauses as contrasting how such a one should arrive (through the sky, attended by multitudes) with his coming on foot. In Śuddhodana's verse, the three bows are taken as the present one together with the two earlier occasions the verse names, at the birth and under the jambu tree, as the king's prose explanation goes on to recount.  The text reads གསེར་གྱི་གཞང་པ, which makes no sense here; the evident reading is གཞོང་པ, "bowl, basin", translated "bowls of gold." Similarly གོས་མན་བས་པ is read as གོས་མནབས་པ, "wearing a garment." In the two bathing verses the text has རེག་པའི་ཡོན་ཏན ("those of virtue who touch it") in the first and རིག་པའི་ཡོན་ཏན ("those endowed with knowledge") in the second; each is translated as written, though the parallel verse elsewhere (Pāli vedagū, "knowers") suggests the second reading is the original in both. The king is addressed as Gautama, his clan name. The ten abodes of the noble ones are the ten āryavāsas.  The grove ཚངས་པས་བསྐོར་བ is translated literally as "Encircled by Brahmā," since its Indian name could not be identified with confidence. In the Buddha's instruction to Maudgalyāyana the text reads སོམས་ཤིག ("consider!"); the context and the reply show that སོངས་ཤིག, "go!", is meant, and it is translated so. ལེ་བརྒན is a madder or crimson red.  The list of the Four Great Kings at the gates names the guardian of the north as Kubera (ལུས་ངན་པོ), while the following dialogue at the northern gate names him Vaiśravaṇa (རྣམ་ཐོས་ཀྱི་བུ); the two are commonly identified, and each is given as the text has it. The view of the perishable collection is satkāyadṛṣṭi, the view that takes the aggregates to be a self; its twenty peaks are the twenty forms of that view.  Droṇodana here says seventy-six thousand Śākyas saw the truth with him in the grove called Encircled by Brahmā, whereas the earlier narrative gave sixty-six thousand; each figure is translated as the text gives it. "Music played without men" renders སྐྱེས་པ་མེད་པའི་རོལ་མོ, the standard phrase for music performed entirely by women. The Śākya king བཟང་ལྡན is identified as Bhadrika.  The pratyekabuddha ཀུན་ཤེས, to whom Aniruddha offered alms in a former life, could not be identified with confidence; the name is translated literally as "All-Knowing." In the bed-maker episode the text once reads ལ་འཆའ་བ for མལ་འཆའ་བ, "bed-maker," and is translated accordingly.  The opening sentence continues Devadatta's private reasoning from the preceding piece; the closing "sñam nas" shows it as his thought before he speaks. ཀ་ཏ་མོ་ར་ཀ and རྒྱལ are taken as two names, Kaṭamoraka and Tiṣya, as the list separates them with དང; in Sanskrit sources this companion of Devadatta is often a single figure, Kaṭamorakatiṣyaka. The syllables མལ་ངས in the Blessed One's reflection make no sense as written and are read as "therefore I" (as if དེས་ན་ངས). ངོམ་ཟུང is read as མིག་ཟུང, "his eyes." The phrase "those who will not would think otherwise" is rendered literally; it seems to mean that those not destined to please would fall short of the 'Come, monk' ordination, which is reserved for those in their final existence.  The "floods" and "yokes" are the standard sets of four oghas and four yogas (sensual desire, existence, views and ignorance). In the third sentence of the Blessed One's command, the Tibetan འདི་རྒན་གྱིས is taken as "he is your senior," since Upāli was ordained first.  སྐུལ་བ་མཉམ་པར is read as སྐལ་བ་མཉམ་པར, "sharing the same lot." དབང་པོ་མཉམས is read as མ་ཉམས, "unimpaired," as in the parallel sentence later in the passage. The phrase about the Blessed One's back follows the familiar Vinaya motif in which the Buddha, citing back pain, hands a discourse over to a disciple.  དོང་ཀ is taken as the karṇikāra flower, its usual Sanskrit equivalent; ཤིང་ཀ་དམ་པ་ཀ is taken as the kadamba tree. Both are golden or yellow blossoms, which suits the comparison of color. The cries of "Get away from before me!" are rendered literally; the point of the narrative is that these old phrases survive in later speech without their meaning being understood.  The naming of the "house" rests on a wordplay in the Indic original (Sanskrit agāra, "house," heard as a place for what is "not to be done," akaraṇīya); the Tibetan keeps the explanation but not the pun. The sentence "This came about in accordance with what is right ..." reflects the refrain of the Sanskrit Vinaya version of this origin story, "by Dharma, not by non-Dharma"; its last clause, "in the Dharma of the Lord of Victors this is what is foremost," is rendered as the Tibetan stands. The syllable ང before ཕྱིར་མི་སྐྱེ is treated as a stray letter. The piece ends in the middle of the beings' recollection, which continues in the next piece.  The names the people bore in successive reigns (beings, come here, the marked ones, cloud-throats, palm-trunks) are translated literally from the Tibetan, since their Indian equivalents could not be identified with confidence. The refrain "through Dharma, not through what is contrary to Dharma" echoes the Aggañña-sutta formula; the Tibetan here has "in the Dharma of the Lord of Victors," translated as it stands. The kṣatriya and rājan etymologies rest on Sanskrit wordplay (protecting fields from harm; delighting the people).  "Mind-born" renders ཡིད་ལས་སྐྱེས, the folk etymology of mānava (from manas) found in the parallel Vinaya accounts. In the genealogy the Tibetan, read strictly, makes Cāru the son of Māndhātṛ, Upacāru the son of Cāru, and the long list that follows the descendants of Upacāru (the possessive with a list joined by "and"); the narrative above it makes Upacāru, Cārumat and Upacārumat successive generations, so the list is best taken as a succession of kings. Several names in it could not be identified with confidence and are translated from their meaning: Relinquisher, Relinquisher-Holder, Aṅga-Chariot, Evil-Shunner, Son of Sāgara, Well-Attained, Great Well-Attained, Light-Maker, Hero, Possessed of Meru, Having Meru, Ray, Rayed, Having Rays, All-Around Ray, and Victor over the Unvanquished. ཡུལ་གྲུ་འཛིན is taken as Potala and ཡུལ་དམག་གིས་མི་ཚུགས་པ as Ayodhyā ("unassailable by armies").  Many of the kings and cities in this genealogy are given in the Tibetan only in translation and could not be identified with confidence; these are rendered by their meaning: Hard to Bear, Elephant-Given, Woolen-Robed, Lying-on-the-Breast, Tamer of Great Elephants, Palm Garland, Dispeller of Darkness, Practitioner of Austerities, Face of the Earth, Lord of the Earth, Held by Kings, Middling, Digger, Near Digger, Possessed of Digging, All-Around Digger, Splendid Appearance, Realization, Great Realization, Army of Realization, Steadfast Army, Fool-Slayer, Dawn, Generator, All-Around Generator, Bull among Men, Provider of Food and Drink, Provider of Abundant Food and Drink, Abiding, Well-Abiding, Rider of Great Strength, Good Intellect, Steady Rider, Victorious Bow and Victorious Chariot. Mahādeva here is a king of Mithilā (the Makhādeva of the Pāli tradition), not the god Śiva. The Tibetan reads "Gautama, these" (གཽ་ཏ་མ་དེ་དག) and "Gautama and" (གཽ་ཏ་མ་དང) in two places, evidently slips for the recurring address "Gautamas" (གཽ་ཏ་མ་དག), and is translated so.  The city ཀུན་ཏུ་སྣང could not be identified with confidence and is rendered by its meaning, "All-Illuminating." The king རྣ་བ་ཅན ("the one with ears") is given as Karṇa, the name the parallel Vinaya accounts appear to have; the courtesan's lover པད་མའི་རྩ་ལག ("lotus root") is taken as Mṛṇāla, and the sage མདོག་གནག ("dark-hued") as Kṛṣṇavarṇa. The second mention of the coin spells it ཀཱ་ཥཱ་པ་ཎ, evidently a slip for kārṣāpaṇa.  The renaming of the sage from Kṛṣṇavarṇa ("Dark-Hued") to Golden-Hued (Tibetan གསེར་མདོག, perhaps Suvarṇavarṇa) rests on the change of his skin color, so the new name is given in English. ཡན་ལག་སྐྱེས ("born from the limbs") is taken as Āṅgirasa and བུ་རམ་ཤིང་པ ("of the sugarcane") as Ikṣvāku, the clan names the Buddhist tradition explains in this way. The Tibetan here spells the sage's name མདོག་ནག, a variant of མདོག་གནག.  The four sons are identified from the parallel lineage account of the Mūlasarvāstivāda Vinaya (Ulkāmukha, Karakarṇin, Hastinika, Nūpura). The youngest prince's name, literally "Delight in Kingship," is translated rather than given a reconstructed Sanskrit form, which could not be confirmed. Several evident scribal slips are read in context: ཤིས་ལྡན as ཤེས་ལྡན ("gentlemen"), ཟླ་བ་བརྒྱ as ཟླ་བ་བརྒྱད ("eight months"), and སྤྱི་མ་མཆིས as "have no child at all." The phrase འཐབ་རིར་བབ་པའི་མཆོག is obscure; it is taken as "the finest of matches."  The river ཆུ་བོ་སྐལ་ལྡན་ཤིང་རྟ is the Bhāgīrathī, and སེར་སྐྱའི་གཞི is Kapilavastu, whose name the text explains as "Kapila's site." The second city, ལྷས་བསྟན, is translated literally as "Shown by the Gods" since its Sanskrit form could not be confirmed. The closing exchange turns on ཕོད ("to dare, be capable"), which the next passage develops into the etymology of the Śākya name (Skt. śakya, "able"). The phrase མཐོང་ཟིན་གྱིས is rendered loosely as "I have seen enough"; it may simply be a formula of leave-taking.  The Śākya name is explained here from ཕོད་པ, "capable, daring" (Skt. śakya). Several names are translated literally because their Sanskrit forms could not be confirmed: གནས་འཇོག ("Abode Founder"), ས་ཁྱིམ ("Earth House"; the following རྒྱལ་པོས་ཁྱིམ is read as རྒྱལ་པོ་ས་ཁྱིམ), and the sons of the four daughters other than Suprabuddha: ཕྲེང་བ་ཅན ("Garlanded"), བཟང་ལེན ("Taker of Good") and དགེ་འཕེལ ("Increase of Virtue"). Śuklodana's son རྒྱལ is identified as Tiṣya following the parallel lineage in the Mūlasarvāstivāda Vinaya; this is less certain. Mahāmaudgalyāyana's discourse on the Śākya lineage, which has been running as a long quotation, closes with Rāhula, and the sūtra's frame narration resumes.  The two sisters' names are explained from སྒྱུ་འཕྲུལ, "illusion" (Skt. māyā), so the meaning is given once beside each name. The hostile mountain people སྐྱ་བསེང ("pale and gaunt") are identified as the Pāṇḍavas, as in the parallel Mūlasarvāstivāda Vinaya account; this rests on the parallel. The exchange about breaking the Śākya law is terse, and its assignment to speakers is inferred. The title is rendered from the Sanskrit Abhiniṣkramaṇa ("going forth, great departure").

It was produced by a careful, doctrine-aware process aiming at a high degree of accuracy, but it has not been reviewed by a human translator and may contain errors. It is offered as a provisional translation for those texts of the Tibetan Buddhist canon that have not yet received an authoritative human translation. Where a published translation exists — above all from 84000: Translating the Words of the Buddha (84000.co) — that translation is definitive and should be preferred; please consult it instead. These translations are released into the public domain (CC0) in the spirit of spreading the Dharma to all corners of the earth.

Just as the Bodhi tree now standing at Bodh Gayā neither is nor is not the same tree beneath which the Buddha attained awakening, so too this stupa is intended as the next generation of stupa — dependently arising according to the conditions of its environment, and ceaselessly proclaiming the Dharma for the benefit of all sentient beings.

May whoever encounters these words derive great benefit.
